- **[Guardian: Detecting Robotic Planning and Execution Errors with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01946)**  `arXiv:2512.01946`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Paul Pacaud, Ricardo Garcia, Shizhe Chen, Cordelia Schmid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust robotic manipulation requires reliable failure detection and recovery. Although recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) show promise in robot failure detection, their generalization is severely limited by the scarcity and narrow coverage of failure data. To address this bottleneck, we propose an automatic framework for generating diverse robotic planning and execution failures across both simulated and real-world environments. Our approach perturbs successful manipulation trajectories to synthesize failures that reflect realistic failure distributions, and leverages VLMs to produce structured step-by-step reasoning traces. This yields GuardianFail-36k, a large-scale failure reasoning dataset built upon the RLBench simulator and the BridgeDataV2 real-robot dataset. Using GuardianFail-36k, we train Guardian, a multi-view reasoning VLM for unified planning and execution verification. Guardian achieves state-of-the-art performance on three unseen real-world benchmarks: RoboFail, RoboVQA, and our newly introduced UR5-Fail. When integrated with a state-of-the-art LLM-based manipulation policy, it consistently boosts task success rates in both simulation and real-world deployment. These results demonstrate that scaling high-quality failure reasoning data is critical for improving generalization in robotic failure detection. Code, Data, and Models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[IndustryNav: Evaluating Spatial Reasoning and Safety of Embodied Agents in Dynamic Industrial Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17384)**  `arXiv:2511.17384`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yifan Li, Lichi Li, Anh Dao, Xinyu Zhou, Wenjun Huang, Tianyi Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual large language models (VLLMs) are increasingly used as embodied agents, yet industrial warehouses demand more than reaching a goal: agents must navigate safely around moving workers and vehicles. To evaluate embodied agents' goal reaching and safety, we introduce IndustryNav, a simulation environment with 24 warehouse scenes for closed-loop navigation to specified destinations. Alongside task success and efficiency, we introduce safety-aware metrics that capture collisions and near-collision warnings along each trajectory. Experiments with eleven leading open- and closed-source VLLMs and three navigation baselines show that agents can reach their goals yet collide with moving obstacles, exposing a gap between success and safe navigation. This gap matters in safety-critical settings, where a single collision can end the task. IndustryNav also supports policy learning through automated oracle trajectories and simulator interaction, with held-out scenes for generalization tests. These experiments show that policies can succeed on new routes in familiar warehouses yet fail to transfer to held-out layouts, making cross-scene generalization an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[Automotive-ENV: Benchmarking Multimodal Models in Automotive Cockpit Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21143)**  `arXiv:2509.21143`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Junfeng Yan, Biao Wu, Meng Fang, Ling Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal models have demonstrated strong capabilities in web, desktop, and mobile GUI environments, but their performance in automotive cockpit systems remains largely unexplored. In-vehicle GUIs introduce unique challenges beyond traditional GUI interaction, including vehicle-state reasoning, implicit intent understanding, and safety-critical decision making. To address this gap, we introduce Automotive-ENV, an interactive benchmark for evaluating multimodal models in automotive cockpit environments. Built on Android Automotive OS, Automotive-ENV combines cockpit-screen interaction with vehicle-state signals and deterministic state-based evaluation. The benchmark contains 420 carefully constructed tasks covering routine cockpit operation and safety-critical scenarios. Unlike prior GUI benchmarks that rely on action matching or LLM-based judging, Automotive-ENV evaluates model behavior through reproducible validators over UI states, vehicle signals, and safety-related conditions. We benchmark representative general-purpose multimodal models and GUI-specialized vision-language models on Automotive-ENV. Experimental results show that current models remain far from reliable in automotive environments, with substantial performance degradation in implicit and safety-critical tasks. Further analysis reveals consistent failure modes in safety-aware reasoning, vehicle-control understanding, and implicit hazard recognition. We release Automotive-ENV, together with tasks, validators, and evaluation tools, to support future research on automotive multimodal systems.
  </details>

- **[RoboGene: Diversity-Driven Agentic Task Generation Framework for VLA Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16444)**  `arXiv:2602.16444`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yixue Zhang, Kun Wu, Zhi Gao, Zhen Zhao, Pei Ren, Zhiyuan Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The pursuit of general-purpose robotic manipulation is hindered by the scarcity of diverse, real-world interaction data. Unlike large-scale web vision-language data, robotic data collection incurs prohibitive physical costs. The key challenge is thus not merely to collect more robot data, but to obtain informative training data that broadens behavioral coverage across objects, scenes, and skills. Yet automatically curating task proposals to maximize the utility of collected data remains under-explored.We introduce RoboGene, an agentic framework for generating diverse, informative manipulation tasks across single-arm, dual-arm, and mobile robots. RoboGene combines (1) diversity-driven sampling for broad task coverage, (2) agentic self-reflection for task-quality assessment, and (3) human-verified feedback memory for execution-grounded validation and continual refinement.Through quantitative analysis and large-scale real-world experiments, we collect 18k trajectories and introduce metrics for task feasibility and diversity. RoboGene significantly outperforms frontier foundation models, including GPT-4o and Gemini 2.5 Pro. VLA models trained on RoboGene-generated data achieve higher success rates and stronger generalization, highlighting the value of high-quality task generation.Project page:this https URL. Datasets: Hugging Face (this https URL) and ModelScope (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization in a Sim-Trained Robot Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817)**  `arXiv:2610.35817`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Benjamin Pikus, Daniel Saunders, Mircea Mironenco, Salman Mohammadi, Mathew Mathew, Sirou Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot policy needs to understand what a task is asking for in a scene, and how to move the robot to accomplish that task. To achieve these two skills, we introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches a small off-the-shelf vision-language model to control a robot via a simple tool harness. We use a pretrained 27B parameter model to bootstrap world understanding and reasoning, followed by supervised fine-tuning and GRPO on simple simulated tasks to teach it how to move the robot. Without any real-world robot data during training, ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness and outperforming the MolmoAct2 VLA (33.3%), while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. We demonstrate that ZAP also generalizes to completely new objects, goals, strategies, and language by seating a test tube in a rack (and choosing a different hole in the rack when prompted), handing a test tube between arms mid-air, drawing an X on a tic-tac-toe board, and playing chess. These goals and types of manipulation don't appear in our training data. Furthermore, ZAP also transfers to an unseen embodiment. On a single-arm Franka, ZAP reaches 55.9% on RoboLab-120, 4.9 percentage points ahead of the leading VLA/WAM baseline.
  </details>

- **[Profiling a Frontier Agent with EBench: From Direct Control to Harness with Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35832)**  `arXiv:2610.35832`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Yao, Yude Zou, Guanbo Jiao, Xing Gao, Ning Gao, Yukai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can control robots without robot-specific training, but how their capabilities compare and interact with post-trained vision-language-action (VLA) and world action model (WAM) policies across manipulation demands remains to be thoroughly evaluated. In this report, we evaluate GPT-6-Astra on EBench as the core of an agent that controls a robot through an agent harness under the same visual and proprioceptive observation protocol as post-trained policies. We study two agent configurations: direct control, in which the agent generates waypoint commands directly, and harness with policy, in which it can also invoke a post-trained VLA or WAM policy for action execution. Under direct control, the agent achieves competitive overall performance relative to post-trained policies. It leads on short-horizon tasks and exhibits the least performance variation across generalization dimensions, whereas it underperforms post-trained policies on long-horizon tasks, tabletop manipulation, and high-precision contact-rich tasks. In the harness with policy, invoking a post-trained VLA or WAM combines the complementary strengths of both components, raising overall task success to the level of the strongest compared post-trained policies while substantially reducing token cost. However, performance remains below the per-task upper bound of the standalone components due to suboptimal policy invocation, fixed-instruction execution without subgoals, and grasp disruption when the agent resumes control. Overall, our study characterizes the capability boundary of a frontier MLLM agent and suggests directions for improving future agent harness design. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Extended LASMP: Language Aided Subset Sampling Based Motion Planner for Drone Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34054)**  `arXiv:2610.34054`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Saswati Bhattacharjee, Anirban Sinha, Mukulika Ghosh, Chinwe Ekenna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper proposes a command-aligned sampling distribution that incorporates navigation commands directly into the exploration process to improve the efficiency of sampling-based motion planning for quadcopters in three-dimensional environments. Inspired by the Language-Aided Subset Sampling Motion Planner, this paper extends command-aware subset sampling to aerial systems. Subset sampling is generalized to command-aligned regions, enabling structured 3D exploration while preserving probabilistic completeness. Natural language instructions are grounded into yaw-conditioned and altitude-aware geometric constraints using a prompt-based large language model for entity extraction and instruction validation. Extensive simulations in complex environments demonstrate that the proposed method generates collision-free, consistent trajectories while significantly reducing sampling effort compared to uniform RRT-based planners.
  </details>

- **[What Can One Sentence Fix? A Failure Taxonomy and a Minimal Language Correction for Frozen VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29967)**  `arXiv:2608.29967`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Owen Kwon, Pablo Ortega-Kral, Arthur Bucker, Jean Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a deployed vision-language-action (VLA) policy fails, fine-tuning it again for every new setting is costly, yet the person watching it can usually say what went wrong in one sentence. We ask what such a sentence can and cannot fix in a frozen policy. Labelling every failing LIBERO-90 task (out of distribution) for OpenVLA-OFT and pi0.5, we find that the failures sort into four modes and that only one, execution misalignment, where the policy reaches the right target but miscalibrates how far or how hard to move, is within reach of a sentence; the other three lack a target or a plan, not a magnitude. For this mode we define the narrowest human correction: one retrospective sentence per task, grounded by an LLM into an action dimension, direction, magnitude term, and time window, becomes a short bias on the frozen policy's actions, after which the policy completes the rest on its own, episode after episode. Re-executed on its task's failed rollouts, it recovers 65 of all 139 in-distribution and 33 of all 328 out-of-distribution failures in simulation; on a real xArm7 after the robot base is moved, it raises pick-and-place from 10/30 to 30/30 in each of two same-object conditions and from 2/30 to 24/30 with a new object, while two long-horizon tasks stay at 0/30 with and without correction, as the taxonomy predicts. A naively prompted VLM that watches the same failures and writes the same kind of correction recovers none in up to 10 attempts, suggesting that the value lies in the person's calibrated sense of magnitude and timing rather than in language itself. Videos:this https URL
  </details>

- **[CRAFT-E: An Interpretable and Editable Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Affordance-Grounded Active Object Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04231)**  `arXiv:2512.04231`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhou Chen, Joe Lin, Carson Bulgin, Sathyanarayanan N. Aakur_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assistive robots operating in unstructured environments must reason not only about what objects are, but how they can be used, including non-canonical and improvised uses. This requires grounding language-based action queries to objects that afford the requested function, are visually present, and can be physically retrieved. Existing approaches often rely on black-box models, fixed affordance labels, or direct object predictions, limiting transparency and controllability. We introduce CRAFT-E, a modular neuro-symbolic framework that composes an LLM-derived verb-property-object knowledge graph with CLIP-based visual alignment and energy-based grasp reasoning. The system exposes interpretable grounding paths, incorporates grasp feasibility into affordance inference, and supports targeted graph edits without retraining. We also construct a benchmark dataset with unified annotations for verb-object compatibility, segmentation, and grasp candidates, and deploy the full pipeline on a physical robot. Across static scenes, real-world trials involving 20 verbs and 39 objects, ImageNet-based functional retrieval, and COCO-Tasks transfer, CRAFT-E provides a competitive training-free prior for affordance-grounded retrieval while exposing component-level diagnostics and graph-level interventions. Under staged and full-pipeline perception, CRAFT-E matches strong flat LLM priors in several settings while adding failure attribution and editable reasoning paths. By coupling symbolic reasoning with embodied perception, CRAFT-E offers an inspectable and customizable framework for affordance-grounded object retrieval.
  </details>

- **[FISRF: Flexible Instructional Scaffolding Reflection Framework for Robotics Task Planning with Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35686)**  `arXiv:2610.35686`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qingmiao Liang, Jiatao Zhang, Runxin Zou, Yufan Song, Wei Song, Yuhua Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely adopted for reflective error correction in task planning; however, existing approaches primarily rely on autonomous exploration, rendering them prone to accumulating erroneous experiences during early interactions with new environments. To address this limitation, we propose the Flexible Instructional Scaffolding Reflection Framework (FISRF), an agent--mentor architecture that estimates the robot's Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD) in task planning and adaptively selects reflection strategies and mentor guidance, thereby facilitating the accumulation of effective error-correction experience. Experiments in ALFWorld and on real-world robotic platforms demonstrate that FISRF reduces the average number of error-correction rounds by 37% and redundant reasoning by 25.1%, while improving the overall task success rate by 8%. Project webpage :this https URL
  </details>

- **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252)**  `arXiv:2610.35252`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shitong Liu, Jingwei Zhang, Sheng Lian, Xinyu Zhuang, Kaiwen Guo, Quan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.
  </details>

- **[How High Can GPT-6 Astra Climb in Contact-Rich Manipulation? PhysLadder: A Hierarchical, Matched Sim-Real Benchmark for Physical Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34527)**  `arXiv:2610.34527`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haibao Yu, Jiaqi Meng, Zijian Zhang, Bowen Jing, Lei Yang, Yue Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce PhysLadder, a hierarchical Sim-Real benchmark for evaluating physical interaction capabilities in contact-rich manipulation. Its 16 household tasks span three cumulative levels: contact selection and geometric alignment (L1), force regulation within sustained contact (L2), and coordinated contact reconfiguration and force regulation (L3). Each task is instantiated in physically and visually matched simulated and real-world environments, with shared task definitions and success criteria. We evaluate GPT-6 Astra zero-shot with three trials per task in each environment. Real-world success rates decline from 80.0% at L1 to 47.6% at L2 and 0.0% at L3, with corresponding simulation rates of 66.7%, 47.6%, and 0.0%. Ten of the 16 tasks have identical success counts across environments; the remaining six differ by one success out of three trials. Across both environments, recurring difficulties in spatial reasoning, contact selection, force regulation, and coordination often prevent local physical progress from accumulating into task completion. These findings characterize Astra's observed capability boundary across increasing interaction demands and, for this model, support matched simulation as a repeatable, scalable testbed for studying the sustained, coordinated physical interactions that limit reliable manipulation.
  </details>

- **[LAW: Unifying Language, Action, and World Modeling for Agentic Physical Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23334)**  `arXiv:2610.23334`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiajun Cao, Kai Tang, Bohua Zhang, Zezhong Qian, Ying Li, Xiaozhu Ju, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models jointly predict actions and future world states, but long-horizon manipulation further requires language reasoning to plan subtask objectives, assess execution progress, and guide action selection. We present LAW, a closed-loop VLM--WAM framework that unifies language reasoning, action generation, and world modeling through semantic subtask progress. Within the LAW agent, a hybrid-expert vision-language model plans the remaining subtasks, estimates the values of and fuses actions proposed by a world-action model, and asynchronously assesses execution status to support continued execution and subtask transitions. Within the world-action model, Mixture-of-Transformer Experts integrates modality-specific and shared computation for joint video--action modeling. Dual-stream hierarchical visual memory maintains temporal context for both models, while subtask-local value learning aligns action evaluation with semantic objectives and preserves supervision from successful prefixes of failed trajectories. LAW achieves 94.21% average success across 50 RoboTwin tasks. Replacing the WAM backbone with OpenWAM-$alpha$ further raises the average success rate to 95.56%, establishing a new open-source state of the art on RoboTwin. Despite having only 2B parameters, our VLM also outperforms variants using Embodied-R1.5 and GPT-5.5 High. On three long-horizon real-robot tasks, LAW achieves 65% average success, compared with 28.33% for $pi_{0.5}$ and 25% for Fast-WAM.
  </details>

- **[CognitiveReality: Robot-Agnostic Semantic Gaussian Mapping with an LLM Agent for Immersive Collaborative VR Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31418)**  `arXiv:2609.31418`  `cs.RO`  
  _Timofei Kozlov, Dmitrii Maliukov, Andrey Marchenko, Dmitrii Plotnikov, Miguel Altamirano Cabrera, Dzmitry Tsetserukou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A photorealistic 3D view tells a teleoperator where a robot is, but not what the scene contains, how well each object has been observed, or how to turn pointing and speech into robot action. CognitiveReality turns a robot's RGB-D stream into a live, semantically indexed Gaussian-TSDF map shared by an operator in virtual reality and a tool-using language agent. One mapper binary serves any platform through configuration alone: it ingests poses from robot SLAM, joint kinematics, motion capture or an inline visual tracker, bridges localization outages with a shadow tracker and keyframe-anchored PnP, and maintains open-vocabulary instance identities with per-object quality at 2 Hz. Speech and controller rays are grounded against persistent scene objects through validated typed tools and operator-confirmed robot actions. In the controlled agent evaluation, the deployed local Qwen3-VL-8B router reaches 81.24\% tool exact match, while merge-aware replay correctly redirects 101 absorbed object identifiers. On robot data CognitiveReality exceeds a Gaussian-plus-SDF baseline by 2-8 dB; pose error through 5-40 s SLAM outages stays within 1-8 cm. Deployed live on two quadrupeds, the agent executed 26 of 30 navigation requests and 20 of 20 re-observation requests, raising object quality by 2-5 dB.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Guided Future Hypotheses for Horizon-Aware Exploration in Multi-Step Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29864)**  `arXiv:2605.29864`  `cs.RO`  
  _Mohammad Khoshnazar, Andrew Melnik, Michael Beetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-step robot manipulation requires acting under uncertainty about how the scene will evolve, making exploration and policy adaptation challenging. We study whether short-horizon, task-consistent future videos can provide useful structured priors for control and reinforcement-learning fine-tuning. We formalize this idea through Future-Experience Conditioning (FEC), a simple interface that conditions closed-loop policies on a latent representation of a short future video. In our simulation setup, future clips are generated in three stages, an LLM reasoner operating over a task ontology initialized from the current scene state, a robot-free digital-twin rollout of the intended object motion, and a mask-free video diffusion model that synthesizes a robot-consistent future clip without requiring segmentation at inference. We instantiate this future-conditioning interface primarily with BC and BC+RL, and compare against a future-conditioned Streaming Flow Policy (SFP) baseline on RoboCasa and CALVIN under NoFuture, GTFuture, GenFuture, and WrongFuture. Generated futures improve performance over no-future conditioning, while mismatched futures degrade it, and our BC+RL instantiation achieves the strongest overall results. An average BC+RL learning-curve analysis across 8 CALVIN tasks further shows that GTFuture improves fastest, GenFuture improves earlier and to a higher level than NoFuture, and WrongFuture remains at zero throughout training. These results suggest that short-horizon future videos can serve as useful structured priors for exploration and policy adaptation under imperfect future predictions.this https URL
  </details>

- **[UMAS: System-Level Uncertainty Quantification for Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33013)**  `arXiv:2610.33013`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hanwen Li, Jinhao Duan, Xiaoshuang Shi, Yue Zhang, Tianlong Chen, Kaidi Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) are a promising paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models, yet they remain prone to hallucinations and erroneous outputs. Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) is crucial for assessing system reliability, but existing methods are largely designed for single-agent settings; how to exploit MAS structure for UQ while keeping inference cost-neutral remains underexplored. We propose UMAS, a parameter-free, cost-neutral, system-level UQ framework that estimates the credibility of inter-agent influence from each generation's intrinsic confidence. UMAS uses these credibility signals to modulate how uncertainty is updated at each node, and then aggregates node-level uncertainty into a system-level estimate. Extensive evaluations on Debate and DyLAN across diverse benchmarks show that UMAS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by an average of 10.54 AUROC points. Beyond uncertainty estimation, UMAS enables hallucination detection and uncertainty-aware answer selection, improving MAS accuracy by up to 12.6 points on specific tasks while enhancing reliability. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EvolveMAS: A Unified Self-Evolution Framework for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34450)**  `arXiv:2610.34450`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Yalun Wu, Qiwei Ma, Bingbing Xu, Dan Zhang, Long Bai, Boyang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decentralized multi-agent systems (MAS) enable large language model agents to interact without centralized control. Agents need to adapt to other agents at test time and reuse accumulated experience to refine their interaction strategies for future interactions across cooperative, mixed-motive, and adversarial settings. However, existing approaches often develop adaptation mechanisms for particular interaction settings. This raises a central question: how can decentralized MAS support test-time adaptation and reliable experience reuse across interaction settings? We propose EvolveMAS, a unified self-evolution framework that supports experience-driven adaptation within and across episodes. EvolveMAS pairs each main agent with a dedicated MemoryAgent for test-time memory governance, which maintains isolated Structured Memory outside the main agent's reasoning context. EvolveMAS organizes adaptation into two complementary processes: Within-Episode Online Adaptation and Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution. During Within-Episode Online Adaptation, MemoryAgent updates episodic, semantic, and working memory from interaction evidence to inform subsequent actions. During Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution, the main agent uses memory and episode outcomes to distill reusable interaction knowledge comprising partner profiles and interaction strategies for subsequent episodes. Across five benchmarks and six evaluation configurations spanning the three interaction settings, EvolveMAS achieves the highest normalized average score of 77.2 among the compared methods. Ablations show that test-time memory governance and cross-episode knowledge reuse both improve performance. Interaction traces further illustrate how updated memory and distilled knowledge guide subsequent interactions. These results support experience-driven adaptation in decentralized MAS without updating model parameters.
  </details>

- **[Knowing When to Escalate: Disagreement Patterns for Uncertainty-Aware Content Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35335)**  `arXiv:2610.35335`  `cs.MA`  
  _Michał Wawer, Jarosław A. Chudziak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic content-moderation systems increasingly rely on collective judgments from LLM agents, while aggregation methods commonly treat inter-agent disagreement as a reliability problem to be removed through consensus or voting. This framing is inappropriate for value-laden tasks, where disagreement may reflect irreducible value pluralism - a distinction standard uncertainty decompositions cannot recover, since they operate on output distributions and discard the reasoning behind each judgment. We propose Reasoning Trace Divergence Analysis (RTDA), which embeds model-generated justifications from perspective-differentiated agents and classifies each case by justification similarity and conclusion agreement. The resulting four-state taxonomy isolates convergent disagreement, where agents reasoning similarly but concluding differently, as a candidate signature of value-laden conflict. On 1,000 items from the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, this structural signal predicts human annotator disagreement better than magnitude-only and output-only baselines. We contribute a design principle: condition human escalation on the structure of disagreement, not its magnitude alone, with RTDA serving as a post-processing layer for systems that already generate reasoning traces.
  </details>

- **[Coral Discovery: The Division of Cognitive Labor in Autonomous Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33281)**  `arXiv:2610.33281`  `cs.MA`  
  _Xinxing Ren, Qianbo Zang, Ziyan Wang, Suman Deb, Qiang Yang, Caelum Forder, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A research community is only as strong as the way it divides its cognitive labour. The philosopher Philip Kitcher argued that researchers should not all pursue the most promising approach. Credit rules, not a central plan, keep some of them on other lines. We bring this question to autonomous research, where Large Language Model agents improve programs against a deterministic grader. Agents that share only a score follow the leader and converge onto one approach. Coral Discovery gives the community a direction board. A verifier labels every attempt with a direction, the mechanism earning the score, and a method, one change within it. Per direction, the board tracks how much each eval improves the score, adds an exploration bonus for little-tried directions, prices the option of opening a new direction, and suggests how many agents each direction deserves. Credit is earned against a direction's own best, so a lagging direction keeps its agents. Agents finish their lines, then treat the board as advice. We test on nine tasks with the same model and budget, without grader access except for Kernel. Coral Discovery beats the strongest open autonomous multi-agent system on 6 tasks, ties on 1, and loses on 2. Against published numbers, it is best or tied on 4. It improves the reported Kernel result from 1103 to 1095 cycles and passes 1103 at eval 275, under half of the 596 used before. It sets a new Polyominoes state of the art, 91.78 against 87.0. Direction labels show that the leading scores rest on directions a score-only community never entered. Code will be released shortly.
  </details>

- **[VibeGame: Prompt-to-Game Development with AI-Native Engine and Self-Evolving Adversarial Agent Team](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33106)**  `arXiv:2610.33106`  `cs.MA`  
  _Wenbo Hu, Ken Li, Jiazhe Wei, Yukang Cao, Weiyi Hong, Jiayi Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents have enabled software development through natural-language interaction, but extending this paradigm to end-to-end game development remains challenging. Building a playable game requires coordinating code, scenes, assets, animations, physics, and configurations, while allowing developers to validate their integrated behavior through interactive testing. We introduce VibeGame, a framework for creating complete 2D game projects from natural-language prompts and optional reference images. Its AI-native game engine represents project state as schema-validated structured text and provides frame-synchronous runtime control, enabling agents to inspect game states, execute actions, and verify behavior at their own pace. Built upon this interface, we design an Adversarial Agent Team of eight specialized agents to separate implementation from review, so that problems are found and fixed through multi-round loops. For each conducted and accepted project, we further introduce a training-free self-evolution mechanism to distill it into reusable skeletons, modules, and collaboration contracts for future development. We evaluate prompt-assets-to-game (PA2G), where agents are given an asset library, and prompt-to-game (P2G), where agents generate their own art. On a GameCraft-Bench subset, VibeGame in P2G consistently outperforms a range of agent harnesses in either P2G or PA2G with the same underlying model, including GPT-6.1-Sol.
  </details>

- **[AutoSwarm: Automating Agent Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33048)**  `arXiv:2610.33048`  `cs.MA`  
  _Zhonghang Li, Zongwei Li, Chao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly automate complex tasks, with multi-agent systems seeking further performance gains through specialization and collaboration. However, distributing work across agents does not automatically yield complementary contributions, and inter-agent dependencies can turn local errors into task failure. To address these challenges, we propose AutoSwarm, a multi-agent framework for collaborative problem solving. Collaboration formation jointly defines responsibility boundaries and conditions for acceptance and downstream use, grounding capability allocation in contributions that other agents can assess and integrate toward a shared objective. Execution coordination couples agents' local progress through a shared state, using dependencies and supervisory relationships to govern agent activation, result handoffs, and integration while adapting subsequent actions to review feedback and emerging blockers. Experiments spanning long-horizon programming, interactive planning, and professional financial analysis demonstrate AutoSwarm's advantages over single-agent baselines and existing multi-agent frameworks. Source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Not Every Agent Needs the Strongest Model: Unified Prompt-Model Optimization for Heterogeneous LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31743)**  `arXiv:2610.31743`  `cs.MA`  
  _Mingxiu Cai, Haoyang Xian, Xinyu Ma, Yutong Wang, Zhenxi Song, Jing Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on role-specific prompts and execution models to solve complex tasks collaboratively. However, prompt optimization typically keeps model assignments fixed, leaving differences in role requirements and their associated inference costs outside the search. We present UniMASO, a Unified framework for heterogeneous Multi-Agent System Optimization through the joint adaptation of prompts and model assignments. UniMASO evaluates candidate updates through local, downstream, and global feedback, using cost-aware Pareto search to retain useful quality-cost trade-offs. Topology-aware co-evolution then coordinates these updates according to agent roles and dependencies. Cross-level trajectory memory further supplies historical successes and failures to guide subsequent optimization. Experiments on five benchmarks and five execution topologies show that UniMASO achieves competitive task performance at substantially lower deployment cost than the strongest homogeneous configurations. Under centralized execution, it improves the average score from 69.79 to 70.40 while reducing token-weighted deployment cost by 85.2% relative to DeepSeek-V4-Pro with MASPO. These results support joint prompt-model adaptation as an effective approach to efficient multi-agent collaboration. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Theory-Guided LLM Pedagogical Agent Supporting Open-Ended STEM+C Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30539)**  `arXiv:2605.30539`  `cs.MA`  
  _Clayton Cohn, Surya Rayala, Siyuan Guo, Hanchen David Wang, Naveeduddin Mohammed, Umesh Timalsina, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-ended learning environments (OELEs) can support STEM+C (science, technology, engineering, mathematics, and computing) inquiry through activities such as computational modeling, which requires students to translate disciplinary knowledge across domains and coordinate scientific concepts with computational representations. However, this is often challenging for students, many of whom require support during problem solving. In this exploratory study, we present CoSMoAgent (Computational Science Modeling Agent), a GPT-5-based multi-agent knowledgeable peer for high-school kinematics modeling grounded in the Evidence-Decision-Feedback (EDF) framework. CoSMoAgent goes beyond dialogue: it infers students' strategies, learner states, and knowledge gaps from environment logs and evolving model states. These inferences guide dialogue policies that prioritize probing and hints over direct answers. We deployed CoSMoAgent with 11 dyads across three modeling tasks (33 dyad-sessions), using a mixed-methods design to examine learning outcomes, processes, and adaptive scaffolding. As task mastery increased, students verbalized their understanding more successfully and sought help less often, and the agent's policies shifted from probing toward suggesting actions and extending reasoning. Two contrasting case-study dyads followed markedly different problem-solving paths, yet achieved similar learning gains despite substantial differences in task mastery and CoSMoAgent interaction. Students valued the agent's questions but were often frustrated by its withholding of answers. The study contributes a design pattern and classroom evidence for LLM scaffolding grounded in environmental activity rather than chat alone within an open-ended modeling environment.
  </details>

- **[Execution Logs Sway VLM Judges of Robot Progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34151)**  `arXiv:2610.34151`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Weiyan Zhao, Sunny Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models now grade robot videos, and text placed beside the frames can change their verdicts. On clips where the robot is losing progress, we add one log line saying the robot is making progress. Across Gemini, Qwen and GPT judges, this flips correct verdicts to progress 10 to 35 percentage points more often than a neutral line does. A one-line warning that the log may be wrong removes most of this effect. Selected fake "task complete" claims flip half of Qwen's correct verdicts, against a tenth for unrelated log lines. They have no detectable effect on Gemini.
  </details>

- **[Exploring perceptual straightness in learned visual representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34678)**  `arXiv:2610.34678`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Anne Harrington, Vasha DuTell, Ayush Tewari, Mark Hamilton, Simon Stent, Ruth Rosenholtz, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans have been shown to use a "straightened" encoding to represent the natural visual world as it evolves in time (Hénaff et al. 2019). In the context of discrete video sequences, "straightened" means that changes between frames follow a more linear path in representation space at progressively deeper levels of processing. While deep convolutional networks are often proposed as models of human visual processing, many do not straighten natural videos. In this paper, we explore the relationship between network architecture, differing types of robustness, biologically-inspired filtering mechanisms, and representational straightness in response to time-varying input; we identify strengths and limitations of straightness as a useful way of evaluating neural network representations. We find that (1) adversarial training leads to straighter representations in both CNN and transformer-based architectures but (2) this effect is task-dependent, not generalizing to tasks such as segmentation and frame-prediction, where straight representations are not favorable for predictions; and nor to other types of robustness. In addition, (3) straighter representations impart temporal stability to class predictions, even for out-of-distribution data. Finally, (4) biologically-inspired elements increase straightness in the early stages of a network, but do not guarantee increased straightness in downstream layers of CNNs. We show that straightness is an easily computed measure of representational robustness and stability, as well as a hallmark of human representations with benefits for computer vision models.
  </details>

- **[Is Oracle Pruning the True Oracle?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.00143)**  `arXiv:2412.00143`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Sicheng Feng, Keda Tao, Huan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Oracle pruning, which selects unimportant weights by minimizing the pruned train loss, has served as the foundation for most neural network pruning methods for over thirty-five years, while few (if any) have thought about how much the foundation really holds. This paper, for the first time, attempts to systematically examine its validity on deep neural networks through empirical correlation analyses and provides meta-framework reflections on the field of neural network pruning. Specifically, this paper focuses on the pruning algorithms with three stages: training, pruning, and retraining. We analyze the correlation in model performance before and after the retraining stage. Extensive experiments (37K models are trained) across a wide spectrum of models (LeNet5, VGG, ResNets, ViT, MLLM) and datasets (MNIST, CIFAR10/CIFAR100, ImageNet-1K, MLLM data) are conducted. For large-scale experiments, we adopt approximate oracle pruning due to the prohibitive cost of exact oracle pruning. The results point to a counterintuitive conclusion: for deep learning models of nontrivial size (already at the scale of ResNet56 on CIFAR-10), pre-retraining performance is negligibly correlated with post-retraining performance. In other words, the weights identified by oracle pruning can scarcely guarantee strong performance following retraining. This further suggests that existing works that derive pruning criteria from oracle pruning may rest on a questionable foundational premise. Further studies suggest that rising task complexity is a primary factor behind the invalidity of oracle pruning nowadays. Finally, given the evidence, we argue that the retraining stage in a pruning algorithm should be accounted for when developing pruning criteria.
  </details>

- **[S3AC: Stabilized, Selective, and Scalable Actor-Critic for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35518)**  `arXiv:2610.35518`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Shuo Shen, Guangran Cheng, Chengqi Lyu, Xiangsen Wang, Yanan Sun, Wenwei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Critic-free methods are widely used in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, avoiding difficult and costly critic training. Common alternatives lack token-level credit assignment and typically rely on grouped rollouts to estimate prompt-specific baselines. A critic can address both limitations, but long-CoT critic training is hindered by insufficient coverage of trajectories with diverse lengths and early gradients dominated by the newly initialized value head. We introduce S3AC (Stabilized, Selective, and Scalable Actor-Critic) for LLM reasoning, built around a practical critic-training recipe. The trained critic provides token-specific advantages and prompt-specific baselines. Using the prompt-level baseline to estimate difficulty, we propose Difficulty-Aware Prompt Sampling (DAPS). DAPS combines this estimate with outcome history to select prompts before generation, reducing rollout waste without offline difficulty initialization. S3AC improves group-rollout performance across dense and mixture-of-experts models from 1.5B to 397B parameters, outperforming a modern GRPO variant (GRPO-X). At 35B, it sustains single-rollout training while tested critic-free baselines deteriorate after initial gains.
  </details>

- **[GPDPO: Learning from Reward Conflicts via Group Pareto-Dominance Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35480)**  `arXiv:2610.35480`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zixuan Xu, Haoxuan Chen, Jiangxin Sun, Jian-Fang Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-reward reinforcement learning offers a promising framework for aligning large language models (LLMs) with diverse objectives, but conflicting reward signals can induce ambiguous update directions and hinder stable optimization. Existing approaches address such conflicts through reward reweighting, gradient coordination, or rollout filtering, yet they primarily operate on per-response or group-level optimization signals and underexploit relational information among sampled responses. As a result, conflict-filtered rollouts may still contain useful supervision that is discarded during training. We propose Group Pareto-Dominance Policy Optimization (GPDPO), a multi-reward policy optimization method that recovers pairwise supervision from conflict-filtered rollouts. GPDPO identifies Pareto-dominance relations among sampled responses and converts them into auxiliary preference signals, which are jointly optimized with the original policy objective. This enables more effective reuse of existing rollouts without additional sampling or reward evaluation. Experiments on API-Bank across multiple model scales and reward configurations show that GPDPO consistently outperforms competitive multi-reward optimization baselines. Further analysis demonstrates that GPDPO reduces both group-relative and pairwise reward conflicts, supporting the effectiveness of exploiting relational supervision for multi-reward alignment. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning Done Right for Explicit Confidence Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35059)**  `arXiv:2610.35059`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zefeng He, Yichen He, Yuan Lin, Yang Yang, Yu Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can produce plausible but incorrect answers when they are uncertain, which can mislead users and undermine model reliability. Training models to abstain with reinforcement learning is a natural approach, but most existing methods use a fixed error penalty and therefore cannot adapt their behavior to different confidence requirements at inference time. We study \emph{explicit confidence targets}, where a user specifies a target \(p\) in the prompt and the model directly chooses whether to answer or abstain, with the goal of achieving an accuracy of at least \(p\) among answered questions. However, learning to respond to different confidence targets with a single model has proven difficult, and prior attempts have failed to distinguish different targets. We show that this failure comes from two problems: the reward signal that distinguishes different values of $p$ is often lost during optimization, while the original error penalty grows without bound as $p$ approaches one. We propose RLEC (\textbf{R}einforcement \textbf{L}earning with \textbf{E}xplicit \textbf{C}onfidence Targets), which preserves the learning signal needed to distinguish confidence targets, introduces a stable reward family that keeps the desired answer condition unchanged, and uses reward scheduling to balance effective learning and training stability. Together, these designs enable the first successful training of a single LLM for explicit confidence target control. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and model scales show that RLEC largely preserves general capabilities and achieves competitive calibration performance. RLEC also supports fine-grained target control without an external decision system or threshold calibration after training. By making explicit confidence targets part of the trained policy, RLEC opens a new and more direct path toward behavioral calibration.
  </details>

- **[Priority-Preserving Advantage Aggregation for Multi-Reward Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34862)**  `arXiv:2610.34862`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xiangchi Yuan, Xingjian Diao, Zheyuan Liu, Chunhui Zhang, Dachuan Shi, Kejing Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM post-training often optimizes multiple objectives with unequal priorities, such as correctness and reasoning efficiency. However, the widely used additive reward aggregation in RL can violate these priorities: when primary and auxiliary advantages conflict, a sufficiently large auxiliary signal can overturn the preference induced by the primary objective. We term this problem \emph{advantage priority inversion}. Although reward-level gating can protect priority more directly, it still removes potentially useful auxiliary distinctions among rollout samples that fail the primary condition. To address this trade-off, we propose Primary Advantage Policy Optimization (\textbf{PAPO}), a priority-aware advantage aggregation method that asymmetrically combines a primary and an auxiliary advantage. PAPO retains auxiliary supervision when the objectives agree and smoothly attenuates conflicting auxiliary signals, preserving the primary sign during fusion for \emph{primary-consistent ordering}. This rule applies to sequence-level advantages in GRPO and token-level advantages in actor--critic PPO. Experiments on multiple domains and models show that PAPO consistently achieves the best primary objective while maintaining best or competitive auxiliary-objective performance across both GRPO- and PPO-based optimization. \href{this https URL}{Code is available}.
  </details>

- **[Mechanistic Insights into LoRA: Layer Sparsity for Adaptive Fine-Tuning via Path Patching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34756)**  `arXiv:2610.34756`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Fei Zuo, Yizhou Huang, Kezhi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has emerged as the dominant paradigm for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models. Standard all-layer LoRA applies adaptation throughout the transformer, despite heterogeneous contributions from its components. In this work, we conduct a systematic mechanistic interpretability analysis to understand how LoRA affects model internals. We observe that LoRA's adaptation effect is highly non-uniform, with significant component-wise and layer-wise sparsity. Most critically, we find that important layers are highly task-dependent, with limited overlap across tasks. Motivated by these insights, we propose Adaptive-LoRA, a calibration-driven method that automatically identifies task-specific layers via frozen-base zero-ablation, enabling efficient layer selection without exhaustive search. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-1.5B across 11 diverse reasoning tasks demonstrate that Adaptive-LoRA outperforms full-layer LoRA while using only 30% of layers, achieving a $3.5\times$ reduction in adapter parameters. This work bridges mechanistic interpretability and practical efficiency, providing both fundamental insights and a principled approach for adaptive fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Self-Evolution Reasoner: Continuous Optimization via Policy-Intrinsic Exploration and Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34137)**  `arXiv:2610.34137`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Wenhang Shi, Yiren Chen, Zhe Zhao, Jinhao Dong, Wei Lu, Xiaoyong Du_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) enables large language models (LLMs) to continuously self-improve their reasoning capabilities. Compared to RLVR's scalar rewards, recent self-supervised on-policy distillation methods leverage denser supervision signals. However, both paradigms suffer from exploration bottlenecks, struggling to escape local optima when the policy fails to sample valid solutions. Relying on external demonstrations can bypass this issue; however, the off-policy nature hinders genuine self-improvement and the mismatched-distribution reasoning can not be fully exploited. In this work, we propose Self-Evolution Policy Optimization (SEPO), a framework that drives continuous reasoning capability evolution via guided exploration and in-distribution exploitation. Specifically, SEPO utilizes labels to elicit correct reasoning from the policy, unlocking the exploration potential. By treating current policy conditioned on this in-distribution reasoning as a self-teacher, SEPO distills the newly discovered solution back into the policy. Through this iterative cycle of policy-intrinsic exploration and exploitation, SEPO achieves robust and continuous capability evolution. Across scientific, tool-use, and complex logical reasoning domains, SEPO consistently achieves the best performance under identical training time or steps, demonstrating superior efficiency and convergence ceilings. Notably, SEPO maintains stable improvements even in scenarios where baselines severely underperform or fail entirely, such as on complex tasks or with small-scale models. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Implicit Beliefs as Scalable Process Rewards for Strategic Persuasion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33527)**  `arXiv:2610.33527`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Minqian Liu, Yuexi Shen, Heajun An, Qi Zhang, Pamela J. Wisniewski, Sang Won Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models (LLMs) for multi-turn strategic persuasion faces two coupled obstacles: (i) realistic simulation of psychologically grounded persuadees, and (ii) dense, faithful supervision for credit assignment in strategic dialogues. Outcome-only rewards are sparse and uninformative about which conversational moves drove success, while LLM-judge process rewards are expensive and decoupled from the underlying interaction dynamics. We address both challenges with a unified framework. First, we introduce PersuArena, a persuasion simulation environment that produces realistic, non-monotonic trajectories. Second, building directly on this environment, we propose Process Rewards via Online Belief Elicitation for Relative Policy Optimization (Probe-RPO), an online RL framework that converts the persuadee's internal state into dense process rewards. At each turn, three introspective probes are read from the frozen persuadee's logits, yielding a multi-dimensional reward signal that is faithfully aligned with persuasion dynamics. With extensive experiments on PersuArena spanning healthcare, financial advising, workplace management, education, and personal relationships, we demonstrate that our Probe-RPO achieves the full acceptance rate of 52.5%, compared with the 35.8% for the strongest GRPO ablation and 25.8% for the best off-the-shelf LLM. We also conduct comprehensive analyses on the generalization across persuadee models and the persuasion dynamics across conversations. Our work offers a promising paradigm that can provide effective process supervision for strategic dialogue.
  </details>

- **[Mark, Don't Erase: Token Inoculation for Dual-Use Knowledge in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18639)**  `arXiv:2607.18639`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Seunghyun Lee, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In a dual-use field, hazardous knowledge and the benign expertise around it share vocabulary, concepts, and causal structure. That overlap makes selective refusal hard, and existing methods such as unlearning and refusal training overgeneralize their suppression into the benign side. We argue for conditioning, not suppression. In this work, we introduce Token Inoculation, which marks hazardous sentences with a special token, the inoculation token, during continued pre-training. We then fine-tune the model to refuse hazardous questions and, on the same questions under the inoculation token, to predict the hazardous answers. At test time the inoculation token is absent, so the model does not express the hazardous answers it learned to predict. Across 1.5B to 14B parameters and three architecture families it holds hazardous accuracy near 18% while retaining 93% of the instruct baseline's MMLU performance, the best safety-utility balance among the baselines we compare. The same advantage reproduces under open-ended generation and in a cybersecurity transfer. We test this with a causal do(X) analysis that randomizes the accuracy of the conditioning signal, and find that refusal becomes more selective the more of the hazardous domain the model still knows. Our findings underscore the importance of training to predict the responses that must be refused, and outline a direction for safety alignment that does not degrade models' growing frontier knowledge. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TellOnce: Correcting Coding Agents Once, Improving Their Future Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13174)**  `arXiv:2606.13174`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yujun Zhou, Kehan Guo, Haomin Zhuang, Xiangqi Wang, Yue Huang, Zhenwen Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive LLM agents increasingly work with users across tasks, yet remembering a previous correction does not ensure that the agent changes its behavior in the future. In a diagnostic constructed from anonymized real-world agent interactions, agents using Mem0 still violate 57.5% of applicable preference checks. We introduce TellOnce, a drop-in personalization layer that turns conversational corrections into persistent, scoped, and revisable behavioral rules without updating the underlying agent model. TellOnce identifies which requirements should persist, determines when they apply, reconciles subsequent feedback with existing rules, and prepares reusable checks when reliable verification is possible. We evaluate whether learned corrections improve future behavior by freezing the learned state and testing on subsequent tasks without additional user corrections. Across three models on ClawArena and MemoryArena, TellOnce achieves the lowest preference violation rate in all six settings, reducing violations relative to PGMem by 19.3-42.3 percentage points on ClawArena and 3.0-22.9 points on MemoryArena, while maintaining broadly comparable task performance. On SWE-Together, TellOnce further improves task quality on new coding tasks by learning from feedback received during earlier tasks. Experiment code is available atthis https URL, and the deployable skill atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Short Convolutions Improve Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03825)**  `arXiv:2606.03825`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Oliver Sieberling, Bharat Runwal, Rameswar Panda, Yoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers have become the dominant architecture for large language models, largely due to the scalability and flexibility of attention, feed-forward layers, residual connections, and normalization. This paper introduces dynamic short convolutions as an additional neural network primitive for improving Transformers. Unlike static short convolutions, dynamic convolutions use input-dependent filters, which preserves the locality bias of convolution while increasing expressivity. Motivating experiments show that applying dynamic short convolutions to key, query, and value representations improves performance on challenging associative recall tasks compared with static convolutional variants. Across language-modeling experiments ranging from 150M to 2B parameters, dynamic convolutions consistently outperform standard Transformers and Transformers augmented with static short convolutions. Fitting scaling laws indicates a 1.33$\times$ compute advantage over compute-matched Transformers when dynamic convolutions are applied to the key, query, and value vectors, and a 1.60$\times$ advantage when adding dynamic convolutions after every linear layer. Dynamic convolutions also offer improvements on linear RNNs (Mamba-2/Gated DeltaNet) and mixture-of-experts architectures. We make these gains practical with custom Triton kernels that enable efficient training with a manageable end-to-end slowdown. These results suggest that dynamic short convolutions are a scalable, hardware-efficient, and expressive primitive for advancing Transformer-based language models.
  </details>

- **[Fast KVzip: Efficient and Accurate LLM Inference with Gated KV Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17668)**  `arXiv:2601.17668`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jang-Hyun Kim, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient key-value (KV) cache management is crucial for the practical deployment of large language models (LLMs), yet existing compression techniques often incur a trade-off between performance degradation and computational overhead. We propose a novel gating-based KV cache eviction method for frozen-weight LLMs that achieves high compression ratios with negligible computational cost. Our approach introduces lightweight sink-attention gating modules to identify and retain critical KV pairs, and integrates seamlessly into both the prefill and decoding stages. The proposed gate training algorithm relies on forward passes of an LLM, avoiding expensive backpropagation, while achieving strong task generalization through a task-agnostic reconstruction objective. Extensive experiments across the Qwen2.5-1M, Qwen3, and Gemma3 families show that our method maintains near-lossless performance while evicting up to 70% of the KV cache. The results are consistent across a wide range of tasks, including long-context understanding, code comprehension, and mathematical reasoning, demonstrating the generality of our approach.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Language Models without Labels: Majority Drives Selection, Novelty Promotes Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15194)**  `arXiv:2509.15194`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yujun Zhou, Zhenwen Liang, Haolin Liu, Wenhao Yu, Kishan Panaganti, Linfeng Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can improve through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), but many settings lack reference answers or external verifiers. Label-free self-improvement instead constructs training signals from the model's own outputs. Because the same policy generates both the candidate responses and the signals derived from them, each update also changes the candidates available for future training, creating a self-reinforcing feedback loop. We identify a failure mode of this loop, which we call selection-induced diversity collapse: repeated self-selection can progressively narrow the candidate pool and make alternative correct solutions harder to sample. To address this problem, we propose EVOL-RL, which balances selection and variation. Majority voting provides a stable selection signal, while embedding-based novelty refines credit among responses with the same selection status; entropy regularization and asymmetric clipping further support this process by maintaining diverse rollouts and preserving rare, high-advantage updates. Across multiple training distributions, model scales, backbones, and matched label-free baselines, EVOL-RL consistently improves pass@n while generally maintaining or improving pass@1. For example, when trained without labels on AIME24, Qwen3-4B-Base improves over TTRL on held-out AIME25 from 4.6% to 17.1% in pass@1 and from 18.5% to 42.0% in pass@16. Training analyses further show that when the current majority is wrong, EVOL-RL better preserves alternative correct solutions. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Continual Learning Exam: Benchmarking Long-Horizon RSI and Experiential Learning in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33956)**  `arXiv:2610.33956`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyu Zhao, Sitian Tu, Yuxuan Huang, Huichi Zhou, Zhangyue Yin, Jiahao Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), a large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as a five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare a single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harness-model configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield a median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Can JEV Support Clinical Trial Criteria Assessment?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35721)**  `arXiv:2610.35721`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Mengxian He, Ziyue Gao, Chun Li, Shunyi Zhang, Hao Wei, Minqing Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical trial prescreening requires distinguishing unmet conditions from insufficient evidence in patient records. Given patient records, criterion questions, and predefined answer options, we retrospectively compared JEV, a non-generative decision model returning native option probabilities, with schema-constrained Qwen Flash across 2,048 test patient-condition pairs from TrialGPT and question-answer datasets derived from MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, all evaluated under strict Zero Data Retention (ZDR) policies to ensure clinical data privacy. Both models assessed conditions individually or in batches sharing the same patient context, with performance measured against reference labels. In batch mode, JEV and Qwen achieved similar macro-F1 scores on TrialGPT (0.652 vs. 0.660) and MIMIC-IV (0.807 vs. 0.814), whereas JEV substantially outperformed Qwen on MIMIC-III (0.768 vs. 0.534; $\Delta$ = +0.234, 95% CI: 0.187-0.281). This advantage largely reflected higher recall for conditions labeled as having insufficient information (56.3% vs. 9.9%). Across all three datasets, JEV also had lower observed median job latency (1.18-1.40 vs. 2.80-8.90 s) and 53%-74% lower estimated cost per condition. These findings position JEV as an initial assessment component that provides criterion-level judgments and flags potential information gaps for review. JEV's observed errors in interpreting clinical evidence underscore the need to verify its outputs against patient records before they inform eligibility decisions.
  </details>

- **[Entropic Control for Homeostatic Optimization: Pre-empting Catastrophic Forgetting via Dynamic Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34182)**  `arXiv:2610.34182`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shangxi Wu, Xiaorong Yan, Xiaojun Meng, Yuxiang Zhang, Jiansheng Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) of LLMs suffers from catastrophic forgetting, and prevailing mitigation strategies struggle to reconcile the plasticity-stability dilemma. To address this, we propose Entropic Control for Homeostatic Optimization (ECHO), a closed-loop control framework. By decomposing the KL divergence, we identify output entropy as a dominant observable component of KL variation. This enables us to leverage entropy as a computationally efficient online proxy to monitor forgetting risks in real-time, bypassing expensive reference-model inference. Consequently, ECHO employs an elastic controller that dynamically modulates the gradient weight of replay data via a proportional control law, using deviations from the pre-trained entropic equilibrium as an error signal. Empirical evaluations across 0.6B to 9B parameter architectures demonstrate that ECHO lies on, and in several comparisons extends, the observed plasticity-stability frontier and maintains high operational robustness against varying data mixing ratios.
  </details>

- **[Counterfactual Test-Time Augmentation for Shortcut Removal in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33904)**  `arXiv:2610.33904`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Katie Matton, Rosalind W. Picard, John Guttag, Maggie Makar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can exploit shortcuts, i.e., correlations present in training data that may not hold at test time. This can lead to poor predictions when correlations shift. Most existing solutions for removing shortcuts operate at training time, requiring labeled training data, model weight access, and significant computational resources. Here, we study the problem of test-time shortcut removal. We propose a family of counterfactual test-time augmentation (CTTA) methods: given an example, each method uses an auxiliary model to generate counterfactual edits in which the shortcut value is changed, and then takes a weighted average of the LLM's predictions across these edits. We prove that the optimal shortcut-invariant predictor is a member of this CTTA family, and we characterize its weights. The weights depend on the training distribution, and therefore are, in general, not identifiable from the trained model and unlabeled test data. However, we present assumptions under which the weights can be obtained using the token probabilities of the trained LLM, and propose tests to detect violations of these assumptions. We use these tests to develop Select-CTTA, an aggregate method that selects among CTTA methods. Empirically, we find that Select-CTTA outperforms test-time baselines and approaches the performance of models fine-tuned on shortcut-balanced data.
  </details>

- **[Evolving to Match Capabilities: Improving Harness Self-Evolution via Skill Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33024)**  `arXiv:2610.33024`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhihao Yang, Haojin Wang, Juncheng Wu, Kuan Zhang, Yukun Chen, Run Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness self-evolution offers a practical route for LLM agents to improve from execution experience without updating model parameters. Its effectiveness depends on whether models can successfully act on the guidance provided by their harnesses. However, we find that harness benefits are not uniform across model capabilities: skills that improve stronger models can provide little benefit or even degrade performance for weaker ones. Across seven models and three benchmarks, we identify 103 task-skill pairs where skills improve GPT-5.6-luna by 31.1 points but degrade Gemma-4-E4B by 8.8 points. In 86% of failed weak-model runs, the violated requirement is already stated in the skill. This motivates skill adaptation: reformulating existing skills to match the executor's capabilities and make their guidance easier to execute. To achieve this, we diagnose failures in tool execution, context management, instruction following, and verification, and distill these diagnoses into a fixed meta-skill that guides adaptation during harness self-evolution. Guided by the meta-skill, a capable external rewriter, Kimi-K3, adapts skills without execution evidence, improving the three weak executors by 5.1-14.1 points over the original skills. We then incorporate this adaptation into a five-round self-evolution loop, where each model revises its own skill from execution trajectories and verifier feedback. Across seven executors, from Gemma-4-E4B to Kimi-K2.5, the meta-skill consistently improves final scores by 2.0-5.1 points over the same loop without the meta-skill. Our findings provide practical design guidance for developing more effective harness self-evolution methods.
  </details>

- **[CausalMix: Data Mixture as Causal Inference for Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01104)**  `arXiv:2607.01104`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zinan Tang, Yukun Zhang, Shaomian Zheng, Zhuoshi Pan, Qizhi Pei, Dingnan Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Large Language Model (LLM) training, data mixing plays a pivotal role in determining model performance. Recent methods optimize mixture weights via proxy models, but they rely on the assumption of static data distributions. As a result, when the underlying data pool shifts, these methods require costly retraining from scratch. This limitation restricts their ability to scale seamlessly from small settings to larger data pools and model sizes. In this paper, we propose CausalMix to address this limitation by casting data mixture optimization as a causal inference problem. We formulate the statistical features of the data pool as covariates and the domain mixture as the treatment. After fitting a causal model on 512 runs of Qwen2.5-0.5B to estimate the Conditional Average Treatment Effect (CATE), we extrapolate the optimal mixture for an 800K data pool and apply it to train a 7B model. Furthermore, we successfully generalize the framework to long chain-of-thought data on Qwen3-4B-Base. By leveraging causal modeling to isolate confounding biases, CausalMix infers state-dependent data mixtures offline, with the selected mixture fixed during each training run. Extensive experiments show that the mixture guided by CausalMix achieves competitive performance across downstream tasks compared with other baselines. The CATE Interpreter visualizes the learned mixing strategy. Overall, CausalMix offers a causal and interpretable framework for optimizing LLM data mixtures.
  </details>

- **[Mathematical Reasoning via Intervention-Based Time-Series Causal Discovery Using LLMs as Concept Mastery Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07600)**  `arXiv:2605.07600`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Tsuyoshi Okita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent methods for improving LLM mathematical reasoning, whether through MCTS-based test-time search or causal graph-guided knowledge injection, cannot identify which concepts causally contribute to a correct answer, as the observed association may be spurious, driven by confounders such as problem difficulty.We propose CIKA (Causal Intervention for Knowledge Activation), a framework that uses the LLM itself as an interventional simulator: a prompt sets the concept state to ``mastered'' and the correctness change estimates the causal effect. We formalize this quantity as an Interventional Capability Probe (ICP), which diagnoses whether the LLM can use a given concept -- distinct from merely possessing knowledge. Because the intervention exogenously sets the concept state independently of problem difficulty, ICP separates confounding that observational methods cannot.On 67 screened problems, the ICP of the top-ranked concept (+0.219) is significantly larger than that of the negative control (+0.039; paired $t$-test, $p < 10^{-6}$, Cohen's $d = 0.86$), confirming that the probe discriminates causally relevant concepts from irrelevant ones. Analysis of 601 Omni-MATH problems further shows that solved problems have 6.1$\times$ higher ATE than unsolved ones (0.338 vs. 0.055), confirming that ICP is predictive of problem-solving success. With a 7B-parameter LLM whose weights are entirely frozen, CIKA achieves 69.7\% on the contamination-free Omni-MATH-Rule benchmark and 64.0\% overall, compared to 60.5\% for o1-mini, and 97.2\% on GSM8K, 46--50\% on AIME 2024--2026, and 46.2\% on MathArena. The Causal Knowledge Activation component contributes 33.8\% of correct answers on problems where the base model alone fails, demonstrating that the LLM already possessed but had not activated the requisite knowledge.
  </details>

- **[GraphDancer: Training LLMs to Explore and Reason over Graphs via Curriculum Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02518)**  `arXiv:2602.02518`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuyang Bai, Zhuofeng Li, Ping Nie, Jianwen Xie, Yu Wang, Yu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly rely on external knowledge to improve factuality, yet many knowledge sources on the Web, such as scholarly indexes and product catalogs, are organized as heterogeneous graphs. Reasoning over such graph-structured knowledge poses two key challenges: (1) navigating structured, schema-defined relations requires precise function calls beyond similarity-based retrieval, and (2) answering complex questions often demands multi-hop evidence aggregation through iterative information seeking. We study how LLMs can learn these interaction capabilities and transfer them to unseen domains. We propose GraphDancer, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that teaches LLMs to navigate graphs by interleaving reasoning and function execution. To improve RL for moderate-sized LLMs, we introduce a graph-aware curriculum that schedules training by the structural difficulty of questions using an easy-to-hard biased sampler. We evaluate GraphDancer on the multi-domain GRBench benchmark, training only on Academic and Biomedical graphs.
