Testing covers held-out questions from these domains as well as unseen E-commerce, Literature, and Legal graphs. With either Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct or Qwen3-14B as the backbone, GraphDancer outperforms graph-agent baselines using the same backbone across all evaluation domains. These results indicate that the learned graph exploration and reasoning capabilities transfer across domains. On average across unseen domains, GraphDancer with a 14B backbone also outperforms baselines powered by the closed-source models GPT-4.1-mini and GPT-5-nano. Trajectory analyses further show that GraphDancer retrieves answer-bearing evidence more often and achieves higher accuracy when such evidence is found, while substantially reducing invalid-protocol outcomes.
  </details>

- **[Audit Where Hacks Pay: Budgeted Strong-Verifier Audits Against Reward Hacking in GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35716)**  `arXiv:2610.35716`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shurun Li, Michael Hua Wang, Haibo Zeng, Long Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards trains on cheap checks, such as unit tests, that a policy can learn to game, while a stronger check is too expensive to run on every rollout. We study how to spend a limited strong-verifier budget in GRPO, separating which weakly passing rollouts to audit from what reward a caught hack receives. At a fixed policy state with centered advantages, the expected update depends on a design only through each rollout's marginal audit probability and correction; reflecting a caught hack's reward about the failure reward negates the advantages of an all-hack group; and auditing whole groups with the fewest weak passes first (low-pass budgeted audits, LB) minimizes the advantage mass left on hacks among pass-count designs with a fixed correction when a label's value falls with the pass count. We tested this analysis in actual LLM training: from each run's starting state, the sign of this mass on a frozen panel predicted whether hacking would be suppressed in 33 of 39 runs, including 13 of 16 preregistered ones. In LeetCode (Wong et al., 2026) and Countdown-Code (Khalifa et al., 2026), LB drives hacking to near zero, while random audits with the same penalty and budget do not and use more labels. We also find that the randomness of the update affects training: in Countdown-Code, two unbiased corrections, per-item reward-level IPW and a group-level advantage correction, bring hacking down too but end 6.1-11.8 and 8.0-11.5 points lower in held-out accuracy than LB at the same budget. A full audit, which the group-level correction reproduces in expectation, stays within about two points of LB in three seeds, while LB uses 28% of its labels, and allocating the same correction by pass count recovers most of the loss (one seed). Training outcomes thus depend not only on the expected update but also on the randomness of its estimate.
  </details>

- **[SkillLifeBench: Benchmarking Security Across the Lifecycle of Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35215)**  `arXiv:2610.35215`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenxu Zhang, Zhou Liu, Wu Xin, Wentao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web agents reuse skills whose stored code, selected version, and loaded implementation can diverge across tasks and updates. SkillLifeBench links these states to task outcomes in 388 paired scenarios spanning implementation, interface, runtime, composition, and version changes. We evaluate 14 models on two paired decision tracks: 89 runtime tasks and 60 autonomous lifecycle tasks spanning 12 templates derived from introduced cases. Lifecycle tasks allow agents to inspect, select, and reconfigure skills while completing verifiable user requests. Joint success requires task completion without the registered attack goal. GPT-5.6-Sol and Sonnet 4.6 both achieve 82.0% on runtime tasks, but reach 96.7% and 56.7% on lifecycle tasks. Across lifecycle evaluations, challenged task success is 77.5%, while joint success is 65.2%; 103 runs complete the task and the attack goal. Adding execution provenance in a matched recovery study raises utility joint success from 49.35% with logs and metadata to 65.58%. A deterministic execution-consistency rule reaches 64.94% with higher benign utility, while full restoration reaches 69.48%. Lifecycle tasks distinguish models with similar runtime scores, and execution evidence improves recovery.
  </details>

- **[ParetoRSI-Gym: Can AI Agents Improve Language Models Across Multiple Domains?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34386)**  `arXiv:2610.34386`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haojin Wang, Yangzhen Wu, Zeyi Chen, Jian Xie, Jiaying Ye, Lili Meng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires an agentic system to design, train, and evaluate its own successor. Data-driven post-training is a central part of that loop, yet existing evaluations measure it on a single benchmark, where an agent selects from a static pool or distills a stronger model to push one score up in one run. Real post-training is multi-domain, and a gain in one domain has to be weighed against a regression in another. We propose ParetoRSI-Gym, in which an agent searches for data, curates it, trains, reads back per-domain results, and repeats, with everything but the data held fixed: the initial LLM, the five domains (math, code, finance, medicine, science), the toolset, and the SFT pipeline. Generalization benchmarks in each domain separate capability gains from overfitting to the scores the agent optimizes against. Across three agent systems, three base models, five rounds and multiple seeds, the strongest system, Claude Code with Opus-5, raises the generalization scores of its proxy-best checkpoint by 9.2% relative to the starting model (+2.3 absolute points), averaged with equal weight over three base models and five domains, with a single-domain relative gain of up to 63% (medicine on Ministral-3-3B). Further analysis shows that the harness matters as much as the model it drives, and that each agent keeps a unique data selection strategy. We see our work as a step towards evaluation of RSI systems in real-world machine learning engineering.
  </details>

- **[SHIFT-JEV: Certified Abstention and Gain-Gated Continual Adaptation for LLM-Based Decision Models under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34329)**  `arXiv:2610.34329`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Atif Saeed, Azka Atiq, Adarsh Kumar, Akhtar Jamil, Uzair Iqbal, Roland Aydin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can act as decision models that return a probability distribution over a fixed set of options, but deployed decision models face domain, label-prior, emerging-class and candidate-set shift. We study how such models fail under shift and propose SHIFT-Jev, a monitoring, abstention and continual-adaptation procedure for them. It scores the risk of each decision from uncertainty, format and option-consistency signals, certifies abstention thresholds with Learn-then-Test, monitors the decision stream with drift and label-shift detectors, and accepts a model update only when an expected-gain gate finds a significant held-out improvement without loss on earlier regimes. On a benchmark of ten shifted test environments with Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-4B (three seeds each) and Phi-4-mini, fine-tuning with KL anchors and temperature scaling lowers calibration error under shift from 0.289 to 0.087. At a target risk of 10%, a learned uncertainty score with a certified threshold exceeds the target in 3 to 5 of 27 environment-seed tests, against 5 to 15 for a max-probability score with the same certification and 9 to 14 for conformal prediction sets; drift-based routing changes little. A chi-squared test on predicted labels detects 95 to 99% of class-prior shifts that confidence- and embedding-based detectors miss. In continual adaptation, SHIFT-Jev reaches 70.5% accuracy on Qwen3-0.6B, above replay, LwF, EWC and fine-tuning baselines in every seed, whereas on Qwen3-4B its conservative gate leaves it 0.4 to 1.4 points behind them.
  </details>

- **[DoubleFree:Exploiting Exploration History for Rollout-Free Multi-Task LLMs Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34272)**  `arXiv:2610.34272`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yingping Wang, Min Cai, Chenyu Yuan, Xi Ye, Zhijiang Guo, Jiayi Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-task post-training can develop domain specialists through independent reinforcement learning (RL) and consolidate their capabilities through multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). However, standard MOPD generates on-policy student responses and keeps multiple teachers online throughout consolidation, introducing substantial system costs. We view this pipeline as domain-wise exploration followed by cross-domain exploitation. Domain RL produces not only the final specialists, but also the trajectories collected throughout their training. Existing methods primarily use the former while leaving the latter underutilized. We introduce DoubleFree, which reuses these historical trajectories for rollout-free consolidation and precomputes teacher supervision for online-teacher-free student training. To understand why this works, we analyze when updates from historical trajectories can approximate on-policy distillation updates and how combining trajectories across exploration stages can better support an evolving student. DoubleFree requires only minimal changes to existing post-training frameworks. Across math, code, and instruction following on Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and SmolLM3-3B, DoubleFree achieves $2.99$-$5.75\times$ consolidation speedups and reduces GPU-hours by $80.9$-$90.1\%$, while maintaining comparable multi-domain performance.
  </details>

- **[SRA: Selective Residual Adaptation for LLM Reasoning Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34199)**  `arXiv:2610.34199`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenxing Dou, Kun He, Mingyang Yi, Yong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation transfers reasoning capabilities from large language models to lightweight students. However, distilled small models still struggle with multi-step reasoning. We observe that the distribution divergences between teacher and student models along reasoning paths are highly sparse, and target tokens almost always fall within the student top-$K$ candidate set. Based on this finding, unlike approaches updating student backbones or requiring runtime teacher computation, we propose Selective Residual Adaptation (SRA), a plug-and-play framework that internalizes localized corrections. Concretely, SRA employs a Discrepancy Gate to identify divergence positions alongside a Candidate Residual Adapter for top-$K$ logit calibration to correct the mismatch. Theoretically, we establish error bounds ensuring strict sequence level performance gains. Across mathematical benchmarks on Qwen3 models from 0.6B to 4B, SRA consistently enhances distilled student models, raising average Pass@16 by up to 4.37 percentage points while preserving general capabilities.
  </details>

- **[BriLLM-MetaPred: A Representation-Prediction Unity Language Model with Adaptive Self-Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34018)**  `arXiv:2610.34018`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Junyuan Liu, Hongyue Lin, Jiale Hong, Yi Zhao, Hai Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present BriLLM-MetaPred, the first engineering implementation of the MetaPred machine-learning framework established by a mathematical theory of AGI, instantiated for language modeling. The theory argues that MetaPred is both necessary and sufficient as a machine-learning primitive for general cognition. Taking this theoretical conclusion as a design premise, we report an implementation of the primitive for language modeling. A single continuously evolving dynamical state serves as both the context representation and the state from which predictions are read out. Continuous measurement of its own state reduces finite-depth inconsistency, maintains internal consistency, and provides an endogenous dynamical self-supervision signal in fixed-point form. The single dynamical state of MetaPred intrinsically couples training and inference. MetaPred therefore exhibits symmetry across almost all key elements of machine learning, an intuitive and observable property of the mathematical optimum over the space of all machine-learning paradigms. BriLLM-MetaPred is neither another recurrent language model nor an extension of BriLLM. It provides a bridge between two prior contributions: the construction and motivation of the MetaPred primitive provided by AGI theory, and the SiFu learning paradigm provided by BriLLM (Zhao et al., 2025). The latter's node-signal-flow design enables the primitive to run on available software and hardware accelerators. Our implementation of BriLLM-MetaPred achieves predictive performance comparable to a GPT baseline of the same size. This provides initial evidence for the engineering feasibility of the MetaPred framework established by the mathematical theory of AGI. MetaPred is the first machine-learning paradigm derived entirely from mathematical theory rather than assembled and incrementally improved through human design---and perhaps the last such paradigm.
  </details>

- **[A Distribution-Matching Perspective on LLM Training Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33740)**  `arXiv:2610.33740`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanxing Huang, Bingzhang Yan, Zihan Tang, Wenzhe Li, Peng Li, Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we propose a unified framework for training algorithms across different stages of modern large language model development, based on the perspective of distribution matching. We show that a broad class of language model training algorithms can be interpreted as minimizing a certain divergence between the current model and a target distribution, and that characterizing the properties of this target distribution provides substantial insight into the behavior of the corresponding training algorithms. By analyzing specific forms of the target distribution, we establish that On-Policy Distillation (OPD) enjoys an advantage over classical Knowledge Distillation (KD) when the current model distribution is close to the target distribution, while exhibiting a disadvantage when the two distributions are far apart. Furthermore, when the target distribution itself depends on the model being optimized, we show that the accumulation of estimation variance can induce a deterministic degradation of the target distribution. These results provide a theoretical explanation, at least in part, for the long-term behavior of existing Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms. With this intuition we further designed Conditional KL (CKL) regularization for mitigating the degradation of the target distribution. CKL can effectively preserve mode diversity while relaxing the constraint imposed by classical KL regularization on reward improvements. We believe this theoretical perspective can bring some new understanding of language model training algorithms and provide useful guidance for future research.
  </details>

- **[When Does Pre-Trained Capacity Help Causal Learning? From Scalar Prediction to Counterfactual Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33311)**  `arXiv:2610.33311`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qi Xu, Zijie Lei, Zhifei Deng, Zhigang Hua, Luke Simon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does pre-trained model capacity relate to performance after adaptation for causal tasks? We study two systems built on the Qwen 2.5 family: CausalLLM, which estimates scalar potential outcomes with treatment-specific heads, and CausalGen, which generates treatment-conditioned text or code using soft prefixes and factual supervised fine-tuning (SFT), counterfactual SFT, and preference alignment. Increasing backbone size from 0.5B to 1.5B yields mixed scalar-estimation results on IHDP, but improves CodeGen pass@1 from 0.267 to 0.575 and treatment-effect score from 0.231 to 0.515 after the complete generative pipeline. Within 0.5B CausalLLM, narrative inputs reduce mean sqrt(PEHE) from 1.842 to 1.780, a 3.4% improvement over tabular inputs; the 5.8% improvement over a no-LLM baseline combines changes in representation and input format. These descriptive comparisons motivate a distinction between available and convertible capacity. They do not isolate the contribution of post-training: the two systems differ in dataset, architecture, output modality, and supervision, and intermediate-stage evaluations are unavailable. With only two small backbones and three replications, the results support a hypothesis about capacity--adaptation alignment rather than a general scaling law or a demonstrated causal mechanism.
  </details>

- **[ProAS: A Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping Framework for Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18683)**  `arXiv:2610.18683`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bowen Ding, Mingda Wan, Yuhan Chen, Jiyao Yuan, Qi Zhu, Futing Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as the primary paradigm for optimizing Large Language Model (LLM) agents, yet efficient critic-free algorithms (e.g., GRPO) suffer from a fundamental uniform credit assignment dilemma that broadcasts trajectory-level advantages equally to all actions. Our analysis reveals that this uniformity inherently reinforces structurally unreasonable actions in preferred trajectories and ignores each action's downstream deviations in unpreferred ones. To resolve this without introducing critic overhead, we propose Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping (ProAS), a plug-and-play framework that leverages a dynamically evolving Agentic Reference Graph (ARG) to achieve fine-grained credit assignment via Masking Unreasonable Incentives (MUI) and Asymmetric Penalty Redistribution (APR). Extensive experiments across Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct demonstrate that ProAS universally elevates mainstream group-based baselines to new state-of-the-art performance, achieving peak success rates of 91.9% and 94.3% on ALFWorld, alongside 66.7% and 70.8% on WebShop, respectively. Furthermore, it accelerates training convergence by up to $2.44\times$ while maintaining strong robustness against imperfect or distilled ARGs. Codes:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Pick the Mixture, Not the Samples: Combinatorial Data Selection for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14028)**  `arXiv:2610.14028`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Luyang Zhang, Jialu Wang, Bebei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data selection for LLM fine-tuning faces two facts about real training: data pools draw from many distinct domains, and evaluation spans diverse tasks. As a result, the same sample helps different tasks differently, and samples interact during training so that a subset's value is not the sum of its parts. Current data-selection methods leave these realities largely unaddressed. We formalize them as Combinatorial Data Selection (CDS), a framework that separates attribution (how samples are scored) from selection (which subset to pick) and models the non-additive interactions among samples. On a real instruction-tuning data pool, we find substantial cross-domain complementarity (joint utility exceeds the sum of singletons), alongside redundancy within domains. Inspired by this, we propose Mixture-then-Select, a two-stage algorithm that first decides how many samples to take from each domain, then ranks samples within each domain by attribution score. Theoretically, the gap to the optimal subset is linear in the selection size and independent of data pool size. Empirically, Mixture-then-Select outperforms strong baselines by 20 to 54%, is robust across attribution methods, and works across different models and data pool sizes. The framework also extends to the data purchasing problem under a budget constraint when sample prices vary. Our findings suggest that allocating selection across domains offers a promising path toward more efficient and effective data selection for modern LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Unbiased Alignment for Large Language Models with Noisy Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03248)**  `arXiv:2607.03248`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jialiang Wang, Xianming Liu, Xiong Zhou, Hui Liu, Haoliang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The alignment of large language models with human preferences is commonly achieved through Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback or Direct Preference Optimization. However, these methods are vulnerable to the significant noise prevalent in real-world preference datasets. To address this critical issue, we present a theoretical framework for unbiased alignment, introducing the Unbiased Reward Model (URM) loss and the Unbiased Direct Preference Optimization (UDPO) loss. By mathematically correcting the distortion induced by preference noise, our novel objectives enable unbiased model training directly from noisy datasets, without requiring clean ground-truth supervision. We provide rigorous theoretical analyses demonstrating that our methods are noise-tolerant, parameter downward compatible, and classification-calibrated. Comprehensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate that our approaches outperform state-of-the-art baselines. Code available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Illusion of Reasoning: Exposing Evasive Data Contamination in LLMs via Zero-CoT Truncation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21856)**  `arXiv:2605.21856`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Lan, Yuanpu Cao, Hanyu Wang, Lu Lin, Jinghui Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive reasoning abilities across a wide range of tasks, but data contamination undermines the objective evaluation of these capabilities. This problem is further exacerbated by malicious model publishers who use evasive, or indirect, contamination strategies, such as paraphrasing benchmark data to evade existing detection methods and artificially boost leaderboard performance. Current approaches struggle to reliably detect such stealthy contamination. In this work, we uncover a critical phenomenon: a model's generated reasoning steps actively mask its underlying memorization. Inspired by this, we propose the Zero-CoT Probe (ZCP), a novel black-box detection method that deliberately truncates the entire Chain-of-Thought (CoT) process to expose latent shortcut mappings. To further isolate memorization from the model's intrinsic problem-solving capabilities, ZCP compares the model's zero-CoT performance on the original benchmark against an isomorphically perturbed reference dataset. Furthermore, we introduce Contamination Confidence, a metric that quantifies both the likelihood and severity of contamination, moving beyond simple binary classifications. Extensive experiments on both previously identified contaminated models and specially fine-tuned contaminated models demonstrate that ZCP robustly detects both direct and evasive data contamination. The code for ZCP is accessible atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TwinRouterBench: Fast Static and Live Dynamic Evaluation for Realistic Agentic LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18859)**  `arXiv:2605.18859`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pei Yang, Wanyi Chen, Tongyun Yang, Pengbin Feng, Jiarong Xing, Wentao Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM routing matters most in long-horizon applications such as coding agents, deep research systems, and computer-use agents, where a single user request triggers many model calls. Routing each call to the cheapest sufficient model can cut costs without sacrificing quality, yet existing router benchmarks evaluate routers only on one-shot prompts. They never expose the router-visible prefix at an intermediate agent step, never test whether a cheaper replacement preserves downstream task success, and often rely on online LLM judges at evaluation time. We introduce TwinRouterBench, a step-level routing benchmark with two tracks. The static track provides 970 router-visible prefixes from 520 instances across SWE-bench, BFCL, mtRAG, QMSum, and PinchBench, each paired with an execution-verified target tier estimated under a released downgrade-and-cascade protocol; scoring is deterministic arithmetic over tier labels, trajectory membership, and token costs, with no online evaluator-side LLM judge. The dynamic track supplies a harness that runs routers on the full 500-case SWE-bench Verified suite; in this paper we report a 100-case held-out evaluation disjoint from the static SWE supervision split. At each LLM call the router selects a concrete model from a locked pool, and success is measured by official task resolution and realized API spend. The two tracks support fast offline iteration followed by end-to-end validation under live agent execution. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Same Signal, Opposite Meaning: Direction-Informed Adaptive Learning for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06908)**  `arXiv:2605.06908`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziming Li, Jiatan Huang, Xiaoguang Guo, Guiling Wang, Chuxu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adaptive test-time compute for LLM agents aims to invoke extra computation only when it improves performance. Existing methods typically use confidence-, uncertainty-, or difficulty-based gates, assuming a fixed direction from the gating signal through compute need to the value of computation. This makes gating a utility-calibration problem: gating signals should align with whether extra computation improves the final outcome over the base policy. We show that this alignment is unstable: the same signal predicts rollout benefit in one setting and rollout harm in another, with reversals across environments and backbones even when the task is fixed. Wrong-direction gates can therefore worsen performance by precisely selecting harmful states. This reversal reflects a deeper distinction between compute need and compute suitability: a high uncertainty signal may indicate decision-difficult states where rollouts help compare alternatives, or intervention-unsuitable states where the current context does not support useful rollout-based improvement. Under this two-source model, fixed-direction gates are unreliable across heterogeneous settings. To address this, we propose DIAL (Direction-Informed Adaptive Learning), a sparse gate trained from signal-agnostic counterfactual exploration to learn the utility direction of state features per (environment, backbone). Across six environments and three backbones, DIAL yields a stronger overall success-cost trade-off than fixed-direction baselines. Our code is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[KV Packet: Recomputation-Free Context-Independent KV Caching for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13226)**  `arXiv:2604.13226`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chuangtao Chen, Grace Li Zhang, Xunzhao Yin, Cheng Zhuo, Bing Li, Ulf Schlichtmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) rely heavily on Key-Value (KV) caching to minimize inference latency. However, standard KV caches are context-dependent: reusing a cached document in a new context requires recomputing KV states to account for shifts in attention distribution. Existing solutions such as CacheBlend, EPIC, and SAM-KV mitigate this issue by selectively recomputing a subset of tokens; however, they still incur non-negligible computational overhead (FLOPs) and increased Time-to-First-Token (TTFT) latency. In this paper, we propose KV Packet, a recomputation-free cache reuse framework that treats cached documents as immutable "packets" wrapped in light-weight trainable soft-token adapters, which are trained via self-supervised distillation to bridge context discontinuities. Experiments on Llama-3.1 and Qwen-3 demonstrate that the proposed KV Packet method achieves near-zero FLOPs and lower TTFT than recomputation-based baselines, while retaining F1 scores comparable to those of the full recomputation baseline. Code and experimental results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Long Delay to Arithmetic Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13082)**  `arXiv:2604.13082`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Laura Gomezjurado Gonzalez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models today still make mistakes on multi-digit arithmetic when they do not have tools, and part of the reason is how numbers are split into tokens. Arithmetic is also a setting where the correct answer to every input and the algorithm that produces it are already known, so training transformers on it lets us study what they learn against a ground truth. These models often fit their training set long before they generalize to held-out inputs, and this delay, known as grokking, decreases as the training set grows. But, how does the representation of numbers change the amount of data a transformer needs to generalize? We study one-step Collatz prediction, in which a transformer reads the digits of an integer in a chosen base and outputs the digits of the next term in its Collatz sequence. We find that the long delay appears only when the training set has few different integers, and that the numeral base determines how many integers are sufficient. In bases where each output digit depends only on a few neighbouring input digits, because the carry between positions depends only on the current digit, a transformer needs several times fewer training integers. We predicted this ordering before training, and input length does not explain it. The same carry structure predicts which bases are easy for a second Collatz map, for integer division, for a decoder-only transformer, and across three model sizes. Finally, a linear probe recovers the low-order residues of the input as accurately from a randomly initialized encoder as from a trained one. Thus, high probe accuracy on such features does not reliably show that a model has learned to compute them. More broadly, we show that the choice of numeral representation affects sample efficiency in ways we can predict from the structure of the task before training.
  </details>

- **[Automatic Configuration of LLM Post-Training Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18773)**  `arXiv:2603.18773`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Channe Chwa, Xinle Wu, Yao Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM post-training pipelines that combine supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning are difficult to configure under realistic compute budgets: the configuration space is high-dimensional and heterogeneous, stages are strongly coupled, and each end-to-end evaluation is expensive. We propose AutoPipe, a budget-aware two-phase framework for configuration selection in LLM post-training. Offline, AutoPipe learns a dataset-conditioned learning-to-rank surrogate from historical runs, capturing within-dataset preferences and providing transferable guidance toward promising regions of the configuration space. Online, for a new dataset, AutoPipe uses the offline guidance to steer Bayesian optimization and models dataset-specific deviations with a Gaussian-process residual surrogate. To reduce evaluation cost, each trial is early-stopped and scored by a learned predictor that maps early training signals to a low-cost proxy for final post-training performance. Experiments on biomedical reasoning tasks show that AutoPipe achieves performance comparable to full-fidelity HPO baselines at a fraction of their online cost, while outperforming offline-only approaches and early-stopped HPO variants.
  </details>

- **[EpiFlow: Bridging Safety and Performance in Offline Reinforcement Learning via Epigraph-Guided Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08054)**  `arXiv:2602.08054`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Manan Tayal, Mumuksh Tayal, Ravi Prakash_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline reinforcement learning (RL) offers a promising framework for deploying autonomous systems in safety-critical settings without the risks of online exploration. However, learning policies that simultaneously achieve high performance and strong safety guarantees from fixed datasets remains a fundamental challenge. Many existing safe offline RL approaches typically rely on soft constraint formulations, which may permit safety violations and are sensitive to distributional shift. In contrast, formal methods such as Hamilton--Jacobi (HJ) reachability and Control Barrier Functions (CBFs) provide rigorous safety guarantees, but often yield overly conservative solutions, often neglecting performance. In this work, we bridge this gap by formulating safe offline RL as a state-constrained optimal control problem, where safety is enforced through hard state constraints and performance is captured via a reward function. The resulting value function satisfies a Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman (HJB) equation, which we approximate using offline RL on fixed datasets by learning an epigraph feasibility value function and extracting policies via flow matching. This formulation enables principled integration of safety guarantees with data-driven policy optimization. Empirically, across safety-critical benchmarks including boat navigation, Safety-Gymnasium, and Bullet-Safety-Gym tasks, our approach achieves competitive returns while exhibiting near-zero constraint violations, demonstrating a favorable balance between safety and performance in the offline setting.
  </details>

- **[NoiseFormer -- Noise Diffused Symmetric Attention Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11619)**  `arXiv:2601.11619`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Phani Kumar, Nyshadham, Jyothendra Varma, Polisetty V R K_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer architecture has been very successful long runner in the field of Deep Learning (DL) and Large Language Models (LLM) because of its powerful attention-based learning and parallel-natured architecture. As the models grow gigantic in terms of memory footprint, difficulties in fitting the model on a device like a GPU or an AI accelerator give rise to the need for multiple computing devices thereby escalating the computing cost. This increased training/inference cost paved the way for optimizing the memory footprint of the models in terms of activation memory as well as model size reduction. In this paper, we start analyzing one of the Attention architectures aimed at model-size reduction called Symmetric Dot-Product Attention (referred to as Symmetric Attention) and propose a novel unified model architecture called Noise Diffused Symmetric Attention Transformer to enhance the model's performance. While maintaining the memory gains of Symmetric Attention, with minute overhead in terms of model parameters and compute in the form of noise diffused attention, the proposed model recovers the loss in performance due to Symmetric Attention compared to conventional attention as depicted through the inference-time sampling and GLUE scores on downstream tasks.
  </details>

- **[FediLoRA: Practical Federated Fine-Tuning of Foundation Models Under Missing-Modality Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06984)**  `arXiv:2509.06984`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lishan Yang, Wei Emma Zhang, Nam Kha Nguygen, Po Hu, Yanjun Shu, Weitong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning with LoRA fine-tuning offers an efficient and privacy-aware solution for institutions to collaboratively leverage their large datasets to train VLLMs. However, participating institutions often possess heterogeneous computational resources, resulting in imbalanced LoRA ranks, which pose a major challenge for effective collaboration. In addition, real-world applications in domains such as healthcare and transportation frequently suffer from missing modalities due to user mistakes or device failures, which significantly degrade global model performance in federated settings. To the best of our knowledge, no prior work has addressed these two challenges simultaneously in federated VLLMs. To tackle these issues, we propose FediLoRA, a lightweight federated LoRA aggregation framework that effectively mitigates the impact of missing modalities in heterogeneous environment. FediLoRA is explicitly motivated by the observation that simple averaging and structured editing can jointly benefit both global and personalized models. Our approach achieves strong performance across multiple general-domain and medical-domain benchmark datasets. Additional experiments on healthcare data further demonstrate that FediLoRA is well-suited for practical, real-world deployment scenarios. Our code is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EF-LLM: A Continual Learning-Based Framework for AI-Assisted Automation, Enhanced Sparse Prediction, and Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00852)**  `arXiv:2411.00852`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihang Qiu, Jayashri Ravishankar, Anam Malik, Zhongju Wang, Renyou Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate prediction helps achieve supply-demand balance in energy systems,thereby supporting operational decision-making andthis http URLmodels,which lack AI-assisted automation,often rely heavily on expert knowledge,incur high labor costs,and perform poorly under sparse-datathis http URLaddress these challenges,this work proposes an Energy Forecasting Large Language Model (EF-LLM),which integrates domain knowledge and temporal data for time-series forecasting while supporting both pre-forecast operations and post-forecastthis http URL-LLM's human-artificial intelligence (AI) interaction capabilities further lower the entry barrier for forecasting tasks and reduce the need for additional expertthis http URLsupport these functions, a continual learning framework with updatable Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and a multi-channel architecture is developed to align heterogeneous multimodal data, enabling EF-LLM to continuously acquire and adapt multimodalthis http URLaddition,EF-LLM improves prediction accuracy under sparse-data conditions by leveraging its ability to process and fuse multimodal information.A Fusion Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (F-PEFT) method is further introduced to effectively exploit both time-series data and textualthis http URL-LLM is also the first energy-specific LLM to detect hallucinations and quantify their occurrence rate, achieved via multi-task learning and semantic similaritythis http URLwork has achieved success in energy prediction scenarios for load, PV, and wind power forecast.
  </details>

- **[Selective Tie Learning: Mitigating Spurious Correlations in Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35787)**  `arXiv:2610.35787`  `cs.LG`  
  _Christian Moya, Purav Matlia, Elliott Thornley, Guang Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference optimization can learn spurious correlations, and augmenting the preference data with ties (equally preferred responses) can reduce spurious reliance. However, ties differ in their corrective effect, making their selection for augmentation important. For log-linear policies, we derive each tie's influence on spurious reliance and show that taking the largest predicted reductions is first-order optimal among subsets of equal size. Computing these influences requires knowledge of the spurious features and the curvature of the training objective, which is often unavailable or expensive to estimate. To this end, we explore using the magnitude of a tie's implicit reward margin and characterize the conditions under which it is a useful proxy for predicted influence. To test the theory and its qualitative transfer to neural models, we estimate influence with a counterfactual proxy for spurious reliance and use a generalized Gauss--Newton approximation to curvature for language models. Across log-linear policies, neural models, and large language models, our selection rules consistently achieve comparable reductions in spurious reliance with fewer ties than uniform selection.
  </details>

- **[A Hybrid Framework for Explainable Aviation Risk: Causal Chain Extraction from Safety Reports with Physics-Based Failure Quantification and Interventional Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35658)**  `arXiv:2610.35658`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yutian Pang, Yifang Tian, Jiacheng Xie, Xinyu Zhao, Jueming Hu, John-Paul Clarke, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aviation authorities hold decades of accident reports whose narratives record how investigators reconstructed each accident. Computational work on these archives has mostly sorted reports into fixed taxonomies. In this work, we propose a hybrid framework in which a text-based corpus identifies the leading factors of a failure mode. Physics-based models then quantify how likely that failure is at a stated operating point. Ordered causal chains extracted from 56,202 American accident reports with a local large language model aggregate into a causation knowledge graph. On an evaluation sample of 2,000 reports, the chains recover 90.8% of investigator-flagged causes. Each physical factor whose drivers the record carries receives an occurrence model calibrated to the accident population. A leaky noisy-OR fitted to the corpus combines the factors into a failure mode probability that supports intervention. For engine power loss, the carburetor icing model separates 1,205 icing accidents from other accidents using temperature and dewpoint alone, reaching 0.61 in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve against a density ratio ceiling of 0.64. Routine weather hours give no valid control, because flying concentrates in fair weather. On later years the physics adds 0.03 to that area for occurrence and nothing for severity, so its value lies in interpretability. On four named accidents, abduction gives a probability of necessity of 0.29 to 0.50 for the heat omission, under monotonicity. The Fast Causal Inference algorithm does not license the largest backdoor adjustment on the learned networks.
  </details>

- **[When Fine-Grained Credit Assignment Helps and Hurts: Multimodal Agentic Reinforcement Learning through Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35497)**  `arXiv:2610.35497`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lei Zhao, Zongheng Cao, Xinyu Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promise for training multimodal agents to reason and use tools. However, it remains unclear when fine-grained credit assignment improves learning and when it fails to improve task performance. Video editing provides a useful testbed for this question, combining multimodal perception, long-horizon planning, and tool use with delayed outcome feedback. We construct four movie-trailer editing task types---Content Selection, Narrative Sequencing, Rough Cut, and Fine Cut---that reflect real-world editing workflows and span different levels of task complexity and interaction horizon. Decaying process rewards provides the largest benefit on Fine Cut, with a 45.4% relative gain over outcome-only GRPO on tasks averaging more than 60 turns under outcome-only GRPO. On the three shorter-horizon task types averaging 30--40 turns under the same baseline, outcome-only GRPO remains competitive, with decaying process rewards yielding at most a 10.4% relative gain. Process rewards from an LLM judge initially improve tool use and encourage visual inspection. As training progresses, however, GRPO+PRM with a fixed process-reward weight increasingly exhibits excessive reasoning and redundant tool calls: process rewards continue to rise while task completion rates and outcome scores decline. Decaying the process-reward weight to zero mitigates this reward-hacking behavior while preserving the early gains. Despite dense token-level supervision, on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) yields little improvement or substantial degradation and is highly sensitive to the choice of privileged information. Our analysis reveals differences in teacher--student disagreement and gradient magnitudes, and suggests possible dilution of informative signals over long trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Amplify, Locate, Estimate (ALE) on Rare-Failure Rates of Fine-Tuned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35472)**  `arXiv:2610.35472`  `cs.LG`  
  _Patrick Reichherzer, Jonathan Evertz, Thorsten Holz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety fine-tuning can make undesirable model behaviors sufficiently rare that measuring their frequency by direct sampling becomes prohibitively expensive. We introduce Amplify, Locate, Estimate (ALE), a method for estimating such rare failure probabilities efficiently. ALE uses the change in token log-probabilities induced by the safety fine-tuning to define an amplification direction, along which we increase the failure rate for cheap measurement. We develop a score-function estimator that uses the selected amplified batch to recover the local slope of the log failure rate from the token probabilities of the sampled failures, enabling extrapolation back to rare target rates. We also use the amplified batch to obtain an importance-sampling estimate. Across 41 evaluation targets, ALE's estimate-to-reference ratios have a median close to one, with 37 ALE estimates within a factor of three of reference values obtained from independent direct measurements. Ablation tests show that with a budget of up to $\sim$1000 labeled rollouts, in addition to a cheap unlabeled scan, ALE estimates failure probabilities for Qwen3-8B fine-tunes down to about $10^{-5}$, where direct measurement can require hundreds of thousands of rollouts, as the required sampling budget scales inversely with failure probability at fixed relative precision.
  </details>

- **[Intent-Preserving Adversarial User Simulation for Multi-Turn Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35426)**  `arXiv:2610.35426`  `cs.LG`  
  _Atri Vivek Sharma, Brian Formento, Alessio Lomuscio_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn agents are commonly evaluated with LLM-based simulated users, which can be overly cooperative and can therefore overestimate an agent's performance. We here introduce StressSim, an intent-preserving adversarial user simulator framework for stress-testing agents with challenging yet valid user interactions. StressSim optimises the simulator's high-level adversarial plan for each task instance. Candidate planner prompts are evolved with a genetic prompt-optimisation loop that uses rollout traces, granular task-completion signals, and validity feedback to favour conversations that reduce task success without changing the user's underlying goal. We evaluate the approach on the MultiWOZ dataset and the retail and airline domains from tau-bench with three target LLM agents, comparing against standard simulators and hand-designed non-collaborative user behaviours. We demonstrate the effectiveness of StressSim in exposing robustness gaps across frontier LLMs, with tau-bench pass^4 scores decreasing by up to 69% on gemini-3.8-flash and 62% on gpt-5.6-sol. StressSim maintains goal preservation performance (which decreases by at most 10%), using an optimisation budget of 20 rollouts per task. Sensitivity analysis suggests that increasing the budget further decreases task success. Overall our results demonstrate that StressSim exposes robustness gaps missed by standard and hand-designed user simulators while maintaining conversation realism. This is achieved through lightweight prompt optimisation alone, without model fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[OneEEG: A Unified Signal-Visual Multimodal Language Model with Task-Aware Mixture of Projectors for Multi-Task EEG Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35355)**  `arXiv:2610.35355`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiacheng Zhang, Xueyi Zhang, Xin Lu, Siqi Cai, Haizhou Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) is widely used in clinical assessment, brain-computer interfaces, and cognitive-state analysis, yet EEG modeling remains fragmented across task paradigms. Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provide a promising interface for flexible multimodal reasoning, but their ability to understand EEG inputs remains limited. In this work, we propose OneEEG, a unified EEG-MLLM framework for multi-task EEG understanding. By training on our curated OneEEG-Instruct, a multi-task EEG instruction-tuning dataset, OneEEG jointly models numerical EEG representations and visual EEG representations, and introduces a task-aware Mixture of Projectors (MoP) to align heterogeneous EEG features across different task families with the LLM embedding space. We further establish OneEEG-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating current MLLMs and our method on multi-task EEG understanding. Experiments show that existing MLLMs have limited ability to interpret EEG inputs, while OneEEG achieves stronger performance by integrating numerical and visual EEG representations with task-aware EEG-to-LLM projection. Extensive ablations and analyses further reveal the effects of visual EEG renderings, MoP variants, router behavior, and feature dispersion across EEG tasks.
  </details>

- **[TUON: A Tensor-Aware Optimizer Utilizing Tensor-SVD and Polarization for Large Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35130)**  `arXiv:2610.35130`  `cs.LG`  
  _Anda Tang, Xuanlin Yang, Junjie Wang, Zhouchen Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large scale language model~(LLM) pretraining is increasingly bottlenecked by optimizer efficiency, driving optimizer design from vector-aware methods such as AdamW to matrix-aware methods. Matrix-aware optimizers such as Muon exploit matrix structure but treat layers independently, neglecting cross-layer correlations. Tensor-aware optimizers are therefore needed, yet existing ones rely on a matricization-based tensor algebra and remain restricted to shallow stacking and specific modules. In this paper, we propose \textbf{TUON}, a novel optimizer built on a transform-based tensor algebra derived from the Tensor-SVD (T-SVD). Unlike methods that polarize a globally unfolded matrix, TUON mixes layers via a unitary transform $\boldsymbol{Q}$ and then polarizes each transformed slice, and inverts the transform to recover the update. This mix-then-separate strategy captures cross-layer correlations while preserving per-layer diversity, avoiding the structural dimension imbalance and cross-layer misalignment of unfolding. Thus it successfully scales to more layers and diverse modules beyond QKV. Theoretically, we prove that TUON performs steepest descent under a $\boldsymbol{Q}$-transformed tensor spectral norm, and identify its update as the tensor polar factor of the T-SVD. We further establish transform non-redundancy, norm and smoothness comparisons with Muon and TEON, and a convergence guarantee. Empirically, TUON improves consistently over Muon and TEON on GPT-124M/350M and Qwen-0.6B/4B, with ablations confirming the transformation mechanism, cross-layer structure beyond two-layer stacking, adaptability beyond QKV, and robustness to the polarization method.
  </details>

- **[BellowsKV: One Latent for Many Layers, Expanded on Demand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35023)**  `arXiv:2610.35023`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiamu Zhang, Hanjie Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) now serve as the backbone of agents that reason over many turns, call tools, and pursue long-horizon tasks. To avoid recomputation, a model stores the attention keys and values of all past tokens in a key-value (KV) cache. Each layer maintains its own cache, so KV-cache memory can become the main bottleneck for GPU serving. Most compression methods operate within a single layer, by quantizing the cache entries, reducing the channel dimension, or evicting less important tokens. All three lose substantial accuracy at high compression ratios. Cross-layer methods share one compressed cache across adjacent layers to go beyond this limit. However, these methods also degrade sharply at high compression ratios. We find that the KV-cache representations of adjacent layers are quite different, whereas the input hidden states of adjacent layers are highly similar. We also find that, under the same memory budget, reducing the dimension of this shared latent loses far more accuracy than quantizing it. Based on these findings, we propose BellowsKV, a training-free method that maps the hidden states of n adjacent layers to one shared latent per token. At inference time, each layer reads its own keys and values from this latent through a layer-specific projection. We keep the latent at full dimension and compress it in two other ways: BellowsKV-Quant stores the latent at low precision, which is nearly lossless because the latent has no dominant outlier channels; BellowsKV-Evict makes one shared eviction decision per block, because the layers rank token importance very similarly. Across Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B, BellowsKV-Quant retains about 99% of full-cache accuracy at 3.6x compression and 97-98% at 13-14x, where the strongest cross-layer method retains at most 84% at 16x. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[End-to-End MXFP8 Reinforcement Learning on Blackwell: What Matching Buys, When It Pays Off, and How It Fails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34915)**  `arXiv:2610.34915`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wengee Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-precision arithmetic can make reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models cheaper, but RL requires the sampler and the learner to agree. Several frameworks now run FP8 on both sides. We implement end-to-end MXFP8 RL in verl (Megatron/Transformer Engine training; vLLM and SGLang rollout; dense and MoE models) and measure on B200 GPUs, with Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-30B-A3B, what this design buys, when it is faster, and how it fails. (1) Matching. For an MXFP8 learner, a sampler on the learner's grid is more consistent than a higher-precision BF16 sampler: in one engine at fixed weights, train-rollout KL is 11% lower with half of the sampler's linear layers on the grid and 16% lower with all of them, and the synchronized weights we checked equal the learner's FP8 forward weights byte for byte. On the MoE model, an FP8 pipeline whose sampler uses another grid has twice the shared-grid pipeline's KL from the first step. Without a quantized KV cache, BF16 end to end remains 2.5-10x more consistent. (2) Speed. MXFP8 training adds a fixed cost per micro-batch and saves a per-token cost, so in our dense setting it pays off only above about 4.5k tokens per micro-batch; a micro-batch sweep on identical data confirms the per-micro-batch cost, and a trace shows the GPU waiting for the host for most of the added time. End to end, MXFP8 cuts MoE step time by 10.7% and generation time by 24%, and slows a short-sequence dense workload by 5%. (3) Correctness. We state seven conditions every weight synchronization must preserve and checks that test them against the live engine. Each of the seven failures we met on hardware broke one condition, five silently; the checks caught two at their first opportunity, each missed by the other checks, and CPU fault-injection tests reproduce five. All runs are single-seed and at most 60 steps long; we make no claim about final model quality.
  </details>

- **[When Respondents Are Scarce: LLM-Derived Semantic Priors for Bayesian Confirmatory Factor Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34843)**  `arXiv:2610.34843`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shaohuai Feng, Mohd Wira Mohd Shafiei, Mohd Sukri Shafie, Siyang Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Across social and behavioral research, measuring latent constructs can be challenging when target populations are small or difficult to reach. Recruiting additional respondents may be costly or infeasible, leaving researchers with limited data for estimating questionnaire factor structures. Yet item wording contains structural expectations before data collection, and whether these can improve small-sample estimation without replacing empirical validation remains unclear. We introduce the Semantic-Empirical Updating Framework (SEUF), which converts large language model paraphrases and embeddings, aligned with hypothesized item-factor assignments, into prespecified priors for Bayesian confirmatory factor analysis. We evaluated SEUF using a 38-item survey of 617 industrial-park stakeholders and two public surveys, FEVS and RIASEC. Semantic structure persisted after wording controls across all three instruments. With 50 respondents, SEUF achieved loading precision comparable to conventional CFA with 70-82 respondents, equivalent to 29-39% fewer respondents at the specified precision benchmark. Results showed lower loading errors and improved credible-interval coverage relative to conventional CFA in both public surveys. Gains were greatest when respondent data were limited, highlighting the value of semantic priors under information scarcity. By harnessing structural information already embedded in questionnaires, SEUF advances a more resource-efficient approach to measurement and opens new directions for study design and empirical inference in hard-to-reach populations.
  </details>

- **[AT-AT: Automatic Tool Optimization from Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34679)**  `arXiv:2610.34679`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maya Ravichandran, Pragya Tripathi, Dharamendra Kumar, Adith Swaminathan, Nathan Kallus_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efforts to improve LLM-based agents typically focus on modifying the agent itself, yet success also depends on the environment in which an agent acts. Tools mediate this interaction: a tool's description shapes how an agent calls it, and its implementation determines the results and errors the agent receives afterwards. Many tools, such as APIs and MCP servers, are shared by many agents and maintained by providers who cannot change those agents. We introduce AT-AT (Automatic Tool Optimization from Agent Traces), an agentic tool optimizer that improves a shared toolset offline from historical agent executions while holding the agents fixed. A lightweight coding agent reads agent trajectories and their evaluation results, and selectively edits tool descriptions and, when permitted, implementations. On $\tau^2$-bench-verified, AT-AT achieves the highest task completion rates in the Airline and Telecom domains at lower cost than strong prompt- and tool-optimizer baselines, and its Telecom gain is statistically significant under paired permutation tests. Optimizing the tools within a coding agent harness also improves held-out performance on TerminalBench-2 and TBLite. When applied to three production MCP servers at a large enterprise, AT-AT lowered cost or tool error rates on every server and increased held-out task passes on one. By turning accumulated execution traces into updates to shared tool environments, AT-AT provides a path toward continual improvement of agent systems through environment-side learning.
  </details>

- **[From Human Intent to In-Orbit Action: A Self-Learning Large Language Model Agent for 6G Satellite Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34387)**  `arXiv:2610.34387`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengqian Xu, Yasheng Zhang, Chenhua Sun, Shan Lu, Chengcheng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous control of sixth-generation (6G) low Earth orbit satellite networks is hindered by the gap between high-level human intent and low-level control logic: translating a request such as "uninterrupted Arctic video" into a valid reinforcement learning reward still takes days of manual trial and error, and existing large language model translators cannot learn from the failures of the policies they generate. This article presents ALMOND (Agentic Large-Model-Orchestrated autonomous Network Design), a cognitive framework that closes this intent-to-action loop under on-orbit compute constraints. The contribution in artificial intelligence is a bidirectional semantic engine that translates natural-language demands into multi-objective reward functions and, conversely, mines implicit operational constraints from failed reinforcement-learning trajectories through counterfactually validated forensics, together with a large language model-guided graph neuroevolution that seeds and critiques policy architectures. The contribution in engineering is a ground-centric, compute-frugal architecture for autonomous 6G non-terrestrial network operation, in which satellites execute only distilled policies below 50,000 parameters with sub-15 ms inference and no on-board large language model. On a 350-satellite digital twin driven by real ephemeris data, ALMOND raises polar handover success from 55% to over 95%, converges about three times faster than baseline reinforcement learning, and reduces reward engineering from 48 hours to 4.5 hours, demonstrating a practical path toward trustworthy in-orbit autonomy.
  </details>

- **[DIGEST: Discovery-Guided LLM Tuning for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33880)**  `arXiv:2610.33880`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaifeng Jin, Ruizhong Qiu, Zihao Li, Xiao Lin, Dongqi Fu, Yinglong Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) seeks to discover mathematical expressions directly from data, without requiring task-specific scientific descriptions. Despite tremendous progress in both conventional search-based methods and recent LLM-based methods, two critical limitations largely remain. First (Limited Generation Flexibility), conventional search-based methods rely on either fixed search rules or specialized learned generators, severely limiting adaptive candidate generation across diverse expression structures. Second (Underutilized Search Discoveries), most LLM-based methods cannot reliably recover ground-truth expressions because they fall short in effectively leveraging the LLM itself to thoroughly digest past expressions or adaptively guide future search. To address these limitations, we propose DIGEST. To render greater flexibility in generation, DIGEST repurposes a pretrained decoder block from a lightweight LLM into a symbolic policy for Genetic Programming (GP) population seeding, thereby leveraging the broader modeling capacity of the LLM. To more thoroughly utilize search discoveries, high-quality expressions discovered by GP are fed back to update the LLM-derived policy online, thereby enabling the LLM to progressively internalize past search discoveries and translate them into improved subsequent population generation. Experiments show that on two ground-truth benchmarks, DIGEST outperforms the strongest competitor by up to 2.59% in recovery. Among LLM-based baselines, it improves recovery by at least 54.91% while using at most 17.4% of the strongest competitor's mean search time. On 122 BlackBox datasets, DIGEST achieves the highest accuracy while maintaining a moderate model complexity.
  </details>

- **[FedSEAM: Federated Continual Learning of LLMs via Subspace Expert Agreement Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33496)**  `arXiv:2610.33496`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fatema Siddika, Abhijit Chunduru, Zilinghan Li, Ali Jannesari, Ravi Madduri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated continual learning trains a model on a sequence of tasks across several clients without sharing their data while retaining knowledge of earlier tasks. Sparse mixture-of-experts models support this setting by adapting selected parameters on a frozen backbone. However, clients learn different expert content as tasks evolve, making it difficult to share knowledge across clients without disrupting task-specific and client-specific specialization. Matching tile coordinates alone does not establish whether their learned content is compatible. We present FedSEAM, a federated continual learning framework that organizes task knowledge into sparse experts and selectively shares their content across tasks and clients. On each client, task-aware tile selection allocates capacity to a new task, while a subspace-based compatibility test determines which existing expert tiles to reuse and where to introduce fresh capacity. On the server, Subspace Expert Agreement Merging (SEAM) compares client updates as subspaces and moves each client towards consensus only along agreed-upon directions, leaving the rest of its update unchanged. We identify two remaining sources of forgetting, routing drift and merge erasure, and address them with three protections, one of which exactly preserves tile components in a selected prior-overlap subspace at every merge. We evaluate FedSEAM on TRACE using Qwen3-4B and Llama-3.2-3B, with component-wise ablations and studies of data size to assess its balance between learning new tasks and retaining prior knowledge.
  </details>

- **[All-in-One: A Composable Attack on LLM Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33298)**  `arXiv:2610.33298`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaohua Zhan, Kazuki Egashira, Robin Staab, Mark Vero, Martin Vechev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization, pruning, and model merging are popular transformations applied to deployment-ready LLMs to improve efficiency and downstream performance. Recent work has shown that these transformations can introduce a security threat: an adversary can train a model that passes safety checks in its pre-transformed state, only turning malicious once it is transformed for deployment. However, prior attacks target one specific transformation (e.g., quantization) and generally rely on transformation-specific techniques, making them difficult to combine in practice. In this work, we introduce a simple, composable attack framework that targets multiple transformations simultaneously and triggers when any one of them is applied by a user. Our key insight is that the effect of each transformation can be approximated as a perturbation to the model weights, allowing us to jointly train the model with a benign loss on the original model and a malicious loss on each simulated transformed model. These perturbations are simple enough to simulate at every training iteration across diverse transformations, making them practically combinable within a single training objective, enabling a unified attack. Through extensive experiments across attack scenarios, model families, and a wide range of deployment-time model transformations, we show that our attack achieves high success rates. This demonstrates that model transformations may be utilized to trigger significantly different behavior from the original model and reinforces the associated security concerns by demonstrating that a single attack can jointly target diverse transformations.
  </details>

- **[Neyman Orthogonal Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33217)**  `arXiv:2610.33217`  `cs.LG`  
  _David Arbour, Erfan Jahanparast, Serina Chang, Avi Feller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering is a popular approach for shifting LLM output toward a desired behavior, and increasingly for generating realistic synthetic data. The standard recipe estimates a steering direction by contrasting LLM activations on prompts with and without a target concept, such as truthfulness. In this paper, we separate the estimand (the steering direction these methods target) from the estimator used to recover it, two notions the literature often conflates. We frame nearly all existing methods as plug-in estimators of the average gradient of an outcome regression, with the binary contrastive label fixed as the outcome. Using this framing, we can immediately incorporate flexible modeling approaches and extend existing methods to allow for continuous outcomes such as log-probabilities or oracle ratings. Flexible ML models that fit the outcome well, however, might not estimate the gradient well, which motivates estimators that target the gradient directly. Drawing on the debiased machine learning literature, we develop a complementary estimator built on the Stein score, the Riesz representer of the gradient functional. Combining the two routes gives an orthogonalized estimator with classical double-robustness properties. We illustrate the framework on activation steering applied to a number of benchmark datasets.
  </details>

- **[PerSPL: Personalized Semi-supervised Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29219)**  `arXiv:2610.29219`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoan Xu, Tingzhou Wei, Juncheng Dong, Korel Gundem, Ethan X. Fang, Zhengling Qi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized reward models require user-specific preference comparisons, but collecting such feedback is costly and provides only limited supervision for each user. At the same time, large pools of responses can be generated without user identities or preference labels, yet existing personalized reward models leave this data unused. Directly learning from these responses is difficult because pairwise objectives constrain relative scores within a prompt, whereas pseudo-labeling individual responses requires scores that are comparable across prompts for the same user. We propose \textbf{PerSPL} (Personalized Semi-supervised Preference Learning), a backbone-agnostic framework that uses pair-mean score centering to stabilize this cross-prompt score coordinate and iteratively turns unlabeled responses into user-specific training signals. Against seven representative baselines across four benchmarks---PersonalLLM, HelpSteer2, Reddit TLDR, and Chatbot Arena---PerSPL achieves the highest reported mean accuracy among the evaluated methods for both seen and unseen users. The two latter benchmarks provide real-user preference comparisons in summarization and open-domain chatbot interaction, respectively. These results indicate that unlabeled responses can provide additional supervision for personalized reward modeling when per-user preference labels are scarce.
  </details>

- **[Where Do LLMs Retrieve Network Information? Identifying the Responsible Heads and Neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08200)**  `arXiv:2610.08200`  `cs.LG`  
  _Md Abdul Kadir, Md Mohasin Hossain, Omair Shahzad Bhatti, Abdulrahman Mohamed Selim, Daniel Sonntag_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) used for Web information processing rely on retrieving relationships between network entities, such as domain names and IP addresses, but the internal components that causally enable this retrieval remain unclear, hindering targeted safeguards against unsafe actions. Therefore, we investigate whether their retrieval capabilities can be localized to specific attention heads or individual neurons within these LLMs. Across five LLMs from three architecture families, we identify heads through causal ablation screening, test their selectivity against matched negative and context-free controls, rank neurons within these heads using causal ablation and correlational measures, and leverage features from both causally validated heads and individual neurons to build network information retrieval detectors, which we evaluate without retraining on an independent reverse-DNS dataset. In every model, a small set of causally validated heads (1--9 out of 128--1152 candidates) builds a detector with 99.5--100\% held-out accuracy. At the neuron level, the results are model-specific, e.g., in one model, causal and correlational rankings identify the same neuron, matching full-head performance (AUC = 1.000), while in another, the best causally ranked neuron achieves only AUC = 0.665 despite the full head reaching 1.000. On independent CAIDA data, head detectors maintain AUC $\geq 0.971$, while single-neuron detectors fall below chance in three of five models. Overall, these findings highlight the importance of choosing an appropriate level of granularity when causally localizing how LLMs retrieve structured network information from context.
  </details>

- **[Exact Degeneracy Under Balanced k-Shot Sampling:Consequences for Small-Sample Discriminant Analysis on LLM Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09860)**  `arXiv:2609.09860`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lingxiao Qu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Balanced k-shot sampling, drawing exactly k labeled examples per class, is the standard protocol in few-shot evaluation. We show that it induces an exact degeneracy in Kernelized Linear Principal Component Discriminant Analysis (KLPCDA), a family of seven supervised dimensionality reduction methods. The within-class operator becomes exactly a scaled orthogonal projector, so two variants have all signal eigenvalues equal and a third has a zero operator, and the truncated pseudo-inverse used to implement them removes exactly the subspace that contains all between-class information. We verify this to float64 precision on sentence embeddings and on residual-stream activations of a decoder-only model, and find that the accuracies of the truncated variants change with the number of BLAS threads, by up to 12 percentage points (57 for the variant with a zero operator).Replacing the truncation with a ridge selected within the support set, we evaluate few-shot text classification on frozen embeddings (d up to 4096) over four datasets, three embedding models, and three trained baselines. The regularized formulation outperforms the best original variant in 45 of 48 settings, but in general not a tuned linear probe or nearest centroid on bidirectional encoders. Its gains are largest on E5-Mistral, the only decoder-based embedding model, and come from regularized within-class whitening rather than from KLPCDA: shrinkage LDA outperforms both baselines in all 16 E5-Mistral settings, and pre-specified tests show that the effect depends on the embedding model rather than on its dimension. E5-Mistral's remaining gap is largely an estimation-efficiency effect: increasing k to 30-50 reduces the probe's gap to a bidirectional encoder by more than 80%. We give five guidelines limited to the conditions tested and release the code and per-seed results.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Prioritized Sweeping in Nonstationary Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06186)**  `arXiv:2609.06186`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hung Pham, Tuan Dam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prioritized Sweeping (PS) accelerates model-based reinforcement learning by selecting backups according to Bellman residual magnitude. In nonstationary reward settings, however, the canonical priority score is shortsighted: after a localized reward shift, residuals propagate only through realized backups, so bottlenecked or topologically distant state estimates may remain static under a limited replanning budget. We introduce the Graph Topology Augmentation framework, which employ the graph's resolvent and its diffusion semantic, to augment the inquired signal. Our application, Graph Topology Augmentation for Prioritized Sweeping (GTA-PS), or which the alias Spectral Prioritized Sweeping (SPS) might be more universal, provides a drop-in ordering score for the setting of fixed dynamics and changing state rewards. GTA-PS uses a smootherized policy, inducing a transition chain, with its in- and out-Laplacian. The standard priority key is augmented with a mixing of regularized Laplacian inverses diffusing the residual magnitude. Furthermore, the topology contribution is annealed by a scheduler based on the Second Largest Eigenvalue Modulus (SLEM), allowing its scale to adapt to the chain's mixing regime. We prove that the forward potential coincides with geometric discounted residual propagation and show that GTA-PS gives active priority instantly to all states. Tabular experiments on FourRooms and GARNET domains demonstrate improved replanning efficiency over standard PS under both exact DP and Dyna-style host planners.
  </details>

- **[CORAL: Learning Amyloid Fibril Ligand Docking with Cooperative Binding Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17412)**  `arXiv:2607.17412`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yasheng Sun, Bohan Li, Youqi Tao, Jürgen Schmidhuber_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A hallmark of neurodegenerative diseases such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's is the aberrant aggregation of proteins into amyloid fibrils, and small molecules that selectively bind to these fibrils hold promise as diagnostics, imaging probes, and therapeutics. Predicting how such ligands bind to fibril targets, however, presents two fundamental challenges. First, resolved co-crystal structures of amyloid-ligand complexes are exceptionally scarce; even with recent advances in cryo-EM only a handful have been structurally characterized, making supervised training of docking models impractical for this target class. Second, amyloid fibrils present a binding mode fundamentally different from globular proteins: ligands intercalate into longitudinal cross-$\beta$ grooves and stack cooperatively along the fibril axis, a geometry that existing docking models are not designed to capture. To address these challenges, we present CORAL (COopeRative Amyloid Ligand docking), a reinforcement learning framework that trains a generative docking model to produce ligand pose distributions tailored to the cross-$\beta$ groove geometry. Our reward explicitly incorporates cooperative ligand-ligand stacking energy alongside protein-ligand docking affinity, directly capturing the distinctive binding geometry of amyloid fibrils. We further introduce a curated evaluation set of amyloid-ligand complexes constructed from model-generated poses validated by domain experts. Experiments on both experimentally resolved structures and this evaluation set demonstrate improved pose quality and binding affinity correlation over existing docking baselines. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Necessary but Not Sufficient: Temperature Control and Reproducibility in LLM-as-Judge Safety Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26185)**  `arXiv:2606.26185`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hiroki Tamba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-judge ("grader") components are now standard in evaluation harnesses, including safety evaluations where a pass/fail verdict may gate downstream deployment decisions. A widespread assumption is that setting the grader's sampling temperature to 0 makes grading deterministic. We test this assumption against a real safety-evaluation codebase (Japan AISI's open-source aisev) and show it fails on two levels. First, the harness invokes its grader without setting temperature or seed; the underlying provider silently applies its default of 1.0, so items near the decision boundary flip pass/fail across identical runs (per-item disagreement up to ~50% over 20 runs). Second, pinning temperature=0 reduces but does not eliminate flips: across 690 API calls spanning two providers, three model tiers, and five sampling configurations, 1-2 of 7 borderline items remain non-reproducible even under forced greedy decoding (top_k=1). Claude Opus 4.7/4.8 has since deprecated temperature entirely, rendering the primary mitigation inapplicable to newer model generations. These findings expose a structural gap: evaluation harnesses that report single-run verdicts without variance or grader-disagreement metrics can present noise as a safety property. We release a reproduction harness (690 calls, 7 conditions) and recommend that harnesses treat grader disagreement as a first-class health metric alongside the scores themselves.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning for LLM-based Event Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15917)**  `arXiv:2606.15917`  `cs.LG`  
  _Amit Arnold Levy_
