We benchmark three representative detectors -- TruFor, DocTamper, and a zero-shot GPT-4o judge -- and find that all existing methods degrade substantially: TruFor achieves AUC=0.751 (zero-shot, out-of-distribution) vs. AUC=0.96 on NIST16; DocTamper achieves AUC=0.563 vs. AUC=0.98 in-distribution, with pixel-level IoU=0.020; GPT-4o achieves only 0.509 -- essentially at chance -- confirming that AI-forged values are indistinguishable to automated detectors and VLMs. These results demonstrate that AIForge-Doc represents a qualitatively new and unsolved challenge for document forensics.
  </details>

- **[Towards Explainable Industrial Anomaly Detection via Knowledge-Guided Latent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09850)**  `arXiv:2602.09850`  `cs.CV`  
  _Peng Chen, Wenqiang Wang, Yunkang Cao, Chengliang Liu, Wei Wang, Mingbo Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial anomaly detection demands precise reasoning over fine-grained defect patterns. However, existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs), pretrained on general-domain data, often struggle to capture category-specific anomalies, thereby limiting both detection accuracy and interpretability. To address these limitations, we propose Reason-IAD, a knowledge-guided dynamic latent reasoning framework for explainable industrial anomaly detection. Reason-IAD comprises two core components. First, a retrieval-augmented knowledge module incorporates category-specific textual descriptions into the model input, enabling context-aware reasoning over domain-specific defects. Second, an entropy-driven latent reasoning mechanism conducts iterative exploration within a compact latent space using optimizable latent think tokens, guided by an entropy-based reward that encourages confident and stable predictions. Furthermore, a dynamic visual injection strategy selectively incorporates the most informative image patches into the latent sequence, directing the reasoning process toward regions critical for anomaly detection. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that Reason-IAD consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple tasks. The code will be publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Out of the box age estimation through facial imagery: A Comprehensive Benchmark of Vision-Language Models vs. out-of-the-box Traditional Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07815)**  `arXiv:2602.07815`  `cs.CV`  
  _Simiao Ren, Xingyu Shen, Ankit Raj, Albert Dai, Caroline, Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Facial age estimation plays a critical role in content moderation, age verification, and deepfake detection. However, no prior benchmark has systematically compared modern vision-language models (VLMs) with specialized age estimation architectures. We present the first large-scale cross-paradigm benchmark, evaluating 34 models - 22 specialized architectures with publicly available pretrained weights and 12 general-purpose VLMs - across eight standard datasets (UTKFace, IMDB-WIKI, MORPH, AFAD, CACD, FG-NET, APPA-REAL, and AgeDB), totaling 1,100 test images per model. Our key finding is striking: zero-shot VLMs significantly outperform most specialized models, achieving an average mean absolute error (MAE) of 5.65 years compared to 9.88 years for non-LLM models. The best-performing VLM (Gemini 3 Flash Preview, MAE 4.32) surpasses the strongest non-LLM model (MiVOLO, MAE 5.10) by 15%. MiVOLO - unique in combining face and body features using Vision Transformers - is the only specialized model that remains competitive with VLMs. We further analyze age verification at the 18-year threshold and find that most non-LLM models exhibit false adult rates between 39% and 100% for minors, whereas VLMs reduce this to 16%-29%. Additionally, coarse age binning (8-9 classes) consistently increases MAE beyond 13 years. Stratified analysis across 14 age groups reveals that all models struggle most at extreme ages (under 5 and over 65). Overall, these findings challenge the assumption that task-specific architectures are necessary for high-performance age estimation and suggest that future work should focus on distilling VLM capabilities into efficient specialized models.
  </details>

- **[PixFoundation 2.0: Do Video Multi-Modal LLMs Use Motion in Visual Grounding?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02807)**  `arXiv:2509.02807`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mennatullah Siam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have shown impressive generalization across tasks using images and text modalities. While their extension to video has enabled tasks such as video question answering and video captioning, their pixel-level visual grounding abilities are less studied. In this work, we raise the pertinent question of whether motion is used in pixel-level visual grounding and whether video MLLMs can segment objects based on natural language expressions describing their motion patterns. We identify the shortcomings in the current benchmarks, where we show that a single frame can often suffice for capturing the referring expression without any temporal reasoning. To address this, we introduce novel motion-centric probing, particularly designed for the visual grounding task, to study video MLLMs' ability to identify true motion from a fake one and their ability to grasp the ordering of motion. Consequently, we introduce MoCentric-Bench, a motion-centric benchmark designed to evaluate video MLLMs based on their ability to capture the interaction between motion and language, rather than relying primarily on static cues. We further establish strong single-image baselines that either match or surpass prior methods. Finally, we explore a simple motion-centric adaptation that provides state-of-the-art performance on our MoCentric-Bench. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PixFoundation: Are We Heading in the Right Direction with Pixel-level Vision Foundation Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.04192)**  `arXiv:2502.04192`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mennatullah Siam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiple works have emerged to push the boundaries of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) towards pixel-level understanding. The current trend is to train MLLMs with pixel-level grounding supervision in terms of masks on large-scale labelled data and specialized decoders for the segmentation task. However, we show that such MLLMs when evaluated on recent challenging vision-centric benchmarks, exhibit a weak ability in visual question answering (VQA). Surprisingly, some of these methods even downgrade the grounding ability of MLLMs that were never trained with such pixel-level supervision. In this work, we propose two novel challenging benchmarks with paired evaluation for both VQA and grounding. We demonstrate that simple baselines that are not unified achieve performance that matches or surpasses some of the pixel-level MLLMs. Our paired benchmarks and evaluation enable additional analysis on whether the failure stems from VQA and/or grounding abilities. Furthermore, we propose a paired prompt sensitivity analysis on both the language and visual prompts tailored for the grounding task. Our benchmarking pushes pixel-level MLLMs to improve in both accuracy and robustness, without sacrificing language abilities. More importantly, we study the research question of ``When does grounding emerge in MLLMs with respect to the output tokens?'' We propose a post-hoc interpretability tool that can be plugged into any MLLM to study the aforementioned question. We show that grounding does not necessarily coincide with the exact referring expression in the output, but can coincide with the object parts, its location, appearance, context or state. Code and datasets are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Geometry of Emotion Representations Across Scale, Depth, and Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35411)**  `arXiv:2610.35411`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Adam Hollowell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We measure the emotion geometry of six LLMs spanning Google's Gemma 2 family. Using an extraction pipeline established by Sofroniew et al. (2026) on Claude Sonnet 4.5 and replicated by Hollowell (2026) on Gemma 2 27B base, we compare emotion geometry across base and instruction-tuned variants of Gemma 2 2B, 9B, and 27B. First, all six model cells exhibit the core emotion geometry. A linear map fit between two cells places held-out emotions nearest their own vectors in the target model. Across all fifteen model pairs, fit in one direction each, this failed exactly once, and the median rank was 1 in every pair. Linear maps refit on cleaned embedding-table vectors recover every held-out emotion at rank 1 in all thirty ordered pairs. Second, scale spreads the geometry's variance across more directions without weakening its tie to valence. The leading component's share of variance falls from 31.6 percent (2B) to 29.8 (9B) to 26.7 (27B) in the base models and from 31.9 (2B) to 29.2 (9B) to 27.3 (27B) in the tuned ones, while its correlation with human valence ratings stays between .71 and .78 in all six models. Third, we project each emotion vector onto the output vocabulary and ask at what depth the vector's own emotion word enters the top 10,000 of the 256,000 tokens. In the 2B and 9B models the median emotion word is there from layer 0, while in the 27B models it begins near chance, stays outside the top 10,000 for half the network, and enters at layer 23 in one step. Fourth, tuning leaves every registered analysis-layer measure within its floor. At every size, valence correlation rises and arousal correlation falls by amounts within their floors, the latter against prediction. PC2's variance share also falls, with a pooled decrease significant after correction. Mid-late layer stability exceeds its floor and traces to base 27B's final layer. Code, data, and audit reports are released.
  </details>

- **[Transformer to GPT-5: Advancements, Limitations, and Future Directions in Large Language Model Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34281)**  `arXiv:2610.34281`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Harsidak Singh Banwait, Chander Prabha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized Artificial Intelligence (AI) by their extraordinary capabilities, like contextual understanding, few-shot/zero-shot learning, code generation, ability to reason and problem-solving. Recent studies have shown immense work on LLMs. Yet, there is limited work on the thorough taxonomy of LLMs. This study would showcase a detailed classification of LLMs, starting with the earlier models like BERT and leading to LLMs like GPT, Gemini, Claude and Grok. We would delve into topics like datasets used to train LLMs, computational power used during training, their hardware specifications, architecture and parameters. Further, we would also look at the limitations, computational challenges and open problems as well. This study would primarily focus on the timeline of LLMs, signifying their growth over the period. Findings reveal a clear pattern: models that optimize the ratio between architectural complexity and data quality exhibit stronger reasoning stability and better alignment, without proportionally increasing the computational load. This desired balance between scaling and sustainability is the defining characteristic of the current generation of AI.
  </details>

- **[Towards a Learnable Retriever for Textual Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33511)**  `arXiv:2610.33511`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Kaifeng Jin, Yuchen Yan, Zhichen Zeng, Xiao Lin, Lihui Liu, Yinglong Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Textual graph question answering (GraphQA) and graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) have gained increasing attention as a way to ground large language models (LLMs) on structured knowledge. Despite extensive efforts, existing methods still face two key gaps. The first is an architectural gap: existing methods typically fine-tune an LLM-based generator, but do not also make the retriever itself a learnable LLM for graph evidence selection within one end-to-end retrieve-then-generate pipeline. The second is a retrieval-supervision gap: existing retrievers rarely use gold answer signals to supervise evidence selection. As a result, they may fail to recall evidence nodes required for reasoning, resulting in insufficient coverage of the gold answer set; meanwhile, naive attempts to boost recall often introduce excessive irrelevant information that compromises compactness and overwhelms the LLM. To address these gaps, we propose TaLenT, an LLM-based end-to-end retriever-generator framework for textual GraphQA. To bridge the architectural gap, TaLenT pairs a learnable LLM-based retriever for evidence selection with a fine-tuned LLM-based generator within one unified retrieve-then-generate pipeline. To bridge the retrieval-supervision gap, we leverage gold answer signals as retriever supervision. Specifically, we stratify graph nodes into gold answers, answer-related nodes, and irrelevant noise. We then adopt a weighted pseudo-label loss to prioritize gold answers and preserve answer-related nodes for coverage, while discarding irrelevant noise for compactness. Experimental results across three GraphQA datasets demonstrate that TaLenT consistently improves downstream performance.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Transfer of Representational Signatures for LLM Reliability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33442)**  `arXiv:2610.33442`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yongtong Li, Yixuan Florence Wu, Jiawei Zhang, Naichen Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are reported to be susceptible to reliability issues including hallucinations and reasoning errors. Existing quality detectors can predict unreliable outputs based on internal representations with decent accuracies. However, training these detectors often requires substantial domain-specific labeled data, which limits their applicability to new domains. We propose to address this challenge by transferring representational signatures across related domains. More specifically, we build a statistical framework that first extracts low-dimensional signatures for each domain, then uses cross-fitting to learn composition weights of domain-specific predictors. The weights adapt to each source domain's predictive relevance to the target domain. Our asymptotic analysis of a least-squares analogue characterizes the limiting prediction risk and aggregation weights in terms of domain discrepancies and representation bias. Experiments across multiple hallucination datasets and open-weight LLMs show that, with only 25 labeled questions in the new domain, adaptive transfer improves hallucination-detection AUROC by 6.8%. For reasoning-step prediction, it improves AUROC by 8.4%.
  </details>

- **[Linear-Time Context Compression with State-Space Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33006)**  `arXiv:2610.33006`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Marc Serra Ortega, Andrey Barsky, Carlos Boned Riera, Tomás Ockier, Dimosthenis Karatzas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer-based large language models (LLMs) impose substantial computation and memory costs as context sizes increase, motivating methods that compress a context into a shorter representation before inference. Existing soft-prompt compression methods typically use the same Transformer backbone as used in the target model, where producing the compressed representation leads to significant latency overheads. This restricts them to the offline setting where context is compressed once and then reused across many queries. We propose the Boa Compressor, a plug-and-play online context compressor built on a Mamba state-space model (SSM) that encodes arbitrary-length context in a single linear-time pass, producing a representation on which we condition a frozen target LLM. The SSM is trained by distillation, matching the next-token logit distribution from the LLM ingesting the full context (as teacher) to the same LLM ingesting the compressed representation (as the student). At equal compression ratios, Boa matches or surpasses the performance of existing state-of-the-art offline compression baselines, while being an order of magnitude faster than Transformer-based implementations, thereby reducing end-to-end latency rather than adding compression time overhead. We demonstrate that Boa also scales well to longer contexts and to compression ratios as aggressive as 16x. By enabling effective compression that is cheap enough to run on every query at inference time, Boa becomes a practical and general tool for efficient long-context inference.
  </details>

- **[OpenESM: Energy-Steered Models Are Scalable Foundation Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33005)**  `arXiv:2610.33005`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuan Pu, Yudong Lu, Ting Wang, Zhicong Hong, Qinuo Li, Xuansheng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Energy-based models have shown promising properties for domain-specific tasks but remain challenging in modeling general-purpose scenarios. To bridge this gap, we design energy-steered models (ESMs) for foundational language modeling. ESMs integrate energy steering and token generation within a single architecture to leverage the energy-guided search to refine token generation. Specifically, ESMs first optimize a latent state over the energy landscape, then leverages the refined latent state for token generation. By performing such energy-guided pre-training on large-scale corpora, ESMs learn generally effective energy landscape where favorable tokens correspond to lower energy values. To explore the scaling behavior of ESMs, we pre-train ESMs on three large-scale corpora, then evaluate across 26 downstream tasks spanning a broad range of domains. Our results demonstrate that ESMs have promising scaling behaviors with increasing model size and training data, and outperform GPTs on most downstream tasks. These findings highlight ESMs as a promising architecture for large-scale pre-training and general purpose problem solving. We fully release the model weights and training infrastructure to enable the community to completely reproduce and extend our results.GitHub:this https URLHuggingFace:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Investigating and Alleviating Harm Amplification in LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02423)**  `arXiv:2606.02423`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruohao Guo, Wei Xu, Alan Ritter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can serve as helpful assistants, yet they can equally function as harm amplifiers that enable malicious users to achieve harmful outcomes beyond their capabilities through extended interactions. This risk manifests along two axes, i.e., democratizing domain expertise that allows novices to produce specialized harmful content, and scaling harmful operations at volumes that manual effort cannot match. Existing works, however, often overlook how LLMs compound harm across multi-turn conversations. We introduce HarmAmp, a new benchmark for multi-turn harm amplification scenarios spanning twelve risk categories. Each scenario is grounded in real-world threats and satisfies rigorous criteria, i.e., substantive amplification, operational specificity, and multi-turn necessity. We further propose TrajSafe, a proactive monitor that anticipates harmful trajectories and intervenes through actions such as probing users' genuine intents and steering the models towards safer completion. Our extensive experiments demonstrate that TrajSafe significantly reduces the harmfulness incurred in multi-turn interactions while preserving a low over-refusal rate and the target model's general capabilities. Our work offers a promising paradigm to alleviate the nuanced safety risks in LLM interactions.
  </details>

- **[LatentRAG: Latent Reasoning and Retrieval for Efficient Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06285)**  `arXiv:2605.06285`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yijia Zheng, Marcel Worring_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-step retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) provides an efficient way to incorporate external information for simple question answering tasks but struggles with complex questions. Agentic RAG extends this paradigm by replacing single-step retrieval with a multi-step process, in which the large language model (LLM) acts as a search agent that generates intermediate thoughts and subqueries to iteratively interact with the retrieval system. This iterative process incurs substantial latency due to the autoregressive generation of lengthy thoughts and subqueries. To address this limitation, we propose LatentRAG, a novel framework that shifts both reasoning and retrieval from discrete language space to continuous latent space. Unlike existing explicit methods that generate natural language thoughts or subqueries token-by-token, LatentRAG produces latent tokens for thoughts and subqueries directly from the hidden states in a single forward pass. We align LLMs with dense retrieval models in the latent space, enabling retrieval over latent subquery tokens and supporting end-to-end joint optimization. To improve transparency and encourage semantically meaningful latent representations, we incorporate a parallel latent decoding mechanism that translates latent tokens back into natural language. Extensive experiments on seven benchmark datasets show that LatentRAG achieves performance comparable to explicit agentic RAG methods while reducing inference latency by approximately 90%, substantially narrowing the latency gap with traditional single-step RAG.
  </details>

- **[ActTail: Global Activation Sparsity in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12272)**  `arXiv:2603.12272`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Wenwen Hou, Xinyuan Song, Wenqian Weng, Shiwei Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation sparsity is a promising approach for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference by reducing computation and memory movement. However, existing activation sparsity methods typically apply uniform sparsity across projections, ignoring the heterogeneous statistical properties of Transformer weights and thereby amplifying performance degradation. In this paper, we propose ActTail, a TopK magnitude-based activation sparsity method with global activation sparsity allocation grounded in Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR) theory. Specifically, we capture this heterogeneity via the heavy-tail exponent computed from each projection's empirical spectral density (ESD), which is used as a quantitative indicator to assign projection-specific sparsity budgets. Importantly, we provide a theoretical analysis that establishes an explicit relationship between the activation sparsity ratio and the heavy-tail exponent under the HT-SR regime, offering principled guidance for sparsity allocation beyond heuristic design. Experiments on LLaMA and Mistral models show that our method improves both perplexity and downstream task performance at high sparsity compared to uniform allocation. At 80% sparsity, perplexity is reduced by 21.8% on LLaMA-2-7B, 40.1% on LLaMA-2-13B, and 9.4% on Mistral-7B.
  </details>

- **[EviGround-CT: Sentence-to-Volume Grounding for Verifying LLM-Generated Chest CT Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33038)**  `arXiv:2610.33038`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziyuan Li, Juncheng Ni, Feng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chest computed tomography (CT) reports generated by large language models (LLMs) can describe positive findings that lack localized evidence in the scan. We propose EviGround-CT to verify these findings after report generation. A deterministic extractor identifies positive finding sentences, and a 3D grounding model predicts an evidence mask for each sentence. Using the full sentence retains anatomical location, laterality, and descriptive modifiers in the grounding query. We evaluate report generation on CT-RATE, and grounding and verification on a held-out 100-case ReXGroundingCT subset. Using the available annotations, sentence-grounded verification raises evidence-supported precision (ESP) from 0.263 to 0.365 across 137 extracted in-scope positives, compared with category conditioning. The corresponding unsupported positive finding rate falls from 0.737 to 0.635. The paired case-level bootstrap ESP difference is +0.102 (95% confidence interval: [0.028, 0.181]). The predicted masks associate individual report statements with spatial evidence that can be inspected alongside the CT.
  </details>

- **[Leadership as Coordination Control: Behavioral Signatures and the Recovery-Advantage Boundary in Multi-Agent LLM Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19111)**  `arXiv:2606.19111`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Haewoon Kwak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Team science holds that leadership is contingent: it helps only under specific conditions. We ask the same question of multi-agent LLM teams: under what measurable conditions does process-level coordination control add value, and do those conditions match what team science predicts? This takes measurement, not just accuracy: we use behavioral signatures (majority lock-in, exploration, recovery from an incorrect round-0 consensus) and per-action ablations, clean because each controller is an explicit action set. We operationalize three classical leadership styles (transactional, transformational, situational) as controllers over a shared action vocabulary (explore, revise, accept, synthesize). A matched controller with the same actions but an arbitrary rule recovers no better than majority voting. Across four task regimes and three open-weight model families, no controller dominates by accuracy. Transactional control matches a shared round-0 vote on all 12 combinations to within 1.3pp, and accuracy gains appear only on the single llama-4-scout social combination, where the round-0 majority is unreliable. Against the stronger flat baseline, only situational still gains (+8pp). A recovery-advantage account, tested with four boundary probes, says a controller beats plain interaction only where the round-0 majority is unreliable, the task is recoverable, and undirected interaction does not already repair it. Because these conditions map onto contingency theory (leadership substitutes, path-goal redundancy, the readiness gap), a largely null accuracy result is what the theory predicts, not a failure of the controllers. We advance this readiness-gap boundary as an explanatory hypothesis rather than an established general principle. Its null side is corroborated across every other combination, including under held-out prediction, while its positive side is observed in a single combination.
  </details>

- **[Learning When to Commit: Schedule Distillation for Fast Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35804)**  `arXiv:2610.35804`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Aidar Myrzakhan, Zhiqiang Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) can generate multiple tokens in parallel, but often require many decoding rounds to complete a response. Efficient generation depends not only on which tokens are easy to predict, but also on how committing to them helps predict the remaining positions. A highly predictable token may provide little useful context, while revealing a less predictable token can help several other positions become predictable sooner. We propose dSpark, a new schedule distillation-based framework for learning when to commit tokens for faster diffusion language generation. To achieve this, we construct unmasking schedules that prioritize tokens whose reveal makes other positions easier to predict, reducing uncertainty earlier in decoding. We then distill these schedules into the model to enable faster parallel sampling, with a KL-divergence regularizer to help preserve generation quality. We extensively evaluate our approach across mathematical reasoning and code generation tasks. On GSM8K, our method achieves remarkable 10.12$\times$ and 5.12$\times$ speedups over the various baselines on LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct, respectively, while maintaining superior task performance. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models: A Plain-Language Overview for Engineers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34585)**  `arXiv:2610.34585`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bahaeddin Türkoğlu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are neural networks trained on huge text collections to generate text by predicting what comes next. Engineers using ChatGPT and other generative artificial intelligence (AI) tools need a clear picture of what training changes and prompts add. The article starts with the Transformer, which connects words in context, and pre-training, where models learn to predict text. It then explains how fine-tuning adapts models to requests, with reinforcement learning from human feedback using human answer rankings. Next, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) supplies documents, while chain-of-thought prompting encourages intermediate steps and agents use tools. FlashAttention lowers training and running costs by reducing data movement during attention; quantized low-rank adaptation (QLoRA) saves fine-tuning memory. Readers get a life cycle figure and tables comparing methods and their limits. A short checklist for judging LLM evaluation claims flags test material in training (data contamination) and model-based grading (LLM-as-a-judge). It also covers very long inputs (long context) and hallucination, meaning false or unsupported claims. Check factual accuracy separately from whether an answer sounds helpful.
  </details>

- **[T2D: Target-to-Draft Model Pruning & Adaptation for Efficient Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34103)**  `arXiv:2610.34103`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shahriar Kabir Nahin, Xiao Ye, Muhao Chen, Ben Zhou, Anshuman Chhabra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding significantly accelerates autoregressive text generation in Large Language Models (LLMs), but its efficacy depends largely on two key factors: the distributional alignment between the draft and the target model, and the draft model's latency during inference. However, existing approaches typically utilize independently pretrained small draft models or seek to prune the target model to a manually chosen architecture, thereby failing to explicitly balance the trade-off between draft model alignment and token generation efficiency. To address this gap, we formulate draft model design as an optimization problem and propose T2D, a framework that constructs draft models directly from the target model using latency-aware structured pruning and knowledge distillation. In this manner, T2D obtains draft models that are jointly optimized for both measured inference latency and cross-entropy loss on the target model's responses, resulting in superlative speculative decoding performance. Through extensive experiments on multi-domain speculative decoding benchmarks and across several target model families of differing scale, we demonstrate how T2D prunes up to 92\% of the target model to obtain highly performant draft models compared to competitive baselines. Moreover, draft models obtained via T2D can also be utilized by other speculative decoding token-alignment and knowledge distillation methods in a plug-and-play fashion, leading to further gains. Our findings underscore the importance of jointly navigating the draft model architecture-alignment frontier to unlock the full potential of speculative decoding for LLMs.
  </details>

- **[StateWise: Teaching Language Models to Track and Obey Evolving Constraints in Multi-Turn Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33766)**  `arXiv:2610.33766`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Samarth Varshney, Ritu Yadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Users of conversational assistants rarely state all requirements up front: they add constraints, amend them, withdraw them, digress, and return. Instruction-tuned LLMs fail here in two ways: active constraints stop being honored as turns accumulate (persistence failure), and withdrawn constraints keep being enacted (relapse). The only existing repair we know of is an external ledger that re-injects a compiled specification every turn. We propose StateWise, a self-improving approach that teaches a single LLM to maintain its own revisable constraint state, a typed state with tombstones for revoked constraints, so persistence and relapse are measured by executable checkers and ground-truth states are compiled for free. StateWise fine-tunes the LLM on two complementary skills, summarizing a dialogue into its constraint state and responding conditioned on that state, then self-trains on its own trajectories, keeping only turns that pass an exact round-trip check against the compiled state. This teaches 7-8B models to track the state almost perfectly but, surprisingly, not to obey it: on Llama-3.1-8B every supervised variant roughly doubles relapse. We therefore add a preference stage on self-generated relapse contrasts, where the rejected response enacts a revoked rule and the chosen one passes the round-trip check. On two backbones with held-out constraint families and unseen phrasings, StateWise cuts relapse by 8-22 points relative to supervised self-training, lifts conversation success by 8-19 points over the backbone, and beats an oracle compiled-ledger baseline on relapse with no inference-time scaffolding. We also characterize the recipe's cost on general instruction-following benchmarks and study weight interpolation as a mitigation.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Semantic Divergence for LLM Agent Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33012)**  `arXiv:2610.33012`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Harshavardhan Abichandani, Penny Chong, Atin Ghosh, Daniel Dahlmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool use exhibit inconsistent behavior across independent runs from identical states, calling different tools, producing contradictory outputs, or pursuing divergent strategies. This variability compounds over time and undermines deployment reliability. Existing evaluation metrics primarily measure task correctness via completion-based scores and do not capture consistency in agent behavior across executions. Separately, agent behavior is analyzed either at the token level using probability distributions or at the single-response level using semantic clustering, but neither captures how inconsistency propagates across future turns of a trajectory. We introduce Recursive Semantic Divergence (RSD), an unsupervised trajectory-level consistency metric adapted from bisimulation metrics in reinforcement learning. RSD measures the expected cumulative semantic disagreement between two independent rollouts of the same agent from the same state, using natural language inference to detect logical inconsistency. It is defined recursively over future states and approximated by a neural network trained on independent rollouts. The learned metric estimates future divergence from the current state, providing a consistency score at each turn without requiring the full trajectory to complete. When used as a reward signal for fine-tuning, RSD reduces the variation in task progress from 132% to 91% on tau2-bench and from 90% to 44% on toolsandbox, while improving performance across benchmarks and model scales. Training with RSD as an additional reward signal matches supervised fine-tuning performance with substantially lower variance, without requiring ground-truth labels.
  </details>

- **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561)**  `arXiv:2610.21561`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sergio Burdisso, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses atthis https URL, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.
  </details>

- **[Amplitude-Only FFN Intervention for Tool-Structured LLM Inference: Method, Gated Evaluation Protocol, and Cross-Model Empirical Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11183)**  `arXiv:2607.11183`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sheng Xu, Zhen Chen, Junhua Wang, Boyuan Huang, Ke Jia, Jiadun Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study amplitude-only rescaling of feed-forward network (FFN) activations to repair tool calls and other structured outputs, using multiplicative coordinate masks rather than additive steering vectors. Amplitude Gating (AG) keeps pretrained weights fixed and applies magnitude/entropy-based masks at six SwiGLU sites, with a Newton-Schulz windowed ranking variant. Across three Qwen checkpoints and six text/tool endpoints, effects are task dependent. Under frozen local scorers, Qwen3.5-9B non-NS configurations improve two function-call endpoints by +2.61/+2.67 percentage points (pp) and static ToolBench in/out-domain by +1.64/+2.82 pp; JSON gains are small. These gains survive comparing the weaker of two observed AG runs with the stronger baseline, supporting observed-run robustness rather than population significance. Scores measure lexical or answer/action-name agreement, not executed-tool success. Under the current experimental conditions, the six-task random-forest refit improves native score by +0.46 pp with a positive conditional paired interval; transfer to other settings requires further evaluation. The results support task-specific interventions; causal mechanisms, equal-budget advantages, repeated-run uncertainty, and serving cost require further evaluation. Code and experimental data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Needles in Document Haystacks: Sensitivity Testing of LLM-as-a-Judge Similarity Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18835)**  `arXiv:2604.18835`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sinan G. Aksoy, Alexandra A. Sabrio, Erik VonKaenel, Lee Burke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a scalable, multifactorial experimental framework that systematically probes LLM sensitivity to subtle semantic changes in pairwise document comparison. We analogize this as a needle-in-a-haystack problem: a single semantically altered sentence (the needle) is embedded within surrounding context (the hay), and we vary the perturbation type (negation, conjunction swap, named entity replacement), context type (original vs. topically unrelated), needle position, and document length across all combinations, testing seven LLMs on tens of thousands of document pairs. Our analysis reveals several striking findings. First, LLMs can exhibit a within-document positional bias distinct from previously studied candidate-order effects: some models penalize semantic differences more harshly when they occur earlier in a document. Second, when the altered sentence is surrounded by topically unrelated context, it raises similarity scores in almost all settings. This is consistent with a contradiction account, in which topically related context allows the alteration to conflict with the surrounding sentences. Third, all models exhibit the same hierarchy in how harshly they score different perturbation types, with negation penalized the most, but express this hierarchy on significantly different scales. Finally, varying the prompt's conception of semantic similarity across several alternatives changes the magnitude but not the direction of these effects. Together, these results demonstrate how LLM semantic similarity scores are sensitive to the aforementioned parameters in ways that go beyond the semantic change itself; our framework offers a practical toolkit for auditing and comparing scoring behavior across current and future models.
  </details>

- **[Towards Real-world Human Behavior Simulation: Benchmarking Large Language Models on Long-horizon, Cross-scenario, Heterogeneous Behavior Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08362)**  `arXiv:2604.08362`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiawei Chen, Ruoxi Xu, Boxi Cao, Ruotong Pan, Yunfei Zhang, Yifei Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has illuminated the potential for a general-purpose user simulator. However, existing benchmarks remain constrained to isolated scenarios, narrow action spaces, or synthetic data, failing to capture the holistic nature of authentic human behavior. To bridge this gap, we introduce OmniBehavior, the first predictive user simulation benchmark constructed entirely from real-world data, integrating long-horizon, cross-scenario, and heterogeneous behavioral patterns into a unified framework. Based on this benchmark, we first provide empirical evidence that previous datasets with isolated scenarios suffer from tunnel vision, whereas real-world decision-making relies on long-term, cross-scenario causal chains. Extensive evaluations of state-of-the-art LLMs reveal that current models struggle to accurately predict these complex behaviors, with performance plateauing even as context windows expand. Crucially, a systematic comparison between simulated and authentic behaviors uncovers a fundamental structural bias: LLMs tend to converge towards a positive average person, exhibiting hyper-activity, persona homogenization, and a utopian bias. This results in the loss of individual differences and long-tail behaviors, highlighting critical directions for future high-fidelity simulation research.
  </details>

- **[Robust and Fine-Grained Detection of AI Generated Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11952)**  `arXiv:2504.11952`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ram Mohan Rao Kadiyala, Siddartha Pullakhandam, Kanwal Mehreen, Drishti Sharma, Siddhant Gupta, Jebish Purbey, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An ideal detection system for machine generated content is supposed to work well on any generator as many more advanced LLMs come into existence day by day. Existing systems often struggle with accurately identifying AI-generated content over shorter texts. Further, not all texts might be entirely authored by a human or LLM, hence we focused more over partial cases i.e human-LLM co-authored texts. Our paper introduces a set of models built for the task of token classification which are trained on an extensive collection of human-machine co-authored texts, which performed well over texts of unseen domains, unseen generators, texts by non-native speakers and those with adversarial inputs. We also introduce a new dataset of over 2.4M such texts mostly co-authored by several popular proprietary LLMs over 23 languages. We also present findings of our models' performance over each texts of each domain and generator. Additional findings include comparison of performance against each adversarial method, length of input texts and characteristics of generated texts compared to the original human authored texts.
  </details>

- **[Adapting LLM-SC to Living Sacred Heritage: Domain-Specific Extraction, Semantic Networks and Human Evaluation on Pashupatinath Temple Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35685)**  `arXiv:2610.35685`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hari Sitaula, Manish Sakhakarmy, Pujan Pant, Sagar Newpane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) pipelines can turn visitor reviews into structured data, but generic schemes may miss rituals, sacred spaces, cultural restrictions and emotionally mixed experiences at living sacred heritage sites. We adapted the LLM-Social Computing (LLM-SC) framework to Pashupatinath Temple, Nepal, by specifying a 12-type entity taxonomy (four original LLM-SC types plus eight sacred-heritage types), four relation types and nine aspect-sentiment dimensions in a GPT-4o extraction prompt, which was applied to a fixed random sample of 2,500 English-language Tripadvisor reviews from a processed corpus of 3,939. The exports contain 11,286 entity, 6,232 aspect-sentiment and 5,590 relation records, covering 2,485, 2,487 and 2,433 reviews, respectively; the eight added sacred-heritage types account for 64.8% of entity records. Against an adjudicated two-annotator reference for 200 reviews, exact normalized surface-and-type matching gave provisional entity scores of 41.0% precision, 21.8% recall and 28.4% micro-F1. Aspect-presence F1 was 53.6% and end-to-end aspect-plus-polarity F1 was 42.6%. Human adjudication supported 153 of 194 first-listed relation candidates (one per review; 78.9%), a candidate-level rate that does not estimate relation recall or whole-graph precision. The contribution is a culturally grounded, domain-specific adaptation with component-wise human evaluation and explicit reproducibility and generalization limitations.
  </details>

- **[Constrained Decoding: Harmful or Beneficial? Revisiting Its Impact on LLM Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35328)**  `arXiv:2610.35328`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ngoc Trinh Hung Nguyen, Alonso Silva, Armen Aghasaryan, Mehwish Alam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Constrained decoding guarantees that Large Language Models (LLMs) produce outputs conforming to predefined formats, but is often assumed to inherently degrade reasoning. We examine this assumption through a taxonomy of three constraint types: \emph{format constraints}, which enforce output structure; \emph{validity constraints}, which mask invalid answer; and \emph{reasoning constraints}, which restrict reasoning trajectories. We theoretically show that appropriate constraints can preserve or improve performance by eliminating invalid paths. Across four model families and eleven open-source models, causal ablations on diverse benchmarks using state-of-the-art constrained decoding strategies reveal that apparent performance degradation largely stems from non-equivalent prompting and output formats rather than from decoder-level masking. Meanwhile, validity constraints improve reliability and extractability by eliminating invalid answers, while reasoning constraints can improve reasoning and, consequently, accuracy when they enforce beneficial trajectories. These findings show that the impact of constrained decoding depends on the type of constraint and the output space it removes. Our code is available.
  </details>

- **[NumTrace: Auditing the Number Provenance of Financial Analyst Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34887)**  `arXiv:2610.34887`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tianlei Zhu, Matt White, Xue Liu, Xiao-Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A numerical claim generated by a financial analyst agent can match a number in a company filing but refer to the wrong entity, concept, period, unit or scope. A check based on reference answers can mark such an answer as wrong but does not explain why it fails. In this paper, we present NumTrace, a new benchmark that audits the provenance of each numerical claim made by financial analyst agents: its source and financial semantics. NumTrace currently has 540 questions over 2,163 SEC EDGAR documents. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first numerical benchmark about who holds a company's shares (Form 13F) and what its executives are owed (Item 402(i)). On a frozen snapshot of these documents, we build the reference answers according to accounting rules. NumTrace then gives each claim three separate verdicts: value, binding and support. By retaining separate value and binding verdicts, NumTrace detects right number, wrong account (RNWA) errors: a claim has an RNWA error when it passes the value check but fails the binding check. The RNWA rate quantifies the gap between value-only and joint value-and-binding pass rates. Finally, we audit three agents, Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. RNWA errors make up 67.5-81.8% of each agent's failing claims. In particular, on 13F co-filing group questions, about 91% of claims pass the value check, but fewer than half pass the binding check.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Retrieval, Selective Answers: Evaluating Conformal Factuality in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34872)**  `arXiv:2610.34872`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Aimal Rehman, Chi-Kuang Yeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can improve factual grounding without guaranteeing that every generated claim is supported. Previous work has studied claim-level conformal factuality in single-stage RAG and, more recently, multi-hop RAG. We examine its behavior in agentic RAG, where a language-model policy adaptively issues follow-up retrieval queries or terminates retrieval before response generation. We retain the retrieval-based relevance-scoring formulation and post-generation conformal filtering and evaluate six model-dataset configurations: GPT-6 Luna and Llama 3.1 8B on HotpotQA, Natural Questions, and TriviaQA. Each configuration uses 60 fixed questions, 100 seeded 30/30 calibration-test partitions, five conformal target levels, and a no-conformal-prediction baseline. Across the six configurations, higher target levels are accompanied by higher observed full-support rates and reduced answer availability. At the 95% target, non-empty response coverage ranges from 17.23% to 60.17%, claim retention from 10.72% to 43.16%, and full support among non-empty outputs from 89.14% to 95.56%. We formulate the adaptive retrieval trajectory explicitly and characterize the reliability-informativeness trade-off when conformal factuality is applied after agent termination.
  </details>

- **[DynEdit: Dynamic Entropy-Guided Sequential Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34768)**  `arXiv:2610.34768`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinhu Fu, Yan Bai, Yihang Lou, Li Sun, Jiahong Wu, Xiangxiang Chu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge editing aims to update outdated or incorrect knowledge in large language models without full retraining. Existing parameter-editing methods remain limited for long-sequence knowledge: single-point editors provide insufficient coverage for extended targets, while fixed chunk-based editors distribute editing effort uniformly across rigid predefined windows, failing to prioritize difficult, information-dense tokens. In this work, we show that token entropy is closely associated with both editing difficulty and semantic importance: high-entropy regions are harder to edit and often contain key factual content. Motivated by this observation, we propose DynEdit, a dynamic framework for long-sequence knowledge editing. DynEdit uses token-level entropy to guide non-uniform sliding, increasing the coverage of difficult high-entropy regions, and further applies entropy-guided token-level optimization to strengthen updates on information-dense tokens within each editing window. Experiments on three benchmarks and two backbone models show that DynEdit consistently outperforms competitive baselines across diverse long-form editing settings, achieving gains of up to +14.0 BLEU points on challenging paraphrase queries and near-saturated semantic scores on several diverse knowledge domains.
  </details>

- **[Entity Matching using Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34626)**  `arXiv:2610.34626`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Aaron Steiner, Ralph Peeters, Christian Bizer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Entity matching is the task of deciding whether two records describe the same real-world entity. Current entity matching methods rely on pre-trained language models (PLMs) which require task specific fine-tuning or generative large language models (LLMs) which do not require task-specific fine-tuning but are slow and expensive at matching large record sets. Decision models are a new class of LLM-based models that return a probability for each predefined answer option rather than generate text. This paper investigates decision models as an alternative to generative LLMs and fine-tuned PLMs for entity matching. We evaluate six decision models in a zero-shot setting across eight benchmarks and compare their performance with that of the generative LLM GPT-6 Luna and the PLM-based matcher Ditto. Our experiments show that, without task-specific training data, the four strongest decision models achieve mean F1 scores close to that of GPT-6 Luna, with the best model slightly surpassing it. This model also outperforms Ditto on six of the eight benchmarks. All decision models are faster than GPT-6 Luna, and most are less expensive. For three of the four strongest decision models, the predicted match probabilities are well calibrated without requiring any task-specific recalibration. A model cascade that routes the least confident record pairs to an ensemble of generative LLMs improves the mean F1 scores of the four strongest decision models by 1.9-4.0 percentage points. Using the best decision model and its best-performing prompt for each benchmark, the cascade surpasses the highest published F1 scores from literature on four of the eight benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[CoLink: Global Compression and Knowledge Linking for Multi-Turn Dialogue Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34335)**  `arXiv:2610.34335`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Lian Yan, Zhenbo Xia, Haotian Wang, Chen Tang, Yi Guan, Jie Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Extracting salient information from long-horizon dialogue histories remains a significant challenge for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to the restricted context windows and efficient retrieval challenges. Current methods typically rely on history organization or local token-level optimization, suffering from information loss, retrieval redundancy, and myopic attention. Inspired by the view in cognitive science that dialogue constitutes sense-making and memory association, we propose CoLink, a framework that enhances LLMs' global context awareness in domain-specific multi-turn dialogues through information linking and compression. CoLink first clusters historical contexts into topic graphs to establish cross-semantic associations with external knowledge. Guided by the adaptive control of thought, it then retrieves and activates knowledge via synergizing semantic relevance and graph-structural topology. Crucially, to achieve effective compression of history and knowledge, we ground multi-turn dialogues in information theory and derive a principled optimization objective for extracting the core sense-making information. Coupled with a competitive slot mechanism, CoLink condenses the global context into dedicated memory and knowledge slots, efficiently distilling lengthy, redundant inputs into an extremely compact latent representation. Extensive empirical results on both public and our MMD datasets validate CoLink's superiority and generalizability in knowledge linking and sense modeling.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Belief: Evaluating and Mitigating LLM Susceptibility to Misinformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33962)**  `arXiv:2610.33962`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Emran Kanaan, Jiho Lee, Ibrahim Odat, Soroush Vosoughi, Weicheng Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using large language models (LLMs) must decide whether external information warrants action. We investigate whether their expressed credibility judgments reliably indicate behavioral resistance to misinformation. We evaluate five LLMs on 196 constructed false notices paired with legitimate counterparts and 204 archived attacks adapted to simulated tool use. When asked to act on the constructed notices, models issue harmful tool calls in 27%--99% of cases they have rated untrustworthy. Removing the preliminary rating step increases harmful execution by 15 percentage points on average, showing that credibility elicitation itself changes the behavior being evaluated. Interventions that improve credibility discrimination do not consistently yield safer actions. A simple forewarning significantly reduces harmful execution in four of five models, but neither tested generated-example prebunk outperforms it in the main evaluation. These reductions can also compromise legitimate task completion. Supervised fine-tuning on generated false notices reduces harmful execution on held-out archived attacks under the rating-first protocol, with model-dependent utility costs and weaker protection without rating elicitation. Our findings motivate evaluating misinformation defenses through both harmful actions and legitimate task completion, while accounting for the behavioral effects of the evaluation protocol.
  </details>

- **[What Makes a Question Curious? Measuring Model Curiosity in Human-LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33950)**  `arXiv:2610.33950`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Angana Borah, Rachana Ramchandar, Inderjeet Nair, Rada Mihalcea_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly act as partners in everyday communication. In these interactions, useful assistance may require not only answering, but also asking the right questions that surface knowledge gaps, assumptions, and alternatives. In this study, we refer to such epistemic question-asking as model curiosity, and introduce a theory-grounded taxonomy that distinguishes curiosity-oriented questions from generic model questioning. Across four conversation datasets, we find that curiosity-oriented questions remain rare (17-28% of all questions), but are associated with more exploratory and epistemically rich subsequent user responses, with varying patterns across datasets. We further conduct a human study to evaluate the usefulness of model curiosity, finding that curiosity can encourage exploration, especially in advice, creative, and research contexts. These findings have implications for building LLMs that ask more selectively to support deeper exploration and human-AI collaboration.
  </details>

- **[RSI Arena: Towards Recursive Self-Improvement with Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33624)**  `arXiv:2610.33624`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Huang, Yanbo Wang, Lichi Li, Yuanyuan Chen, Andreas Haupt, Xiaonan Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can AI agents autonomously train useful language models and improve them using feedback from users? We introduce RSI Arena, a two-stage study of autonomous post-training as a step toward recursive self-improvement. Ten frontier models act as training agents, each starting from NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning 30B-A3B Base. During a six-day first stage, each agent has 1,000 GPU-hours and $300 in API credit on a shared 64-GPU cluster, independently selecting data, implementing training procedures, and choosing checkpoints. We evaluate their models on 20 benchmarks and through blind human evaluations. All ten agents produce working assistants, with the strongest model, trained by GPT-6 Astra, closing a median of 76% of the base-to-Instruct gap across 12 capability benchmarks. Its human-preference rating is also close to NVIDIA Instruct after statistical adjustment for answer length and formatting. In Stage 2, each agent receives the comparison records and votes involving its Stage 1 model and is given three additional days for further training. Using NVIDIA Instruct as a common reference across the two evaluation rounds, we observe an improvement from Stage 1 to Stage 2 in the style-adjusted relative rating of eight of the ten models. We show that benchmark gains were modest, with no clear link to preference gains, while some agents encouraged longer responses and one model showed increased harmful compliance on StrongREJECT. RSI Arena provides an auditable setting for studying how human feedback can guide autonomous model development. It takes a step toward recursive self-improvement with human feedback (RSIHF), in which people help define what improvement means for the AI systems they use.
  </details>

- **[Mycelium: A Meta-Study of Hallucination Detection Across Provenance, Typology, and Granularities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33608)**  `arXiv:2610.33608`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Julia Belikova, Elisei Rykov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucination detection is increasingly important for evaluating and deploying large language models. However, existing methods are often compared across benchmarks that define and measure hallucination in substantially different ways, making reported scores difficult to interpret across settings. We study hallucination detection as a benchmark-conditioned measurement problem and organize existing benchmarks along three axes: provenance, which describes how model outputs and evidence conditions are obtained; typology, which captures the factuality and faithfulness failures represented by benchmark labels; and granularity, which specifies the unit at which hallucinations are annotated, predicted, and evaluated. Using this framework, we analyze how benchmark construction, annotation, and evaluation choices shape the underlying prediction task, and relate detector mechanisms and assumptions to the settings in which their performance is measured. We also examine span-level evaluation, where variation in annotation boundaries can substantially affect reported results, and propose Span Coverage F1 as a complementary containment-based metric. Our analysis clarifies what detector scores capture, highlights limitations of cross-benchmark comparisons, and motivates more explicit evaluation of generalization across benchmark conditions.
  </details>

- **[SEDATE: Do Large Language Models Share an Aggression Circuit Across Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33542)**  `arXiv:2610.33542`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sagnik Basu, Sayan Das, Somnath Banerjee, Animesh Mukherjee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As the applications of AI continue to expand across various multimodal and multilingual domains, ensuring safe and responsible deployment of these systems has become an important concern. A wide-range of interventions have been proposed to make AI responses safer and more reliable. They often incur substantial computational or annotation costs, or cannot be extended to multilingual settings. In this work, we propose Selective Detection And Targeted Editing (SEDATE), a training-free approach based on detect-edit-verify strategy. SEDATE first detects the responsible neurons, edits them as and when necessary and finally verifies the model responses; all without any additional model training. We evaluate SEDATE across 7 languages and 4 model families. We further evaluate its effectiveness across 4 out-of-distribution languages and 4 additional datasets. We show that SEDATE achieves substantially superior safety scores across all languages and models over the best-performing baseline while retaining the general capabilities and incurring negligible overhead. Our results are robust, with the trends remaining similar for out-of-distribution languages and datasets.
  </details>

- **[Quantifying Meaning with SCAPE-T: Semantic Complexity from Encoder-Only Transformer Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33441)**  `arXiv:2610.33441`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Maxim Khovansky, Lev Mukhanov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated semantic complexity estimation remains underexplored in NLP, despite important applications in linguistics and interpretability. This paper defines semantic complexity as the code length required to uniquely specify a text's semantic meaning, and operationalises it via SCAPE-T, an attention-derived metric based on encoder-only transformers. Existing measures either model reader effort (readability, lexical difficulty), which correlates with surface features (word length, syntax), or quantify surface features directly (type-token ratio, word frequency). SCAPE-T targets semantic integration directly by combining three submetrics: (i) inverse-weighted self-focus (token-to-self), (ii) CLS focus (token-to-CLS), and (iii) inter-head redundancy (cosine similarity across heads), using layer-weighted aggregation; data-driven learning is confined to hyperparameter tuning for calibration. Development uses philosophical prose vs. MCTest stories; evaluation is on children's stories vs. technical Wikipedia articles, with pairwise accuracy on contrasts as the primary criterion. Baselines include readability, syntactic/lexical, and LLM-derived measures. SCAPE-T substantially outperforms all tested baselines under word-length control, with a pairwise accuracy of 78.5% (95% bootstrap CI: 76.1-80.4, N=10k pairwise comparisons; +12.7 percentage points over the strongest baseline); without that control, readability heuristics overtake, consistent with their reliance on surface features. On a short-terms abstractness dataset, SCAPE-T also leads. Ablations show each submetric contributes materially. Overall, the results show SCAPE-T isolates conceptual structure from lexical/syntactic confounds better than existing methods, and generalises across two held-out domains.
  </details>

- **[Merge-Based Vocabulary Extension Stalls at the Pre-Tokenizer: An English-Preserving Repair for Nepali, Tested in Continued Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33016)**  `arXiv:2610.33016`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Aarjan Chaudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Llama-3, Qwen3 and many other open LLMs pre-tokenize text with a regular expression whose word class admits only Unicode letters. Devanagari vowel signs are combining marks, so this regex splits Nepali words at every vowel sign, and models that use it spend 2.6 to 5.8 times as many tokens on Nepali as on the same English text. Merge-based vocabulary extension cannot join the split pieces: for Llama-3 and Qwen3 no number of new merges brings this premium below about 2.2, and ten released extensions already end within 4% of this pre-token floor. We give a small edit to the deployed regex that provably keeps every token id of text without combining marks or Devanagari characters. With 32,000 new merges it brings Nepali to 1.01 times English, about 54% fewer Nepali tokens than standard extension. In small-budget continued pretraining of Llama-3.2-1B and Qwen3-0.6B, the original tokenizer gave the lowest held-out Nepali bits per byte under every recipe we tested. Matching the repair's training steps to standard extension, with about 35% of the data repeated, reversed their order on Qwen but not on Llama. Whether the token savings give a favourable latency and quality trade-off in generation remains untested. We release the tokenizers and code.
  </details>

- **[SLICE-Mem: Salience-Gated Lightweight Information Curation for Efficient LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33015)**  `arXiv:2610.33015`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaobin Chen, Dian Jin, Lin Shu, Defeng Sun, Xiaoming Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory enables large language model (LLM) agents to maintain personalized and temporally coherent interactions, but repeated LLM inference for memory admission, extraction, and organization introduces substantial cost and latency. We present SLICE-Mem, a lightweight memory framework that combines salience-guided admission and predicate-centered information extraction. A compact classifier identifies user turns worth retaining, and a semantic-dependency-based extractor converts admitted turns into concise memory entries. This online pipeline requires no generative LLM calls, while cross-entry reconciliation is deferred to an offline consolidation stage. We evaluate SLICE-Mem on the weekly subset of Memora and LongMemEval-S using 8B and 30B Qwen backbones for memory construction and consolidation on both benchmarks. Compared with the generative-memory baseline MemOS under the Qwen3-30B configuration, SLICE-Mem's online pipeline achieves 46.60 versus 46.68 FAMA (Forgetting-Aware Memory Accuracy) on Memora Weekly and 56.8% versus 59.3% accuracy on LongMemEval-S, while reducing total LLM token consumption by 92.4% and 99.5%, respectively. These results demonstrate that SLICE-Mem can preserve useful evidence for long-term memory with substantially less reliance on LLM-based memory construction.
  </details>

- **[TraceQuery: learning what to recompute from full-denoising trajectory in diffusion language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33008)**  `arXiv:2610.33008`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yukai Jin, Zhankai Ye, Jiayue Yang, Fangyi Li, Shuoqiu Li, Xu Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) enable flexible generation through iterative masked denoising, but incur substantial inference cost because token representations are repeatedly recomputed across denoising steps. Existing cache-based methods reduce this cost by reusing intermediate results across denoising steps and recomputing only a subset of positions at each step, chosen either on a fixed schedule or by heuristic signals of change. Our trajectory experiments instead point to decoding trajectory as the more relevant quantity. Cached generation remains accurate when it stays close to the full-denoising trajectory, with errors concentrated around unmask-order deviations and reduced as longer reference prefixes are recovered. This suggests a more direct principle: limiting representation error helps, but keeping generation on the full-denoising trajectory is a more direct route to accurate decoding. We therefore formulate efficient dLLM inference as trajectory-guided querying. Rather than broadly repairing representation error, we use full-denoising trajectories to supervise which unresolved positions should receive limited fresh computation. Based on this formulation, we introduce TRACEQUERY, a lightweight sparse decoding framework that predicts a small set of near-future trajectory-relevant positions, queries only those candidates, and lets the backbone select which one to commit from their fresh predictions. TRACEQUERY delivers the strongest quality-computation trade-off among cache-based approaches, sustaining high accuracy with substantially fewer fresh token computations, and generalizes across tasks, model variants, and families. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[The LLENS Platform for On-Premises Large Language Model Agents in a Japanese University Hospital: Development and Evaluation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32151)**  `arXiv:2610.32151`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ken Enda, Jien Saito, Masami Mukai, Feng Han, Emir Turkes, Tatsumi Kusakabe, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents that use tools could take on part of the electronic health record and desk work that occupies physicians' time, but in many hospitals the medical information systems run inside closed networks, so the model and the agent harness must also be inside the network. We designed, implemented, and deployed LLENS, a clinical support AI agent platform that runs inside the closed network of Hokkaido University Hospital on a single host with 8 NVIDIA H200 GPUs, the open-weight model Kimi K2.6 (~1 trillion parameters), and a web application rebuilt from Open WebUI. The harness has 10 components, including page image reading, full-text attachment input, a path-addressed document tree without a vector index, a network-isolated sandbox, artifact return, self-checking of generated output, subagents, and saved procedures. Kimi K2.6 was chosen from 3 open-weight candidates and scored 480/500 on the 2026 Japanese Medical Licensing Examination; it generated 119 tokens/s for a single user and served 16 concurrent requests at 908 tokens/s in total. On 180 questions over hospital documents (3 seeds each), mean accuracy was 10.7% with no documents, 81.3% with fixed retrieval (top 10 chunks of one lexical search), and 97.2% with interactive access, in which the model reads with document tools and the sandbox (15.9 percentage points above fixed retrieval, 95% CI 10.9-21.3); the difference lay in filtering/counting questions (16.7% vs 87.8%). In an automated test of 13 work scenarios (4 runs each), the expected components were triggered in all runs for 12, and 5 scenarios were run with synthetic data on the deployment host. The platform has run on the hospital network since September 14, 2026.
  </details>

- **[CulturalRiddles: A Multicultural Benchmark of Riddles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29968)**  `arXiv:2610.29968`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _My Chiffon Nguyen, Tianyi Hu, Vinod Anbalagan, Henry Gagnier, Pouya Sadeghi, Farah Abdou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLMs expand their language coverage, evaluating how well they understand and represent diverse cultures becomes increasingly important. However, many benchmarks remain English-centric, with limited cultural coverage and a focus on factual knowledge over its application in real-world cultural contexts. We propose CulturalRiddles, a benchmark of original, culturally grounded riddles created entirely by humans through a participatory community effort, spanning 62 cultures (45 countries, 51 languages). These riddles are deeply rooted in their respective cultures and require abductive and compositional reasoning, alongside detailed factual and social knowledge, language skills, and an understanding of culturally specific associations. We benchmark 28 LLM configurations using both automatic and large-scale human evaluation. Even frontier models achieve at most 22% accuracy in some communities. We publicly release all artifacts alongside an open contribution process to expand the benchmark's linguistic and cultural coverage.
  </details>

- **[LTEM: LLM-Friendly Table-Enhanced Markdown for Table Understanding and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29188)**  `arXiv:2610.29188`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Eunbin Kim, Hyeonseok Kang, Sangkeun Jung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tables in real-world documents, including scientific papers, financial reports, and statistical summaries, frequently use merged cells, multi-level headers, and sectioned layouts. Yet the lightweight formats LLMs handle best (Markdown, CSV) cannot express these structures, while expressive formats (HTML, LaTeX) impose excessive token cost and parsing complexity. To enable systematic analysis of this trade-off, we introduce a taxonomy decomposing table structure into six atomic features (F1-F6) and corresponding complexity levels (L1-L6), and construct STB (Synthetic Table Benchmark), a controlled benchmark with 1,089 tables systematically distributed across complexity levels. Using this framework, we propose LTEM (LLM-friendly Table-Enhanced Markdown), a formally specified, minimal extension of Markdown that explicitly encodes spans and hierarchical headers. Experiments across five state-of-the-art LLMs show that LTEM achieves accuracy comparable to HTML, offers a favorable accuracy-token trade-off relative to HTML and LaTeX, and produces substantially fewer invalid responses than LaTeX; for table generation, LTEM is comparable to MultiMarkdown though HTML is best. We release our taxonomy, benchmark, and format specification to support future research on LLM-table interaction.
  </details>

- **[Back-Translation Still Matters: Leveraging Monolingual Data for Low-Resource Machine Translation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18818)**  `arXiv:2610.18818`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Divyanshu Bathla, Parag Singla, Jitin Singla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained Large Language Model (LLMs) are effective on many tasks in zero-shot and few-shot settings. For machine translation of low-resource languages, the obvious options are few-shot prompting and fine-tuning on a small parallel set. We ask: what if moderate amounts of low-resource monolingual data are also available? We argue that, rather than continued pretraining, a more effective use of this data is the pre-LLM era technique of back-translation (BT). The current fine-tuned LLM translates monolingual sentences in each language, and the resulting pairs train it in the reverse direction. Updates on the parallel set are interleaved for stable learning. With 1K parallel pairs on Odia and Swahili, BT beats continued pretraining into the low-resource language by 7.7 to 16.4 XCOMET-XXL points in domain. With beam-decoded intermediate translations, it also beats a model with 10 time more parallel data. In-context examples retrieved from the parallel data further improve BT. We also analyze the source, quality and amount of monolingual text. Across five low-resource languages, with Hindi as a high-resource control, and two 3B backbones, BT improves translation into the low-resource language by 16 to 173% XCOMET-XXL over parallel-only fine-tuning. Blind human evaluation on Odia and Sanskrit agrees with the automatic metrics.
  </details>

- **[LLMs are not stochastic parrots: Evidence for meaning-mediated abstraction from conlang-like tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34187)**  `arXiv:2609.34187`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Julia Witte Zimmerman, Calla G. Beauregard, Tabia Tanzin Prama, Parisa Suchdev, Kathryn Cramer, Elisabeth Kollrack_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The strong version of the stochastic parrot argument claims that, although large language models (LLMs) may exceed rote regurgitation, they cannot move beyond statistical pattern matching into abstraction or reasoning, remaining ontologically near the lower bound of pattern reuse despite producing alluringly fluent text. We test this hypothesis using conlang-like tasks. Several LLMs are given only natural-language descriptions of fictional languages that subvert prominent superficial patterns in training data by combining statistically uncommon and unattested features. Crucially, no example outputs are given. We argue that if the models exhibit rule-following behaviour, they cannot be relying solely on superficial statistical patterns; such patterns often work against the correct output. Instead, successful performance requires representations of the constraints specified in the prompt.
