Across three complementary task families, models systematically move in the meaning-predicted direction: they distinguish prompt exposure from instructed use, alter semantic relationships in response to novel constraints, and sometimes produce exact matches to complex translation answer keys. Although performance varies across the spectrum of models used, these results provide evidence for meaning-mediated abstraction in LLMs and refute the strong stochastic parrot hypothesis. Our work shows that, under appropriate architectural and contextual constraints, statistical learning can produce meaning-mediated abstractions, although generation remains strongly constrained by superficial plausibility. We discuss implications for model development and for understanding how increasingly abstract representations may emerge from plausible-text-generation objectives.
  </details>

- **[Frozen Memory Is Not Enough: Rethinking External Memory as Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17050)**  `arXiv:2608.17050`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingyuan Li, Guangsheng Yu, Xu Wang, Shaoxiong Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Methods for improving knowledge use in large language models typically fall into two regimes. Non-parametric retrieval offers flexible access to external knowledge, but adds retrieval latency, context overhead, and only shallow integration with the backbone. Parametric adaptation is efficient at inference time, but entangles knowledge with model weights and can be hard to update, audit, or transfer. Engram-style hashed memory occupies a middle regime: it stores learned information in an external, addressable table, yet consumes that table through a small learned reader. This raises a basic question: when such a memory is moved across backbones, what matters more, the frozen memory itself or the target-side reader? We study this question through cross-model frozen-memory extraction, in which a memory trained on a source model is frozen and attached to a different target model, with only a lightweight reader trained. Ablations show that learned memory content and correct addressing both matter, while the amount of usable signal depends on how well the reader is aligned to the target model. In downstream question answering tasks, a dual-layer, four-branch reader nearly closes the gap between same-model and cross-model reuse, achieving an average score of 38.8 under our controlled evaluation protocol. Moreover, when the provider reader is directly compatible with the target interface, the frozen artifact can provide substantial utility without target-side training, while optional reader adaptation yields further improvement. These results suggest that Engram can serve as a reusable external knowledge artifact, provided that the target has access to a compatible reader interface; target-side adaptation can further improve alignment when direct reader reuse is insufficient.
  </details>

- **[From Atomic Evidence to Logical Composition: Structured Compositional Reasoning over Compound Answer Options](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12836)**  `arXiv:2608.12836`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Obed Junias, Maria Leonor Pacheco_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often fail when answer options require combining atomic judgments under explicit logical operators, even when they judge the individual atoms correctly. We study compound options connected by AND, OR, and NEITHER/NOR, introducing a framework that decomposes each option into atomic answers and scores contrastive hypotheses about each one, so the model never sees a compound option. An operator-constrained integer linear program then composes the calibrated scores into a single prediction. We evaluate on LOGICAL-COMMONSENSEQA and introduce LOGICAL-SATA, a reading-comprehension benchmark derived from SATA-Bench. Our framework improves Macro-F1 from 48.3 to 77.0 on the human-validated LOGICAL-COMMONSENSEQA split and from 47.6 to 75.6 on LOGICAL-SATA, with the largest gains on NEITHER/NOR.
  </details>

- **[SURGELLM: Rethinking Multi-Task Evaluation through Task-Aware Feature Gating with Class-Balanced Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24259)**  `arXiv:2606.24259`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Noor Islam S. Mohammad, Ulug Bayazit_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuned encoders deployed across heterogeneous NLP tasks face three compounding problems: mismatched inductive biases, class-imbalance corruption of feature statistics, and no mechanism to condition attention on external lexical knowledge. We introduce \textbf{\surgellm}, a unified transformer framework that addresses each with a dedicated lightweight module: a \emph{surgical feature gate} (learned per-dimension sigmoid over curated lexical indicators and \texttt{[CLS]}; provably degenerates to identity when features are uninformative), \emph{task-conditioned prefix tokens} (quantized feature values and task identity prepended to every input), and \emph{Instance-Weighted Normalization} (IWN; removes class-prior bias from gate statistics). We prove an excess-risk bound linking gate benefit to \emph{surgical feature alignment}. Across four tasks, SST-2, multi-hop retrieval, LLM-prompt attribution, and authorship detection, covering 17,830 examples and eleven model variants over three seeds, the IWN variant achieves macro-F1 \textbf{0.940} ($+0.036$ over the strongest non-IWN baseline; $+0.130$ on authorship detection). A random-vocabulary control ($-0.028$ avg.\ F1) confirms gains are lexical, not parametric. Code, vocabularies, and a $99.5\%$-recovery auto-extraction recipe are released.
  </details>

- **[Learning Faithful Reasoning Processes with Spatio-Temporal Latent Dynamics for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26893)**  `arXiv:2605.26893`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Weijiang Lv, Wentong Zhao, Jiayu Wang, Yuhao Wu, Jiaheng Wei, Xiaobo Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly enhanced the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, existing reinforcement learning methods primarily optimize outcome-level rewards, providing limited supervision over the reasoning process. As a result, models may produce shortcut-prone or unstable reasoning trajectories even when the final answer is correct. We propose GeoFaith, a spatio-temporal latent dynamics framework for learning faithful reasoning processes without process reward models. GeoFaith combines final-state latent uncertainty, which measures whether a completed trajectory lies in a well-supported manifold region, with predictive-entropy dynamics, which penalize abnormal uncertainty patterns such as premature collapse, oscillation, and prolonged uncertainty. These signals are incorporated as process regularization into GRPO, jointly optimizing answer correctness and reasoning quality without step-level process annotations. Experiments show that GeoFaith consistently improves reasoning accuracy, produces more efficient reasoning trajectories, and outperforms both outcome-only reinforcement learning and process-supervision baselines.
  </details>

- **[Repetition over Diversity: High-Signal Data Filtering for Sample-Efficient German Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.28075)**  `arXiv:2604.28075`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ansar Aynetdinov, Patrick Haller, Alan Akbik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent research has shown that filtering massive English web corpora into high-quality subsets significantly improves training efficiency. However, for high-resource non-English languages like German, French, or Japanese, aggressive filtering creates a strategic dilemma: should practitioners prioritize diversity by training once on large amounts of lightly filtered web data, or prioritize quality by strictly filtering for a high-quality core and repeating it over multiple epochs? We investigate this trade-off for German by applying hierarchical quality filters to 500M web documents, comparing multi-epoch training on filtered subsets against single-pass training on a diverse corpus. Our experiments across multiple model scales and token budgets show that repeating high-quality data consistently outperforms single-pass training on larger, less filtered sets. Notably, the performance gap persists even after 7 epochs. Our findings suggest that for non-English LLMs, semantic concentration through quality filtering offers a more viable path to efficient language modeling than simply maximizing unique data volume. We release our German language models (called Boldt), as well as our cleaned evaluation benchmarks to the research community. Our experiments indicate that they achieve state-of-the-art results despite training on 10-360x fewer tokens than comparable models.
  </details>

- **[LinearARD: Linear-Memory Attention Distillation for RoPE Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00004)**  `arXiv:2604.00004`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ning Yang, Hengyu Zhong, Wentao Wang, Baoliang Tian, Yuan Zhou, Haijun Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The extension of context windows in Large Language Models is typically facilitated by scaling positional encodings followed by Continued Pre-Training (CPT). While effective, this paradigm is notoriously data-hungry and computationally expensive, requiring massive long-text corpora to recalibrate the model to the shifted positional distribution. We propose LinearARD, a restoration distillation method that restores Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE)-scaled students through attention-structure consistency with a frozen native-RoPE teacher. Rather than next-token prediction or opaque hidden-state matching, LinearARD aligns row-wise distributions of dense $Q/Q$, $K/K$, and $V/V$ self-relation matrices from the final attention layer to directly supervise attention dynamics. To remove the quadratic memory bottleneck of $n \times n$ relation maps, we introduce a linear-activation-memory kernel that stores only per-token log-sum-exp statistics and recomputes logits in the backward pass to obtain exact Kullback-Leibler divergence gradients. Across LLaMA2-7B, LLaMA3-8B, and Mistral-7B-v0.1 extended to 32K context, LinearARD recovers 93.1%/94.2%/94.3% of native short-context performance and achieves strong long-context robustness on RULER using only 4.25M tokens---amounting to just 1.6% (a $\sim$60$\times$ reduction) of the 256M-token budget required by state-of-the-art baselines. Under this severely constrained budget, CPT and LongReD remain near zero on RULER, demonstrating that relation-level restoration provides a substantially better efficiency-quality tradeoff. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do Latent Tokens Think? A Causal and Adversarial Analysis of Chain-of-Continuous-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21711)**  `arXiv:2512.21711`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuyi Zhang, Boyu Tang, Tianjie Ju, Sufeng Duan, Gongshen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent tokens are gaining attention for enhancing reasoning in large language models (LLMs), yet their internal mechanisms remain unclear. This paper examines the problem from a reliability perspective, uncovering fundamental weaknesses: latent tokens function as uninterpretable placeholders rather than encoding faithful reasoning. While resistant to perturbation, they promote shortcut usage over genuine reasoning. We focus on Chain-of-Continuous-Thought (COCONUT), which claims better efficiency and stability than explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) while maintaining performance. We investigate this through two complementary approaches. First, steering experiments perturb specific token subsets, namely COCONUT and explicit CoT. Unlike CoT tokens, COCONUT tokens show minimal sensitivity to steering and lack reasoning-critical information. Second, shortcut experiments evaluate models under biased and out-of-distribution settings. Results on MMLU and HotpotQA demonstrate that COCONUT consistently exploits dataset artifacts, inflating benchmark performance without true reasoning. These findings reposition COCONUT as a pseudo-reasoning mechanism: it generates plausible traces that conceal shortcut dependence rather than faithfully representing reasoning processes.
  </details>

- **[Think Parallax: Solving Multi-Hop Problems via Multi-View Knowledge-Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.15552)**  `arXiv:2510.15552`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinliang Liu, Jiale Bai, Shaoning Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) still struggle with multi-hop reasoning over knowledge-graphs (KGs), and we identify a previously overlooked structural reason for this difficulty: Transformer attention heads naturally specialize in distinct semantic relations across reasoning stages, forming a hop-aligned relay pattern. This key finding suggests that multi-hop reasoning is inherently multi-view, yet existing KG-based retrieval-augmented generation (KG-RAG) systems collapse all reasoning hops into a single representation, flat embedding space, suppressing this implicit structure and causing noisy or drifted path exploration. We introduce ParallaxRAG, a symmetric multi-view framework that decouples queries and KGs into aligned, head-specific semantic spaces. By enforcing relational diversity across multiple heads while constraining weakly related paths, ParallaxRAG constructs more accurate, cleaner subgraphs and guides LLMs through grounded, hop-wise reasoning. On WebQSP and CWQ, it achieves state-of-the-art retrieval and QA performance, substantially reduces hallucination, and generalizes strongly to the biomedical BioASQ benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Alignment: Block-Level LoRA-DPO for Preference Tuning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12044)**  `arXiv:2510.12044`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yishen Chen, Qi Dong, Yukun Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference tuning typically distributes adapter updates across every Transformer layer. We study how the placement of a fixed LoRA budget affects Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). Hierarchical Alignment partitions decoder depth into Local, Intermediate, and Global regions and restricts LoRA updates to a chosen contiguous block. Across Qwen and Llama models, three-seed sliding-window sweeps reveal broad low-loss basins around intermediate depth. Under exact trainableparameter matching, Middle achieves lower held-out DPO loss than an all-depth allocation on Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. Generationlevel evidence is more variable. LLM judges and a blinded human study leave Selected versus All unresolved on Qwen; on Llama, two pairwise judges favor All while the human interval includes zero. Trained-adapter interventions show that learned depth components affect the optimization objective; removing the Llama Selected window also lowers generation quality. Across Qwen2.5 at 3B, 7B, and 14B, Middle versus All remains unresolved under Qwen3-Max; at 3B and 14B, both improve over Base. In our implementation, Middle also uses less peak memory and takes shorter steps than a parameter-matched all-depth allocation. Together, the results support intermediate depth as a practical starting point for fixed-budget preference tuning, though the best window and its behavioral value still need per-setting validation.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating LLM-based Agents for Multi-Turn Conversations: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22458)**  `arXiv:2503.22458`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shengyue Guan, Jindong Wang, Jiang Bian, Bin Zhu, Jian-guang Lou, Haoyi Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This survey examines evaluation methods for large language model (LLM)-based agents in multi-turn conversational settings. Using a PRISMA-inspired framework, we systematically reviewed nearly 250 scholarly sources, capturing the state of the art from various venues of publication, and establishing a solid foundation for our analysis. Our study offers a structured approach by developing two interrelated taxonomy systems: one that defines \emph{what to evaluate} and another that explains \emph{how to evaluate}. The first taxonomy identifies key components of LLM-based agents for multi-turn conversations and their evaluation dimensions, including task completion, response quality, user experience, memory and context retention, as well as planning and tool integration. These components ensure that the performance of conversational agents is assessed in a holistic and meaningful manner. The second taxonomy system focuses on the evaluation methodologies. It categorizes approaches into annotation-based evaluations, automated metrics, hybrid strategies that combine human assessments with quantitative measures, and self-judging methods utilizing LLMs. This framework not only captures traditional metrics derived from language understanding, such as BLEU and ROUGE scores, but also incorporates advanced techniques that reflect the dynamic, interactive nature of multi-turn dialogues.
  </details>

- **[Preserve to Expand: Source-Aware Adaptation and Model Merging for Multilingual LLM-Based ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35846)**  `arXiv:2610.35846`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Haralambos Mouratidis, Ravi Shekhar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expanding multilingual ASR systems to new languages is challenging when adaptation must preserve previously supported languages without replaying source-language training data. We study this problem in connector-based Speech LLMs, where a trainable connector maps speech representations into a frozen large language model. We evaluate target-update construction and merging strategies for adapting the connector while preserving source-language performance. In single-target expansion, target-only fine-tuning and standard TIES merging learn new languages but cause severe source-language forgetting, while SSU-Wanda+TIES gives the best source-preserving trade-off. In multi-target expansion, update merging provides stable source retention, whereas recursive expansion can improve target adaptation under favorable orders but is highly order-sensitive. These results show that multilingual ASR expansion requires balancing target acquisition, source retention, and interactions among target-language updates.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Jev-Style System One Models: Does Structure Reduce Selection Bias?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35826)**  `arXiv:2610.35826`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng, Zhiyuan Ma, Xinpeng Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selection bias in large language models (LLMs) can persist despite instructions to ignore presentation order or prompts that elicit reasoning. Recent efforts to reproduce Jev-style System One behavior have produced a growing family of models that predict probabilities directly over supplied candidates. Yet systematic comparisons of selection bias across Jev versions, Jev reproductions, and generative LLMs remain limited in multi-option tasks. This leaves a central question: does Jev-style decision structure reduce selection bias? We address this question with a simple test that shuffles options while keeping question and candidate content fixed. Across two Jev versions, twelve Jev reproductions, and six generative LLMs, we trace individual answers through eight presentations and compare them with fixed-order repetition. On our primary collection of 1,700 questions from two benchmarks, Jev's accuracy varies by less than two percentage points, yet its cumulative failure set expands by 27--29\% relative to the original order, with first failures continuing into the final presentation. This loss of initial successes is substantially larger than under fixed-order repetition, even for highly repeatable Jev reproductions. Nor does Jev show a general advantage over the LLM baselines in preserving correct choices. Aggregation offers limited protection: voting yields modest gains for Jev, while even unanimous answers are frequently wrong. These results expose a gap between repeatable decisions and decisions that remain correct across equivalent presentations, showing why stable accuracy alone cannot establish resistance to selection bias.
  </details>

- **[Quantifying Claim Smoothing by LLM Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35793)**  `arXiv:2610.35793`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tiasa Singha Roy, Manya Wadhwa, Greg Durrett_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research, or any use of large language models (LLMs) to read a large document collection and produce insights, requires synthesizing information from different sources. In this work, we find that LLMs produce long-form research reports that read as confident syntheses but which fail to accurately reflect the underlying documents. We call this failure smoothing. In collapsing diverse and potentially conflicting sources into a single narrative, the model discards the evidential "jaggedness" of its corpus and asserts conclusions more decisive than the citations can support. We introduce a claim level scoring pipeline, SmoothScore, to quantify this effect. Our pipeline extracts key conclusive claims from model generated reports, then finds evidence relevant to each claim and clusters that evidence along high level aspects. Finally, each claim is scored with respect to the evidence to quantify the degree of smoothing done by the agent. Across 7 systems and 4 datasets, we find that agents consistently produce stronger claims than warranted.
  </details>

- **[SEMA-Auto: Sighted and Efficient Multi-Turn Attack with Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35743)**  `arXiv:2610.35743`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingqian Feng, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Chenliang Xu, Jialin Song, Qiyao Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing jailbreak attackers fall into two classes: static planners fix their single- or multi-turn adversarial plans in advance, remaining simple but losing sight of the victim's replies, wasting turns and limiting transferability; interactive generators read each victim reply to generate the next-turn attack but require in-loop evaluators for success signals or hints, incurring substantial costs and inflated performance. Bridging this gap, we propose SEMA-Auto, a sighted and efficient multi-turn attacker with autonomy, interacting with the victim model and terminating the session by itself upon success. To stabilize its behaviors and boost its capabilities, SEMA-Auto is trained in two stages. Synthetic bootstrap tuning installs the notion of attack targets and conforming decisions by fine-tuning the base model with bootstrapped trajectories. Decision-supervised reinforcement learning then optimizes two objectives jointly yet separately, reinforcing the attack with higher jailbreak reward while supervising the decision at each turn against an absolute target. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) attack success rates (ASR) across multiple datasets, victims, and judges, outperforming all static and interactive baselines, within almost the fewest turns, demonstrating both effectiveness and efficiency. For example, SEMA-Auto performs a $76.0\%$ ASR@1 within an average of $3.0$ turns against DeepSeek-V4-Flash on AdvBench, while the best static baseline is $54.8\%$ with fixed $7$ turns and the best interactive baseline is $43.8\%$ in $3.7$ turns. Against safety-hardened frontier models, GPT-oss-120B, GPT-5.4, Claude-Haiku-4.5, and GPT-6-Astra, our counter-trained attacker achieves an average $59.6\%$ ASR@1 on HarmBench, $13.8\%$ over prior SOTA. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[From Self-Confidence to Statistical Accuracy: Scaling Human-in-the-Loop Evaluation with Direct and LLM-Assisted Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35698)**  `arXiv:2610.35698`  `cs.CL`  
  _Harine Choi, Chenchen Kuai, Zihao Li, Jiwan Jiang, Yunlong Zhang, Zhiwen Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For large language models (LLMs) and multimodal large language models (MLLMs), model-reported self-confidence is often miscalibrated, yet it may still be useful for efficient evaluation. At the same time, LLM-as-a-judge offers a scalable alternative to direct human auditing, but its practical benefit depends on calibration quality and inference cost.This paper develops a unified statistical framework for both evaluation routes under alternative sampling strategies. For direct auditing, confidence-stratified sampling is used to recover population accuracy from partially verified outputs without requiring calibrated confidence. Finite-sample uncertainty is characterized, and the analysis shows how highly uneven confidence strata affect audit efficiency and label allocation. For judge-assisted evaluation, the judge is modeled through sensitivity and false-positive rate, yielding an error-corrected estimator with calibration uncertainty. The two routes are then compared through human-label and total-cost efficiency boundaries.Experiments on MMLU-Pro and MMMU-Pro show that miscalibrated self-confidence can still support statistically valid stratified auditing. Under highly imbalanced confidence distributions, proportional allocation substantially reduces estimation error relative to equal-per-stratum allocation. For judge-assisted evaluation, only a small subset of target--verifier pairs satisfies the idealized human-label efficiency condition, and automated inference costs can further limit potential savings. These findings clarify the statistical and economic conditions under which confidence-guided auditing and automated verification are useful.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017 for loss of control and harmful manipulation risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35671)**  `arXiv:2610.35671`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yao Huang, Zhiyuan Zeng, Yu Wang, Zhitong Zhu, Dingcheng Duan, Hao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As frontier language models grow increasingly capable and autonomous, independent third-party evaluations are critical for characterizing their systemic safety risks. While closed-source releases are routinely accompanied by comprehensive system cards, open-weight models receive less scrutiny regarding frontier risks. To address this gap, we present an independent safety evaluation of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017, the latest version of V4.1 Flash that departs from V4 with a new architecture, scaled-up training, and strong agentic capabilities. Drawing on the EU General-Purpose AI Code of Practice, we evaluate the model across 34 benchmarks, including 5 in-house suites, spanning evaluation integrity, loss of control, and harmful manipulation. Conforming to the AEF-1 standard, we compare V4.1 Flash-1017 against V4.1 Flash, earlier DeepSeek V4 models, and leading closed-source systems (GPT-6 Astra, GPT-5.6 Sol, and Gemini 3.8 Flash). Overall, V4.1 Flash-1017 shows substantial safety improvements over the V4 generation and approaches or matches leading closed-source baselines on several dimensions, while still exhibiting notable behaviors that warrant continued attention. Building on our findings, we highlight three key observations and recommendations. First, because safety can shift and even regress across minor updates, developers should integrate safety evaluation into incremental releases. Second, as final-turn outputs and end-state checks alone cannot reliably detect loss-of-control behaviors, deployments require process-level observability across both reasoning traces and action logs. Third, because public benchmarks elicit strong evaluation awareness and face data contamination risks, developing private benchmarks is essential for trustworthy frontier evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Skill Evolution for Coding Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35616)**  `arXiv:2610.35616`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jun Gao, Yun Peng, Changhai Zhou, Yuhan Zhu, Xiangyu Zeng, Shiyang Zhang Yuhua Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skills provide agents with reusable experience to guide future decisions without modifying the underlying model. However, coding trajectories are often entangled with task- and repository-specific contexts, making it challenging to distill procedural skills that generalize across diverse coding environments. To address this challenge, we present Hierarchical Skill Evolution (HSE), which progressively distills procedural knowledge from coding trajectories through hierarchical evidence aggregation. HSE first extracts task-level evidence from individual trajectories, then aggregates recurring evidence within the same repository to form repository-specific skill candidates, and finally consolidates consistent patterns across repositories into transferable skills across repositories. The evolution is driven by an external writer-evaluator loop that generates and assesses skills while keeping the underlying coding model frozen. The loop externally updates the writer and evaluator policies to improve subsequent skill generation and assessment. This hierarchy progressively filters task-specific details while preserving procedures that generalize across diverse coding environments. Successful trajectories yield reusable guidance for future tasks, while failed trajectories reveal recurring failure modes that guide avoidance and recovery. We evaluate HSE on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE, using GLM-5.2 across Pi and Claude Code harnesses and GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8 with Pi. Across both benchmarks, 10 skills evolved solely from SWE-Gym improve coding performance across all evaluated settings, demonstrating transfer across benchmarks, coding models, and agent harnesses. We further evolve the skill library from target-benchmark trajectories without verifier feedback, relying solely on the learned evaluator for candidate assessment and achieving additional gains through benchmark-tailored skills.
  </details>

- **[MATCHA: A Chinese Multi-Disorder Mental Health Dataset with Cross-Lingual Analyses on Social Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35512)**  `arXiv:2610.35512`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bingrui Jin, Baihan Li, Zitong Li, Kunyao Lan, Shimeng Li, Kenny Q. Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mental health research on social media has been dominated by English-language datasets and DSM-5-centered resources, limiting cross-lingual and cross-cultural understanding of online mental health expression. We introduce MATCHA, the first Chinese multi-disorder mental health dataset that links disorder-specific Weibo Super-Topic communities with users' broader public timelines. MATCHA covers seven DSM-5 disorders and a control group, comprising 46,025 cohort-level users and 1.8 million posts. The dataset distinguishes users with explicit self-disclosure evidence from broader disorder-related participants through an LLM-assisted annotation pipeline, and is released in a rigorously de-identified, controlled-access format. Using MATCHA, we analyze behavioral, linguistic, symptom-related, and life-event-related patterns across disorders, and conduct cross-lingual comparisons with Twitter-STMHD. Our analyses show that Weibo Super-Topics function as concentrated spaces for affective and health-related expression, while cross-platform differences between Weibo and Twitter often exceed cross-disorder variation. MATCHA provides a new resource for community-aware and cross-lingual mental health NLP research.
  </details>

- **[Probing the phon* representations of the Mimi codebooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35432)**  `arXiv:2610.35432`  `cs.CL`  
  _Artem Saloev, Nicolas Ballier, Erin Pacquetet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural audio codecs are increasingly used to encode speech for integration with large language models. This paper investigates the phonetic representations in the first codebook (CB0) of the Mimi codec. ABX style evaluation is poorly suited to Mimi fixed 80 ms tokenization window, which often spans multiple phonemes; we instead use forced alignment, entropy analysis, and Phone-Normalized Mutual Information (PNMI). Using TIMIT speech (58/55 speakers, SA1/SA2), individual tokens are strongly associated with specific phonemes (PNMI = 0.84, above random), yet phonemes are realized by multiple competing tokens rather than a one-to-one mapping.
  </details>

- **[INVAR-Agri: Detecting Discrepancies in Multilingual Operator Manuals of Agricultural Implements with a Fine-Tuned LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35423)**  `arXiv:2610.35423`  `cs.CL`  
  _Timo Oksanen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Operator manuals of agricultural implements are delivered in many languages, and the standards require that competent persons review every translation, which makes this review the bottleneck. This paper presents a proof of concept that compares two training workflows on the same LLM, EuroLLM-1.7B-Instruct, trained on operator manuals of one manufacturer. In approach A, a back-translator translates each language version back into English, and an English reader compares it with the English version. In approach B, a checker reads the English version and the translation together, where the checker reports differences. The test sets contain errors planted in numbers, units, opposite words, obligations, negations, and conditions in 24 languages, and they include unmodified text and meaning-preserving edits for alerts and false alarms. The trained back-translator concealed many planted errors, most of all weakened obligations, while translation-quality scores rose. In contrast, the separately trained checker detected most planted errors, although it was weak for units and for number changes unlike those in training. A check by the author confirmed the concealment. In addition, a simple rule check caught the number and unit changes, but it alerted on harmless rewrites. A check of alerts on real Finnish translations showed that most alerts were false, caused by language-specific wording, so the precision on real text remains a limitation. Within this setting, a trained checker might support, not replace, the review by competent persons, and firmer conclusions need more data.
  </details>

- **[ImgMT: Wikipedia-based Dataset For Evaluating Text-in-Image Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35416)**  `arXiv:2610.35416`  `cs.CL`  
  _Šárka Uramová, Petar Kirilov Petrov, Josef Jon, Ondřej Bojar, Michal Novák_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-in-image translation aims to translate all textual content embedded in an image. In Text Image Translation (TIT), the output is translated text; In-Image Machine Translation (IIMT) additionally renders the translation back into the image. We introduce ImgMT, a massively multilingual dataset and benchmark mined from localized SVG illustrations on Wikimedia Commons, focusing on diagrams, maps, and infographics. ImgMT contains 35,612 aligned image pairs grouped into 1,855 image sets, spanning 129 languages, 20+ writing systems, and 4,886 language pairs.We propose a multi-level evaluation protocol that measures source-side text-region detection and OCR accuracy alongside target-side translation quality. We benchmark several multimodal large language models and a dedicated TIT system in English->X and X->English settings, and find that the source language and writing system substantially affect TIT quality. Finally, we show that ImgMT is not well suited for isolating the contribution of visual information in multimodal translation.
  </details>

- **[MechAlign: Evaluating Semantic Alignment at the Project Level in Mathematical Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35269)**  `arXiv:2610.35269`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Shen, Ruichen Qiu, Lihong Zhi, Xiao-Shan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lean verifies that a formal proof establishes its stated proposition under declared assumptions, but does not check whether that proposition faithfully expresses the mathematical claim in a manuscript. Assessing this correspondence requires interpreting the main theorem through its project dependencies, which may be scattered throughout a large development. We introduce MechAlign, a framework for project-level semantic alignment review that combines formal dependency extraction with LLM-based assessment. Its extraction tool, LeanTrace, constructs a statement dependency graph from elaborated declarations, retaining definition bodies and structure constraints while excluding proof bodies and stopping at an explicit library boundary. The reviewer uses this context to investigate alignment and report discrepancies with localized evidence. We conduct a preliminary review of 201 main results from 173 OpenAI Math projects, using supplied informal descriptions and available paper context. A hyperbolic-group case illustrates how dependency inspection localizes a missing dimensional constraint relative to the manuscript, consistent with the upstream declaration of formalization scope. We also construct LeanReviewBench, comprising 501 fixed claim-statement-context triples from OpenAI Math, Fermat's Last Theorem, and Kakeya, with description provenance recorded. The resource supports controlled comparisons of more project-level semantic alignment evaluations.
  </details>

- **[The Lay of the Land in Idiom-Translation Quality Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35261)**  `arXiv:2610.35261`  `cs.CL`  
  _Malik Marmonier, Benoît Sagot, Rachel Bawden_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Idioms have long been a challenge for machine translation, and while large language models (LLMs) have proven increasingly adept at handling them, whether quality estimation (QE) systems have kept pace and can reliably score idiomatic renderings of these expressions above faulty literal ones remains unclear. We introduce the first parallel dataset pairing idiom-bearing English sentences with both literal and idiomatic translations across nine target languages, augmented into multi-sentence segments and a graded literality continuum. Using this dataset, we benchmark eleven conventional QE metrics from the COMET and MetricX families and ten LLMs from the Qwen-3, Gemma-4 and OLMo-3 families used as GEMBA-style judges. We find that most systems, especially older metrics and smaller models, display a marked literality bias, rating literal translations of idioms above idiomatic ones. Higher parameter counts and newer training sets appear to reduce this bias to an extent; nevertheless, of all the benchmarked systems, only MetricX-24-Hybrid (XL, XXL) and Gemma-4-31B consistently rate idiomatic translations of idioms above literal ones, and only Gemma-4-31B reliably ranks intermediate degrees of idiomaticity and literality. Our findings caution against deploying standard QE metrics in domains rich in figurative language, where higher scores might be rewarding undesirable literalism.
  </details>

- **[Style over Substance? Linguistic Bias in LLM-based Peer Review Copy title](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35254)**  `arXiv:2610.35254`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sai Suresh Macharla Vasu, Sumedh Santosh Joshi, Philipp Christmann, Ivaxi Sheth, Ruta Binkyte, Koel Dutta Chowdhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The number of paper submissions to major AI conferences has increased dramatically in recent years, substantially increasing the peer-review workload of researchers. At the same time, large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support peer review, both by individual reviewers and by conference organizations. While LLM capabilities have improved considerably, their evaluations may be influenced by linguistic characteristics that are unrelated to scientific content. In this work, we introduce a controlled experimental framework for studying such effects by systematically perturbing the linguistic style of research papers. Specifically, we rewrite papers to exhibit (i) linguistic features associated with native or non-native English writing, and (ii) high- or low-confidence linguistic features. To construct these interventions, we derive fine-grained rewriting instructions grounded in the linguistic literature and apply them using LLMs. Crucially, our interventions are designed to preserve the underlying scientific content. We then evaluate the resulting papers using different LLM reviewers. Our experiments expose a critical vulnerability in LLM-based peer review: reviewers systematically reward confident rhetoric independent of scientific content, raising serious concerns about the robustness of their assessments.
  </details>

- **[CalibraRL: Confidence-Calibrated Reinforcement Learning for Mitigating Hallucinations in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35142)**  `arXiv:2610.35142`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qianchi Zhang, Hainan Zhang, Liang Pang, Hongwei Zheng, Zhiming Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often produce plausible but incorrect answers, motivating reinforcement learning methods that reward correct answers, penalize incorrect answers, and allow abstention. However, fixed outcome-based rewards do not distinguish incorrect responses with different confidence levels or account for differences in answering reliability across queries. Consequently, such rewards may inadequately penalize overconfident errors and fail to discourage unnecessary abstention. We propose CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework that incorporates response confidence and query-level answering evidence into reward design. For non-abstaining responses, CalibraRL combines correctness-based utility with a Brier-based penalty that encourages agreement between confidence and correctness. For abstentions, it estimates conditional answering reliability using a Beta posterior derived from the correctness of non-abstaining responses within each sampled group. The abstention reward increases with the posterior probability that the model's accuracy on non-abstaining responses is below a predefined threshold. CalibraRL reuses training rollouts without requiring additional annotations indicating whether the model knows the answer to each question. Experiments on three QA datasets demonstrate that CalibraRL improves answer precision and reduces hallucinations compared with strong baselines. Further analysis shows that CalibraRL reduces mean expected calibration error (ECE) by 30.8\% across two backbone models and three datasets, highlighting its potential to improve the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in web search.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Evaluation for Principled Generalization Using a Kripkean Challenge to Meaning as Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35111)**  `arXiv:2610.35111`  `cs.CL`  
  _Niyar R Barman, Omar Naim, Nicholas Asher_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models operationalize an intuitive, view of meaning as use. We argue that it faces a rule-following challenge. Using Kripke's interpretation of Wittgenstein, we argue that finite histories of use do not determine a unique rule for future extension. This reframes the debate about OOD generalization but also about NLP evaluation; benchmarks that measure performance on fixed use cases or local robustness, we argue, do not by themselves establish semantic competence. To evaluate whether models preserve rules and concepts under principled domain extension, we propose to evaluate two capacities, central to this ability: abstraction and recursion; and we offer an experimental paradigm for testing both.
  </details>

- **[RAGing Against Misogyny: Improving Implicit Misogyny Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35094)**  `arXiv:2610.35094`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ivana Crescenzi, Arianna Muti, Maarten Sap, Tommaso Caselli, Debora Nozza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Implicit misogyny often requires background knowledge to be understood. To address this challenge, we propose a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework for detecting and explaining implicit misogyny in English and Italian, leveraging encyclopedic, slang and feminist knowledge sources. Using the ImplicIT-Mis and SBIC+ datasets, we evaluate the contribution of each knowledge source to detection and explanation quality, investigating also whether RAG addresses known LLM failure modes in implicit misogyny. Results show that RAG improves recall on ImplicIT-Mis, but does not improve detection performance on SBIC+. However, human evaluation shows that RAG-based explanations are preferred over no-RAG explanations in both languages. Feminist literature emerges as the most consistently useful source for detection and explanation.
  </details>

- **[Ask Before You Answer: A Question-Driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture (QERA) for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35083)**  `arXiv:2610.35083`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinhu Qi, Minda Hu, Wentao Zhang, Zixing Song, Irwin King_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI assistants are increasingly asked to reason over a user's own web traces: group chats, community threads, activity logs and revision histories. On CL-bench Life, a benchmark of such real-life context learning, frontier LLMs solve fewer than one task in five. We find that the request is not the specification: over 80% of the benchmark's rubrics share less than 60% of their content words with the final request, as for a rule set two turns earlier or every record that meets the user's criterion, and LLMs answering directly pass only 67% of these implicit rubrics against 81% of the others. We propose QERA, a Question-driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture that asks before it answers. QERA first turns the request into a requirement checklist and typed information needs, filtered by code for faithfulness to the user's words; it then answers from the whole conversation, asks the same questions again with quotes verified by code, and applies a guarded local repair. It needs no training or rubrics and makes about four LLM calls per task. On all 405 tasks with four frontier LLMs, QERA attains the highest solving rate on every LLM (21.0% on average, against 18.2% for the strongest of three inference-time baselines and 14.6% for direct answering) and leads every baseline significantly when pooled across LLMs, while using fewer tokens than PSCI and 4-5% of the tokens of Ctx2Skill. Ablations on GPT-6-Luna show that the gain is settled before the answer, by a fixed answer protocol (+3.8 rubric points) and the request-specific questions (+1.6), and that it is concentrated on implicit rubrics, with checking afterwards refining mainly numeric requirements.
  </details>

- **[SimPOTrans: Self-Adaptation for Offline-to-Simultaneous Speech Translation via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35042)**  `arXiv:2610.35042`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haotian Tan, Hiroki Ouchi, Sakriani Sakti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) aims to translate ongoing speech with low latency while preserving translation quality. Recent studies have adopted large language models (LLMs) for SimulST and formulated it as a multi-turn dialogue task for efficient generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance. However, existing dialogue-style SimulST approaches typically rely on specialized training schemes to acquire simultaneous translation behavior, either using synthesized read/write trajectories, which may provide suboptimal training signals, or jointly optimizing translation quality and latency, whose competing objectives may cause optimization instability. In this paper, we introduce SimPOTrans, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that enables offline speech translation models to self-adapt to dialogue-style SimulST. Through a simple two-stage RL post-training pipeline, SimPOTrans rewards an offline model for exhibiting translation behaviors beneficial to simultaneous scenarios when given partial speech inputs, thereby enabling offline-to-simultaneous self-adaptation without synthesized read/write trajectories or explicit latency optimization. At inference time, SimPOTrans requires no external decision policy and enables flexible quality--latency control by simply adjusting the chunk size of speech input. Experiments on the ACL 60/60 and MCIF benchmarks across three language directions (En$\rightarrow$De, En$\rightarrow$Zh, and En$\rightarrow$Ja) demonstrate that SimPOTrans outperforms several strong baselines, achieving a superior quality--latency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[FATE-5: Faithful Alignment of Trait Expression for Continuous Big Five Personality Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34889)**  `arXiv:2610.34889`  `cs.CL`  
  _Gunhee Cho, Hongyeob Kim, Yun-Gyung Cheong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Controlling personality in large language models is important for building conversational agents that can be customized to different users and interaction settings. However, existing personality control methods often face a tradeoff between strong trait expression and natural dialogue generation. We introduce FATE-5, Faithful Alignment of Trait Expression for the Big Five, a two-stage framework that separates personality encoding from naturalness alignment. FATE-5 first learns continuous soft prompt representations of Big Five profiles using dialogue supervision and an auxiliary survey objective. It then improves dialogue naturalness through preference alignment based on both dialogue quality and personality agreement. Across three language model backbones, FATE-5 maintains strong trait expression while achieving substantially higher dialogue naturalness than other trait-strong methods. On held-out questionnaires, it achieves monotonicity correlations of 0.97-0.99 and recovery RMSE of 8.9-12.4, substantially improving over the evaluated baselines. These results demonstrate effective continuous personality control while maintaining natural conversational behavior.
  </details>

- **[RubriX: A Fast, Fine-Tunable Non-Autoregressive Rubric Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34875)**  `arXiv:2610.34875`  `cs.CL`  
  _Balamurugan Paramasivam, Sudalai Rajkumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based evaluation asks which level of a rubric a response meets. It is usually done by prompting a large language model (LLM) as a judge, which needs a generative call per judgment and reads every level description, so its cost grows with the rubric. We present RubriX, a rubric judge whose architecture is inspired by contrastive language models (CLM): a LoRA-adapted encoder embeds the input and each level description, and a softmax over scaled cosine similarities gives a probability for every level without text generation. Trained on public rubric data and proprietary synthetic data, RubriX reaches 75.4% exact accuracy on held-out rubrics, against 57.1% for TypeSafe's Jev and 49.8% for a Qwen3-8B judge. On eight human-labelled benchmark sets it is within 6 percentage points of Jev's accuracy on most of them, while Jev keeps a larger lead on two and ranks responses best on average; against the results reported in the G-Eval paper, RubriX outperforms G-Eval with GPT-3.5 on every SummEval dimension and both QAGS subsets. With level embeddings cached, RubriX's latency stays at about 28 ms per judgment as level descriptions grow from 10 to 3,000 tokens, while Jev's (approximate network time subtracted) rises from 44 ms to 211 ms and the Qwen3-8B judge's from 32 ms to 516 ms, making RubriX 7.7x faster than Jev and 18.8x faster than the Qwen3-8B judge at 3,000 tokens per level.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Atoms for Scalable Text Analysis in Data Analytics in the Era of LLM-Based Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34860)**  `arXiv:2610.34860`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinxiang Xie, Zihao Li, Rui Ding, Bartuer Zhou, Shi Han, Dongmei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Much of the information needed for data analysis is buried in text columns: an analyst may infer customer frustration from complaint text and use it to explain resolution time. We call workloads in which processing text columns is central to subsequent data analysis TADA (\emph{Text Analysis in Data Analytics}). In an agentic setting, the user's query dynamically specifies this processing, and answering it interleaves LLM-based semantic steps with exact SQL or Python operations. This mixed execution raises two design questions: which work should be neural and which symbolic, and which semantic outputs later steps can reuse. We address them for agent-planned TADA workflows with two complementary, query-driven semantic atoms. Each atom takes a sequence of text items and a natural-language query specifying the semantic task: \textbf{Tagging} returns one string per input item, while \textbf{Categorization} returns $M$ category objects over $N$ input items. Tagging results are materialized as a row-aligned column and Categorization results as a category table; SQL or Python then performs exact computation over this exposed state. We realize this interface in TADA-Agent, whose atoms run inside generated Python programs and internally handle inputs beyond a reliable context window. We evaluate TADA-Agent on TABench, a benchmark of 40 complete analysis requests over spreadsheet tables spanning workflow composition, input--output structure, and literature-grounded analytical scenarios. Against a semantic-operator baseline and relative to a held-out TADA reference run, TADA-Agent delivers comparable row-level semantic quality, has higher complete-artifact preservation estimates, and uses over an order of magnitude fewer semantic-model requests under the evaluated native execution paths.
  </details>

- **[Text Is the Bottleneck: A Small-Scale Replication of LLM Game Playing in Riichi Mahjong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34791)**  `arXiv:2610.34791`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingge Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We replicate the riichi-mahjong track of "Can Large Language Models Master Complex Card Games?" (arXiv:2509.01328) under a drastically smaller budget: models of at most 2B parameters, one 16 GB consumer GPU, about five GPU-days. (1) With matched data and labels, text is the bottleneck: on the same 100k-decision training set, a 2.6M-parameter convolutional board encoder agrees with the strong AI Mortal on 80.6% of test decisions, above every text-prompt LLM we train (best 77.8%), and places better at the table (2.85 over 1,000 games vs. ~3.0 for the LLMs; paired difference at n=100 not significant). Scaled to 1.71M decisions, a 4.9M-parameter Transformer reaches 94.7% agreement, beats the 0.8B LLMs on identical seeds (-0.45 ranks, sign test p=0.005), and is statistically equivalent to parity against three Mortal champions (TOST within 0.10 ranks, p=6e-4); parity against a teacher-opponent is roughly the ceiling of pure imitation. (2) Training toward the teacher raises teacher agreement more than a 2.5x parameter increase does (+2.5 vs. +1.5 points); on human-record agreement both effects are +1.0, and neither is visible at the table. (3) Few-point agreement differences do not resolve into playing strength within a family: +2.5 agreement points coincide with a paired placement difference of +0.02 +/- 0.12 ranks (p=1.0), and +2.0 points at 2B with -0.06 (p=0.37) - both below the ~0.25-rank resolution of 100 paired games. We also document a paper-code mismatch in the original winner filter, reconstruct its two private engine patches, and show that a divide-and-conquer pipeline makes template-generated Japanese rationales 93% sentence-exact (43% without facts). Per Tenhou's terms we release no trained weights - only code, configurations, and evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[Does the Outfit Change the Price? Context Halo in Vision-Language Model Valuation of an Identical Low-Cost Necklace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34591)**  `arXiv:2610.34591`  `cs.CL`  
  _Brianne Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A chunky gold-tone chain necklace bought online for 2.43 USD was photographed four ways: worn with formal attire against a wood-panelled wall, worn at a party, worn with casual clothing in a recycling yard, and laid flat with no person present. We asked six frontier language models to estimate its price in 1,164 independent and sequential API sessions (4,604 logged rows) and compared estimates across settings. Among the four models that answered image prompts, the same necklace was priced 1.9 to 3.3 times higher in the formal photograph than in the flat-lay (all p < 0.01). Against the yard photograph, the ratio ranged from 1.23 (Grok 4.5, not significant) to 3.57 (Kimi K3), with Claude Fable 5 at 3.32. A text-only version of the task, in which the setting is described in words, produced formal-to-yard ratios of 2.2 to 3.9 in all six models, so the effect does not require pixels. Flat-lay estimates themselves were 7.8 to 18.7 times the receipt price, so part of any headline "outfit multiplier" computed against the receipt is baseline overestimate, not a context effect. A single instruction to ignore clothing and setting significantly reduced the effect in one model (ratio of ratios 0.77, 95% CI 0.65 to 0.91) and not detectably in the others; telling the model the photo came from a company website or a personal account changed nothing significant. Asked afterward whether a different outfit would have changed their number, Claude Fable 5 said yes in 24 of 24 sessions, GPT-5.6 in 69%, and GPT-4o in 18%, while GPT-4o also refused 79% of image estimation prompts. The study isolates a mechanism with one object and one wearer at about 10 sessions per model and condition; we outline the replication needed next, with multiple objects and a human benchmark. Data, code, and raw outputs are released.
  </details>

- **[STEP: Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction Correctness via Per-Step Probability Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34537)**  `arXiv:2610.34537`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chenyang Yu, Zifan Zeng, Qunli Zhang, Feng Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating the correctness of large language model (LLM) predictions without access to ground truth is critical for reliable deployment in high-stakes settings. Existing approaches, such as entropy and perplexity, operate solely on final output statistics, discarding the rich information encoded in intermediate reasoning steps. To address this limitation, we propose STEP (Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction correctness), a training-free framework that scores each reasoning step via lightweight forward passes to obtain a soft probability distribution qi over candidate answers, from which we derive a set of soft trajectory features capturing per-step confidence and its progression toward the final answer. Extensive experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements, particularly in AUROC, over output-based baselines and hard-indicator trajectory methods. Notably, Temporally-Weighted Consistency, which assigns higher importance to later reasoning steps where answer distributions have stabilized, contributes the largest gains, highlighting the importance of temporal structure in reasoning trajectories. These results further validate the advantage of preserving continuous probability signals over binary discretization.
  </details>

- **[A Russian, a Brazilian, and a German Walk into a Bar, Ask the Same Question-- And Get Three Different Answers: Profiling LLM Perspective Diversity on Contested Topics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34278)**  `arXiv:2610.34278`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wiem Ben Rim, Yao Lu, Pontus Stenetorp, Emine Yilmaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models(LLMs) have shown strong performance on tasks with verifiable answers, but many real-world queries involve subjectivity, disagreement, and conflicting evidence. Most existing benchmarks for such queries remain English-centric, overlooking the vast diversity of perspectives held by users worldwide. To systematically study how LLM perspectives vary across languages, we curate PerspectivesQA, a manually verified multilingual dataset of globally contested topics covering 18 languages, and propose ROOTS, a framework that profiles linguistic, perspective, and framing diversity. We evaluate a diverse set of eight models covering both open-source and proprietary families at varying scales, establishing that LLMs hedge their answers in English but commit to positions in lower-resource languages. Larger models maintain a more consistent cross-lingual worldview, while smaller models are more easily swayed. All models also exhibit high sensitivity to minor surface-level input variations, producing notable stance even in larger models.
  </details>

- **[Higher Reasoning Effort Improves Reference Verifiability and Changes Reasoning Summaries in GPT-5.5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34212)**  `arXiv:2610.34212`  `cs.CL`  
  _Melika Mobini, Andres Algaba, Vincent Ginis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly being used in different stages of scientific knowledge production, including the suggestion of references. These models can operate at different levels of reasoning effort and provide summaries of their reasoning processes. This raises two questions: Does increasing reasoning effort improve the verifiability of generated references, and do the resulting reasoning summaries reveal how the model's behavior changes with greater effort? We examined summaries from GPT-5.5 as it generated scholarly references for 1,000 papers under paired low- and high-effort settings. Generated references were matched to external bibliographic records, and reasoning summaries were scored for four predefined features and explored for additional features discovered by GPT-5.5. High effort increased the mean paper-level resolver-verified reference rate from 78.3% to 84.8%, and produced longer reasoning summaries. All four predefined features received higher scores under high effort. The discovered features changed less uniformly: some appeared equally often in both conditions, whereas others were more common under high effort. Within each effort condition, neither feature set showed a consistent increase in verification rates with higher scores.
  </details>

- **[The LALA Benchmark: Evaluating Literary and Linguistic Analysis in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34080)**  `arXiv:2610.34080`  `cs.CL`  
  _Michael Foster, Barbara McGillivray, Gard B. Jenset, Jakub Mácha, Jason Harding, Marcus Perlman, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Literature and Linguistics Analysis Benchmark (LALA), comprising 236 open-ended questions written by experts in linguistics and literary studies, together with expert-provided ideal answers. By comparing models within families and at similar training scales using human and LLM judges, LALA identifies differences in linguistic competence associated with post-training, offering a framework for evaluating future models and guiding targeted improvements. We administered questions to 17 language models and evaluated their responses using both human expert assessment and two LLM evaluators, Sol and Qwen. Base models displayed little of the linguistic competence assessed by the benchmark, whereas their post-trained versions performed markedly better, suggesting that post-training plays a central role in making linguistic knowledge accessible and usable. Parameter count, by contrast, showed no statistically significant association with partial-credit performance, and competencies remained unevenly distributed across linguistic domains. We interpret this pattern as evidence that post-training adds linguistic competencies piecemeal, without producing a general linguistic intelligence capable of extending them beyond the learned policies that support them. Models performed best on logical relations between statements and selected discourse-level tasks, while even the strongest post-trained models continued to struggle with ambiguity, literary style, reference, and misleading assumptions. Human and Sol evaluations produced nearly identical model rankings in both the original and ReliableEval runs, supporting the replicability of the comparative results.
  </details>

- **[When Does GEPA Merge Help? A Mechanism-Level Diagnostic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34027)**  `arXiv:2610.34027`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chong Zhao, Ruyi Yang, Changming Liu, Alice Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GEPA is among the strongest reflective prompt optimizers for language-model programs, but its system-aware merge operator is unstable across models: merge improves gpt-4.1-mini on average while degrading Qwen3-8B, an asymmetry noted but left unexplained by the GEPA paper. We investigate this instability across five datasets and two models. Our nine-configuration sweep over merge timing and merge operator shows that merge can improve both models when configured appropriately. This suggests that merge instability is better explained by configuration sensitivity than by an inherent flaw in the merge operator. We then test whether prompt-level features predict GEPA's merge-accept decisions and find that they do not, ruling out static-feature gating. As a proof of concept, a behavior-based probe built from these diagnostics avoids large merge failures in our multi-seed runs by abstaining on high-risk tasks, although it does not consistently outperform NoMerge. Code is available as GEPA-MergeProbe atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **["High-Quality" Data for Early-Stage Instruction Tuning: An Empirical Study of Data Quality Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33969)**  `arXiv:2610.33969`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vladimir Necula, David Gvadzabia, Sofia Serrano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  "Data quality" is widely invoked in instruction tuning, but the term is often underspecified. We study it as an operational property by asking which simple example-level metrics, drawn from prior work on data selection, text quality, and instruction tuning, best predict training utility under a fixed finetuning budget. For each metric, we sort instruction-response examples by score, partition them into low, middle, and high bins with roughly equal token mass, finetune separately on each bin, and compare validation perplexity and LLM-as-judge scores for the models' generated answers as a function of cumulative training tokens. Our results suggest that answer-dense supervision, as captured by response ratio, is a stronger and more portable predictor of instruction-tuning utility than commonly used alternative proxies.
  </details>

- **[HerHealthGPT: An Evidence-Grounded Conversational AI System for Women's Reproductive Health Copy title](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33931)**  `arXiv:2610.33931`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hassan Saeed Hassan Albattra, Mazen Mohammed Bahgat, Rahatara Ferdousi, Hana Essam Sayed Ahmed Amrya, Mariam Mousa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  "Why is my period late?" Sometimes, the most useful response begins with another question. Yet conversational AI systems may respond to incomplete health queries without seeking essential context or grounding their guidance in verifiable evidence. This paper introduces HerHealthGPT, an evidence-grounded conversational AI system designed to address these challenges in women's reproductive health. HerHealthGPT integrates retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), context-aware clarification, and source validation within an interactive web interface. Rather than responding immediately to incomplete queries, the system maintains conversational context, asks targeted follow-up questions, checks for premature conclusions, and grounds its responses in retrieved evidence. We evaluate the AI components using 540 English messages derived from 90 reproductive-health cases and conduct a prototype questionnaire study with 30 adults. Evidence retrieval improves concern-category accuracy from 54.1% to 59.3% compared with fine-tuning alone. The clarification router achieves 85.6% recall and 86.2% specificity on constructed targets. In the user study, 83.3% of participants preferred the retrieval-enabled configuration, while 25 of 30 considered its follow-up questions relevant. These findings suggest that integrating evidence retrieval with interactive clarification offers a promising approach to more transparent, context-aware reproductive-health information systems, while further clinical validation remains necessary.
  </details>

- **[When Personas Become Harmful: Guiding LLMs with Global Citizenship Values Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33899)**  `arXiv:2610.33899`  `cs.CL`  
  _Siqi Shen, Lajanugen Logeswaran, Moontae Lee, Honglak Lee, Rada Mihalcea_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs are increasingly being used by users from diverse demographics with pluralistic values and distinct backgrounds. Maintaining safety while respecting value-laden personas is uniquely challenging because persona-conditioned requests are often not overtly malicious, unlike those in existing safety benchmarks. Alignment methods aimed at adversarial requests therefore may miss the harm that emerges from persona use, leaving models to either refuse legitimate value expression or blindly follow unsafe personas. We construct a dataset of 11k controversial topics paired with value-based personas mapping to value dimensions from the Schwartz Value Survey. Through an agentic pipeline that iteratively refines the topic and persona, we surface and collect harmful responses from well-aligned commercial LLMs with a more than 47% success rate. We use UNESCO's global citizenship principles as a minimal common ground for distinguishing valid value expressions from harmful behaviors, and introduce a controllable alignment model that lets users balance persona adherence and safety at inference time. The resulting model is safer and more persona-adherent than baselines on most samples.
  </details>

- **[MTA-Zero: Self-Improving Multimodal Deep Search via Online QA-Solver Co-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33799)**  `arXiv:2610.33799`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiangyu Peng, Jiaxin Zhang, An Yan, Yu Li, Ran Xu, Zeyuan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong visual reasoners, yet remain limited in open-world, multi-step search that requires grounding images, selecting tools, and integrating web evidence. Existing approaches typically construct and filter fixed offline corpora whose question distributions cannot adapt as the target deep-search agent improves. Building on MTA-Agent's image-grounded QA construction, we present MTA-Zero, an online multimodal QA-Solver co-training framework for self-improving multimodal deep-search agents that requires no precomputed multi-hop corpus. Starting from image-grounded VQA seed contexts, a trainable QA Generator retrieves evidence and constructs verified multi-hop questions online. A separate deep-search Solver then selects tools, gathers evidence, and answers the generated questions. To focus generation on the Solver's learning frontier, we derive a Goldilocks reward for the QA Generator from the Solver's group-wise rollout success rate: questions solved by all rollouts or by none receive zero reward, whereas questions near the current frontier are favored. We optimize the two policies independently with GRPO, without sharing parameters or gradients. Across six multimodal deep-search benchmarks, MTA-Zero improves a Qwen3-VL-8B Solver from 36.91% to 51.58% mean accuracy and achieves performance comparable to GPT-5 under the same agent workflow. The same framework also improves a newer Qwen3.6-27B Solver by 10.82 points. These results show that verified online question generation provides a policy-specific curriculum for continually improving multimodal deep-search agents.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Prompts: A Systematic Study of LLM-Based Machine Translation Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33741)**  `arXiv:2610.33741`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dmitry Popov, Roman Bokhyan, Ekaterina Enikeeva, Artem Mekhraliev, Stepan Vysotsky, Nikolay Karpachev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based evaluators have rapidly become a standard tool for machine translation evaluation, yet the design choices that shape their behavior remain poorly understood. We present a systematic study of how instruction design, few-shot demonstrations, backbone model choice, and multi-step workflows affect span-level and ranking-based MT evaluation. We collect new expert RATE annotations on WMT24 and WMT25 English-Russian translations, with transfer validation on MQM- and ESA-annotated benchmarks across five additional language pairs. Our experiments reveal four robust findings: instruction structure shapes evaluator behavior, high-quality demonstrations matter more than quantity, frontier backbones exhibit distinct evaluator profiles (recall-, precision-, or ranking-oriented), and fusing outputs across diverse backbones outperforms single-evaluator multi-call setups. Although evaluator design exposes a large combinatorial space, several discovered design principles transfer consistently across annotation protocols and language pairs. We synthesize these findings into ORBIT, a family of LLM-based MT evaluators. Our strongest configurations achieve expert-level performance in error span detection and in Accuracy and Fluency ranking at roughly 20x lower cost than expert human annotation.
  </details>

- **[Words Are the Voice of the Mind: Depression Severity Estimation from Social Traces via Mental-Guided Evidence Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33586)**  `arXiv:2610.33586`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nada Xu, Xueqin Chen, Qiang Gao, Hongru Liang, Wenqiang Lei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depression severity estimation is crucial for mental health assessment and intervention, yet traditional approaches depend on clinical interviews or self-report questionnaires that require users' active participation. With the widespread use of online social media, users leave rich social traces that may reflect their mental states, motivating the task of social media-based depression severity estimation. Existing efforts have progressed from textual-feature-based classification to LLM-based questionnaire completion over standardized scale items, yet they still fall short in user-specific mental priors modeling, diversified semantic retrieval, and fine-grained reasoning over mental evidence. To address these limitations, we propose UMer, a user-specific mental evidence reasoning framework for social traces-based depression severity estimation. UMer first distills a user-specific mental prior from historical social traces to capture the user's psychological tendencies, behavioral patterns, and self-expression style. It then performs scale-guided multi-query retrieval to construct evidence memories from item-relevant posts. Finally, it conducts coarse-to-fine evidence-aware reasoning to distinguish direct and indirect evidence and predict item-level scores with interpretable rationales. Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate that UMer not only achieves competitive performance over existing baselines, but also provides evidence-grounded interpretability and generalizes to other scale-based tasks.
  </details>

- **[Small Edits, Shifting Decisions: Benchmarking LLM Accuracy and Reliability on French Input Variants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33493)**  `arXiv:2610.33493`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaming Yu, Xiaotian Xie, Jian Ma, Yingjie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  French users may omit apostrophes and diacritics or make keyboard typos in everyday input. The effects of these common variations on language model performance and prediction reliability remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we construct a controlled French surface-variant benchmark covering seven task types, with 4,200 original-low-high triplets (12,600 input instances). Each high-intensity variant retains all low-intensity edits and adds further changes. We evaluate six models from two LLM families. The results show that accuracy decreases and Brier score and negative log-likelihood (NLL) increase with perturbation intensity. Larger models show smaller accuracy losses under high intensity, but increases in Brier score and NLL do not consistently decrease with scale. Keyboard typos cause the largest losses in accuracy and the quality of predicted probabilities at the predefined high-intensity settings. Finally, calibration temperatures fitted on original inputs transfer effectively to surface variants. Combining calibration with input restoration further improves Brier score and NLL over either method alone while preserving restored accuracy. Our benchmark provides a unified framework for jointly evaluating task accuracy and prediction reliability under common French surface variations.
  </details>

- **[Examining form-based linguistic productivity in people and large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33433)**  `arXiv:2610.33433`  `cs.CL`  
  _Michael Wilson, Robert Frank_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linguistic productivity, as evidenced by the use of words in novel contexts, is one of the most striking facts about human language. However, how abstract the knowledge that enables such productivity is remains unclear. We compare people's and computational language models' linguistic productivity in contexts where successful generalization depends on form-based information. We focus on extending the use of a novel verb learned in the active voice to passive contexts. We find people extend active to passive uses in a way consistent with abstract generalizations concerning linguistic form, even when no plausible meaning can be assigned to the novel verb. In contrast, large language models succeeded only when a meaning like an existing verb's could be assigned to the novel verb. We conclude that computational models' syntactic knowledge remains tied to the distribution and meaning of specific lexical items in a way that human syntactic knowledge is not.
  </details>

- **[How Does Post-Training Shape LLM Value Preferences?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33371)**  `arXiv:2610.33371`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Liu, Shaoyang Xu, Masahiro Kaneko, Chengzhi Zhong, Wenxuan Zhang, Chenhui Chu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  During post-training, large language models (LLMs) learn value preferences from corpora that may contain imbalanced proportions of opinions associated with different demographic groups. Amplifying these imbalances may further underrepresent some groups' opinions. However, how opinion mixtures shape value preferences across post-training paths remains unclear. To answer this question, we conduct controlled experiments using post-training corpora with different opinion mixtures and five post-training paths based on SFT, DPO, and GRPO. We find that post-training amplifies opinion imbalances differently across post-training paths. Once value preferences are amplified, whether further post-training can remove them depends on both the post-training method and the opinion mixture in the corpus. More concerningly, a model's stability under interventions depends on whether further post-training changes its value preference. Moreover, these value preferences are transferred to student models even when distillation uses corpora with no explicit opinion questions.
