These findings suggest that both the opinion mixture in post-training corpora and the choice of post-training method should be considered when developing, evaluating, and reusing LLMs. Our code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Distill, Debate, Discover: Grounding Multi-Agent Research Ideation in Graph-Distilled Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33366)**  `arXiv:2610.33366`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qianran Ma, Muxin Liu, Grace Kim, Xiao Liang, Chris Callison-Burch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support research idea generation and scientific discovery. Multi-agent debate offers a collaborative approach to this task by bringing together diverse perspectives to develop research ideas. However, a key challenge is how to equip agents with distinct and meaningful expertise to guide their contributions. Inspired by how researchers draw on accumulated research experience during collaboration, we introduce InsightAgents, a framework that constructs literature-grounded agent expertise by distilling insights from graphs of related papers. Guided by insights that trace evolving research approaches, reveal underlying assumptions, and identify unresolved challenges, within a line of research, agents jointly identify a research problem and propose, challenge, and refine potential approaches through debate to develop a research proposal. We evaluate InsightAgents against three research idea generation frameworks on 100 NLP research topics through pairwise comparisons by two LLM judges. In both online and offline knowledge settings, InsightAgents achieves the highest average score across the five evaluation dimensions under both judges.
  </details>

- **[FlexA-Mem: Budget-Adaptive Agentic Memory for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33274)**  `arXiv:2610.33274`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wujiang Xu, Jiaojiao Han, Xi Zhu, Qizheng Zhang, Kaiqu Liang, Yutong Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents work in scenarios with very different memory needs. A question about past conversations needs the exact facts it names, while a multi-step task needs a trace of the steps already taken together with details that the next step cannot name. Existing memories are each designed and tested for one kind of scenario. Retrieval memories such as A-Mem miss steps that the current query does not express, summaries drop exact strings, and pairing the two fixes the split of the budget in advance. We propose FlexA-Mem, a general memory that writes each unit of history once into an immutable store and renders a fresh memory for every call. The rendered memory is a compact timeline of records plus detail that a fixed rule fits into the remaining room without an LLM call. The detail is either relevant units as raw text or excerpts or the verbatim pieces that best match the query. With one store, FlexA-Mem serves both kinds of scenario. On AppWorld challenge tasks at 4k, it outperforms the second-best method by 11.5%, and on 147 unused tasks with predictions fixed in advance it outperforms A-Mem with a summary by 12.2%. On LongMemEval and LoCoMo, it matches a dedicated keyword retriever that writes no memory, while memories built on text written by an LLM fall far behind at tight budgets. Ablations show that the timeline carries AppWorld completion, whereas recovery mainly saves steps there and decides accuracy on exact facts. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Hey, guess what: CUES lead to inferences about typicality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33239)**  `arXiv:2610.33239`  `cs.CL`  
  _Alison Chi, Sanghyeok Choi, Hannah Rohde, Emily Allaway_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Throughout life, we accumulate knowledge of how typical certain events are. We use this knowledge in daily conversation, often interpreting meaning beyond what has been said explicitly. In this work, we take steps to better understand this meaning recovery, investigating both atypicality and typicality inferences. First, we construct two corpora, EventTyp and the human-validated EventTypGold. We conduct large-scale human and LLM experiments and find that for humans, three CUES predict inferences about typicality: Commonsense Knowledge, Utterance Emphasis, and Snark. However, in most experimental settings, we find that LLMs lack the prerequisite Commonsense Knowledge necessary to accurately assess an event's general typicality, and therefore struggle to follow these CUES.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge-Guided LLM for Intent-Driven Service Orchestration in Integrated Terrestrial and Non-Terrestrial 6G Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33007)**  `arXiv:2610.33007`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wajdi Hammami, Irfan Azam, Soumaya Cherkaoui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language service intents in integrated terrestrial and non-terrestrial (TN/NTN) 6G networks can be incomplete, ambiguous, or inconsistent, making unconstrained LLM generation challenging for reliable network-facing orchestration. This paper proposes a knowledge-guided LLM framework that combines grammar-constrained semantic parsing with a knowledge-graph (KG)-driven clarification loop to produce validated, orchestration-ready service profiles across four representative service classes. On a 200-sample intent dataset, the framework achieves 89% classification accuracy and 95% policy validity with an average of 0.66 clarification turns per request. Compared with a Pure-LLM baseline, the proposed framework improves policy validity and contradiction detection while requiring fewer clarification turns, demonstrating the value of explicit knowledge-guided reasoning for robust intent processing in integrated TN/NTN 6G orchestration.
  </details>

- **[AfriGuard: Safety and Robust Refusal For African Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26474)**  `arXiv:2610.26474`  `cs.CL`  
  _Israel Abebe Azime, Jesujoba O. Alabi, Iffat Maab, Alexander D. Cai, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Atnafu Lambebo Tonja, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) designed to interact with users are typically trained to respond appropriately to harmful or toxic content. However, ensuring appropriate responses becomes more challenging in diverse linguistic and cultural contexts, where safety risks may be expressed or interpreted differently. Building safe AI systems therefore requires detecting safety risks, understanding their nature, and responding appropriately, while accounting for linguistic and cultural variations that add complexity to each stage. In this work, we introduce AfriGuard, a culturally grounded multilingual dataset for studying harmful intent, safety-category understanding, and refusal behavior across 10 African languages and English. Our findings reveal that guardrail models underperform both open- and closed-source LLMs on safety-intent and safety-category classification, while models show limited understanding of appropriate safety behavior, scoring below 60% F1. We further find that models often fail to respond appropriately to unsafe prompts not because they explicitly refuse, but because they produce linguistically inadequate responses that result in incidental non-assistance rather than proper refusals. Finally, using AfriGuard, we develop efficient guardrail models that achieve performance competitive with models up to 30x larger, introduce refusal detectors that improve average performance by 43.9% over existing detectors, and demonstrate that supervised fine-tuning can substantially improve overall safety performance.
  </details>

- **[Truncated Reasoning Under a Budget: Evaluating System-1 Models as Decision-Makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25768)**  `arXiv:2610.25768`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongyin Wang, Zhaoqian Xue, Junting Mu, Haozheng Liu, Ye Yuan, Haolun Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often generate more reasoning tokens than needed. Limiting the reasoning budget reduces cost, but forcing an answer from an incomplete trace can hurt accuracy. System-1 decision models select among candidate options without producing a reasoning response. We evaluate Jev as a decision reader of truncated reasoning across seven LLM reasoners and five multiple-choice benchmarks (35 pairs). Jev achieves higher mean accuracy than forced answering at tested budgets from 500 to 16,000 tokens, including the estimated costs of reasoning and answering. At a 1,500-token budget, it improves accuracy by 4.1 pp overall and 8.6 pp on truncated questions, with significant gains in 17 pairs under Holm-corrected bootstrap tests. Offline replay estimates 34.5% of the full-run cost, with a 1.13 pp accuracy loss. A policy selected on training folds saves 71.3% of the estimated full-run cost on held-out folds, with a 0.58 pp accuracy loss. We also find that Jev shows strong question-only accuracy, exceeding 500-token forced answering on average (68.0% vs. 65.5%), although this advantage varies by benchmark. These findings suggest that System-1 decision models can complement LLM reasoning and reduce the need for costly reasoning completion.
  </details>

- **[Strong yet Controllable LLM Abstention without Learning to Abstain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16318)**  `arXiv:2610.16318`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junwei Zhang, Yifei Wang, Wanli Yang, Hexiang Tan, Rui Tang, Xin Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can reduce hallucination by abstaining from questions they would likely get wrong. Abstention can be implemented outside the model (selective answering) using a confidence threshold that controls the answer rate (coverage), or learned inside it (abstention training), which can perform better but fixes coverage once trained. To compare the two, we match their coverage and decompose risk (error rate) into answering ability and distinguishing ability, i.e., how well the model answers and how accurately it abstains, respectively. The decomposition shows that abstention training performs better mainly by answering better: with strong confidence estimators, the two have nearly equal distinguishing ability. This motivates Train for Accuracy, then Post-hoc Select (TAPS), a modular approach that keeps coverage controllable, needs only standard post-training and an off-the-shelf confidence estimator, and benefits from advances in either component. Across three LLMs and three factual QA benchmarks, TAPS with a probing-based confidence estimator attains 1.5--9.4 percentage points lower mean risk than three representative abstention training methods at matched coverage. Within TAPS, accuracy training often weakens the model's own confidence signals, yet refitting the estimator largely preserves distinguishing ability. Together, these results establish TAPS as a strong yet controllable baseline and raise the bar for future abstention training: sacrificing control over coverage should yield gains beyond what can be achieved by optimizing answering and abstaining separately.
  </details>

- **[Old Ideas, Novel Problems: The Instability of LLM-Based Novelty Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02022)**  `arXiv:2610.02022`  `cs.CL`  
  _Noy Sternlicht, Simra Shahid, Peter Jansen, Daniel S. Weld, Pao Siangliulue, Tom Hope_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-based ideation systems are often evaluated on the novelty of their ideas, and that judgment is increasingly delegated to large language models. Such judges are typically built ad hoc and validated, if at all, on human-authored papers rather than on the generated ideas they are meant to score. So, how do novelty judges perform? Not well. We present a systematic controlled study of novelty evaluation design choices. We build an evaluation set automatically, mining OpenReview for passages where reviewers explicitly affirm or dispute a paper's originality and keeping only submissions with unanimous agreement at the extremes of their research area; we pair these with ideas from a vanilla LLM generator. Across six judges, we find that seemingly small prompt design choices have large consequences; e.g., simply telling the judge that reviewers found one idea novel and the other not can change its verdict on more than half of the identical idea pairs it is shown, shifting pairwise accuracy by over 50 points and occasionally pushing it below chance. The same change helps one judge and hurts another. Retrieval and larger reasoning budgets help little, and two purpose-built novelty evaluators are outperformed by our cheapest prompted baseline. These results raise questions about reported novelty gains of automated ideation systems, and call for robust novelty evaluation methods.
  </details>

- **[Late Attention Layers Alone Can Copy Entity Tokens, but Not Without Attending to Their Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35663)**  `arXiv:2609.35663`  `cs.CL`  
  _Muyu He, Yuchen Liu, Ran Tao, Li Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) reliably perform entity copying, in which a model copies tokens referring to an entity, termed entity tokens, from the prompt into its output to answer a question. Although entity copying is straightforward for most LLMs, existing research does not provide a systematic account of which layers specialize in this fundamental task or how other tokens in the same sequence, termed context tokens, influence the model's ability to copy the entity tokens. To address these questions, we conduct experiments on Qwen3-8B using two novel methods: genie-in-a-bottle, which controls exactly which layers can participate in an entity-copying task, and attention lobotomy, which cuts off specific tokens' attention to entity tokens without affecting the remaining attention distribution. We find that two distinct groups of layers in the second half of the model are both necessary and sufficient for entity copying. Moreover, in addition to the decoding position's attention to entity tokens, context tokens' attention to entity tokens also proves necessary for copying the exact tokens, even though context tokens do not store entity information themselves unless they satisfy particular semantic properties. Our findings establish the critical role of late layers in entity copying under the guidance of context tokens, calling for future work on how models propagate and consume entity information.
  </details>

- **[Open-Jev Judgments on CallScreenBench: Calibrated One-Pass Scam Screening with a Small Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23959)**  `arXiv:2609.23959`  `cs.CL`  
  _Simiao Ren, Kidus Zewde, Xingyu Shen, Yuchen Zhou, Dennis Ng, Ankit Raj, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Screening a phone call for fraud needs a trustworthy probability after every caller turn, in milliseconds. Jev-style typed decisions promise exactly that: declared options go in, one calibrated probability per option comes out of a single forward pass, with no generated text. We test an open implementation of this readout, JevLite, on scam-call screening: Qwen3-4B is LoRA-tuned so that the temperature-scaled softmax over two answer-label logits is P(scam). On 41 held-out CallScreenBench scenarios (577 per-turn decisions) a three-seed ensemble reaches AUROC .974 with calibration error .052, non-inferior to an LLM judge (MiniMax-M3) at a pre-registered .02 margin, with no false alarms on legitimate calls, decisions 1.14 turns earlier under the same hang-up rule, and 64.5 ms per decision on one consumer GPU, 4.9x lower than the same backbone fine-tuned to generate its answer. The gain is in the readout and calibration, not accuracy: a fine-tuned ModernBERT encoder is not significantly worse, the recipe was selected with test-set exposure, and all callers are synthetic. We claim no architectural novelty; the contribution is the application and an evaluation reporting calibration, false alarms and decision timing alongside AUROC.
  </details>

- **[Recognition-Refusal Misalignment in LLMs: Why Models Answer Structurally Unanswerable Questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29109)**  `arXiv:2608.29109`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yucheng Du, Xiyang Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often answer structurally unanswerable questions, such as computing cot(-540°) or evaluating (1).startswith("1"), instead of abstaining. We ask whether this failure reflects missing recognition or failed routing from recognition to abstention. Across instruction-tuned models from 1.7B to 70B parameters, a single linear direction in the hidden state separates answerable from structurally impossible math and code prompts, showing that models represent impossibility before generation. Yet this recognition direction is nearly orthogonal to the canonical safety-refusal direction that mediates trained harmful-content refusal. An in-domain behavior-defined invalidity-aware direction is closer to recognition, but only partially aligned with it, and remains near-orthogonal to safety refusal. Generation-time steering along the recognition direction changes invalidity-aware behavior bidirectionally and dose-responsively on structural math and code cells, while random directions do not. Base/instruct comparisons further show that the low-cosine geometry is already present at the pretraining endpoint. The confident-on-impossible failure is therefore better explained as a routing failure than as an encoding failure: the model has a usable "no admissible answer" signal, but the safety-refusal pathway is not aligned to use it.
  </details>

- **[Human-LLM Alignment in Language Attitudes Toward Non-Native Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01629)**  `arXiv:2608.01629`  `cs.CL`  
  _Naho Orita, Hayato Ogawa, Daisuke Kawahara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly evaluate human writing in high-stakes domains such as hiring and academic assessment, putting non-native speakers at particular risk. Drawing on the language attitudes framework, we compared human and LLM evaluations of parallel L1- and L2-written Japanese emails on three dimensions: fluency, status, and solidarity. Japanese raters rated L2 texts significantly lower on all three dimensions, with a fluency gap roughly twice the size of the status and solidarity gaps. Six LLM judges reproduced the direction of this bias, and five reproduced its ordering across dimensions. The models diverged from humans in two ways: all understated the solidarity gap, the most socially grounded dimension, and all differentiated among learner L1 backgrounds where humans did not. LLM judges thus reproduce native speakers' language attitudes in a structured yet attenuated form, and the language attitudes framework offers a ready-made yardstick for auditing them beyond English.
  </details>

- **[Pancasila-Dilemmas: Evaluating Large Language Models on Indonesian Human Value Dilemmas Grounded in Pancasila](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18066)**  `arXiv:2607.18066`  `cs.CL`  
  _Supryadi, Irfan, Julianti, Darren Keanly Martin, Jayvin Fernando, Yuqi Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The value alignment of large language models (LLMs) is crucial for ensuring responses align with human intention and value preferences. However, most evaluations of value alignment focus on Western or universal values, while assessments grounded in the value systems of specific countries remain scarce. In this paper, we introduce Pancasila-Dilemmas, an evaluation dataset of 1,834 questions derived from Indonesian news, classified by 5 values of Pancasila: Religion, Humanity, Unity, Democracy, and Social Justice. This dataset reflects daily life in Indonesia, making it suitable for measuring the value alignment of LLMs deployed for Indonesia. To ensure a more rigorous evaluation, we choose scenarios containing dilemmas. The dataset is proofread by native speakers and answered by 5 diverse Indonesian citizens. We evaluate 50 closed- and open-source LLMs on our dataset. Results reveal that all evaluated LLMs achieves less than 0.5 Probability Match Score (PMS) and 0.72 Max-Vote Agreement Score (MVAS). Compared by each values, LLMs mostly struggle in Religion and Unity dilemma cases. This highlights a significant gap in capturing Indonesian values. The dataset is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Root Memory: Benchmarking and Enhancing Implicit Logical Memory Retrieval for Personalized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23283)**  `arXiv:2606.23283`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hongxun Ding, Xiang Yu, Chengbing Wang, Jianfei Xiao, Keqin Bao, Wenjie Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems are essential for personalized Large Language Models (LLMs). However, retrieval within these systems primarily relies on semantic similarity, potentially missing logically critical memories with limited semantic overlap. We refer to this failure as semantic deflection, which remains insufficiently evaluated by existing benchmarks. To address this gap, we construct IMLogic, a benchmark for implicit logical memory retrieval in long dialogues. Each question is designed around the divergence between semantic and logical relevance, with diagnostic response options that probe semantic deflection, hallucination, and lack of personalization. To help systems utilize associations that are difficult to discover through semantic matching, we introduce root memory, a structured representation that distills reusable, response-guiding logic from user histories through LLM reasoning and grounds it in supporting evidence. We further develop RootMem, a plug-and-play framework that constructs and routes root memories to complement semantic retrieval. Extensive experiments reveal persistent challenges across retrieval methods and memory systems, including substantial preference-driven errors even under full-memory access. RootMem consistently improves performance, demonstrating the potential of structured personalized logic while leaving considerable challenges unresolved. Our benchmark and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Differences Is Not Understanding Structure: Large Language Models Fail at Graph Isomorphism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09484)**  `arXiv:2606.09484`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kumar Thushalika, Sukumar Kishanthan, Asela Hevapathige_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown impressive performance on diverse reasoning tasks, yet their capacity for structural reasoning in graphs remains unclear. We investigate whether LLMs can genuinely understand graph isomorphism -a fundamental problem in graph theory. While LLMs achieve near-perfect accuracy on isomorphism detection, we show this performance is illusory. When identical graphs are presented with permuted node labels, LLMs fail to identify their isomorphism. This finding suggests that LLMs exploit patterns rather than reasoning about abstract graph structure. Since permutation invariance is a fundamental requirement for valid structural reasoning, these results indicate that success on graph reasoning benchmarks should not be interpreted as evidence of genuine topological understanding.
  </details>

- **[Unified Context Evolution for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02304)**  `arXiv:2606.02304`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zixuan Zhu, Yitong Hu, Yong Dai, Junfeng Fang, Chunyang Jiang, Senkang Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents can solve multi-step interactive tasks by combining reasoning with environment feedback, yet each episode starts from the same fixed context and any useful strategy discovered along the way is lost once the task ends. Existing approaches either limit learning to the current task or pool all experience into a single untyped store, without distinguishing knowledge types, tracking quality through use, or balancing what the library still lacks. We introduce Unified Context Evolution (UCE), a gradient-free framework that externalizes agent experience into an evolving library of typed Evolvable Context Units (ECUs). UCE decomposes experience into four complementary types (Memory, Strategy, Workflow, and Skill), each generated from trajectories under type-specific conditions, retrieved at decision time, scored through repeated usage outcomes, and pruned when no longer valuable. A scheduling module allocates each cycle's generation budget toward the types where the library is weakest. Across two interactive benchmarks, UCE raises ALFWorld success from 75.4% to 96.3% and WebShop task score from 45.1% to 61.3%, and remains effective across alternative actor reasoning backbones without model retraining.
  </details>

- **[HyLaT: Efficient Multi-Agent Communication via Hybrid Latent-Text Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25421)**  `arXiv:2605.25421`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyi Mou, Siyuan Wang, Zejun Li, Yulan He, Zhongyu Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Communication protocol design is a central challenge in large language model-based multi-agent systems. Existing single-channel approaches face an inherent communication trilemma: text-based methods are interpretable but verbose, while latent-space methods are efficient but opaque and limited to unidirectional workflows. Inspired by multi-channel communication theory, we propose HyLaT, a hybrid latent-text communication protocol that transmits elaborate cognitive signals through a latent channel for efficiency, while expressing concise critical signals in natural language to preserve interpretability and precision. We introduce a two-stage training framework combining single-agent hybrid generation learning and multi-agent interactive co-training, enabling agents to generate and interpret hybrid messages across multiple rounds of interaction. Experiments demonstrate that HyLaT reduces communication overhead significantly while maintaining competitive task performance, with strong generalization and robustness across diverse settings.
  </details>

- **[Naturalistic measure of social norms alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23420)**  `arXiv:2605.23420`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yevhen Kostiuk, Kenneth Enevoldsen, Peter Bjerregaard Vahlstrup, Márton Kardos, Kristoffer Nielbo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social norms reflect shared expectations on acceptable behavior. Measuring social norms alignment remains challenging, with existing approaches typically relying on artificial closed-form evaluations such as multiple-choice questionnaires or measuring agreement with predefined statements. In the context of this work, social norms alignment refers to measuring an agreement between solutions with respect to the social problem or dilemma. We propose a framework for measuring social norm alignment in naturalistic, free-form settings through solution matching. The framework enables us to measure alignment between any two dilemma responses e.g., LLMs to a human, LLMs to LLMs, or human to human. We introduce two metrics: stated and explicit agreement accuracy, and construct a dataset of 3k non-trivial social dilemmas in Danish. All dilemmas are assigned reference solutions derived from three panelists, who serve as culturally grounded judges. We evaluate the agreement of several LLMs and human responses in an interaction setup that resembles natural user-model conversations. Our results show that the proposed metrics produce consistent model rankings and reveal variation in agreement across different types of dilemmas, with higher agreement observed for topics such as neighbor conflicts and shared living situations. Overall, our work introduces a dataset and evaluation framework for studying culturally grounded social reasoning in naturalistic open-ended conversations.
  </details>

- **[VerifyMAS: Hypothesis Verification for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17467)**  `arXiv:2605.17467`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hezhe Qiao, Hanghang Tong, Ee-Peng Lim, Bing Liu, Guansong Pang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model-driven multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) excel at complex tasks, yet unreliable agents remain a key bottleneck to system-level reliability. Automatic failure attribution is therefore critical, but existing approaches, such as direct prediction of agent-error pairs and agent-first failure attribution, rely on local logs of agents and miss global failures that only manifest over full interaction trajectories, such as cross-step inconsistencies and inter-agent coordination errors. Moreover, directly predicting failures induces a large combinatorial search space, hindering fine-grained attribution. To address these challenges, we propose VerifyMAS, a hypothesis verification framework for agent failure attribution. Instead of directly predicting faulty agents and error types, VerifyMAS formulates and verifies failure hypotheses against full trajectories. This verification-based approach decomposes attribution into trajectory-level error validation and fine-grained agent localization, providing an error-first attribution approach that captures global failure patterns while substantially reducing the search space. We further introduce a hypothesis-based data construction strategy grounded in a structured error taxonomy and fine-tune a specialized LLM verifier model for trajectory-level failure verification and agent attribution. Experiments on Aegis-Bench and Who&When show that VerifyMAS consistently improves diverse backbone models, including open-source Qwen and API-based GPT models, outperforming prior methods without sacrificing inference efficiency for long multi-agent trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Why Are Some Emotions Harder for LLMs? Uncovering the Causal Mechanisms of Emotion Inference via Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25866)**  `arXiv:2604.25866`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bangzhao Shu, Arinjay Singh, Mai ElSherief_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in emotionally sensitive human-AI applications, where reliable emotion detection is essential. However, their emotion recognition abilities remain uneven: models often perform well on some emotions while consistently struggling with others. Although recent work has explored emotion mechanisms in LLMs, little is known about why models are weaker on some emotions than others from a mechanistic interpretability perspective. In this work, we investigate emotion-specific biases through the causal mechanisms of emotion inference using sparse autoencoders (SAEs). We systematically identify causal sparse emotion features that drive emotion inference and analyze their sparse causal organization within and across emotions. We show that some emotions, such as surprise and fear, rely on highly concentrated feature sets, whereas disgust exhibits a more distributed sparse causal organization: its causal features are generally weaker, frequently co-activate with features for other emotions, and are often overshadowed by causal features for anger. These representational differences provide a mechanistic explanation for why LLMs struggle more with certain emotions. Finally, we conduct two intervention experiments: targeted steering of weaker causal features to mitigate emotion-specific failures, and global optimization of a steering vector over the identified causal features to improve overall emotion recognition performance.
  </details>

- **[OdysseyArena: Benchmarking Large Language Models For Long-Horizon, Active and Inductive Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05843)**  `arXiv:2602.05843`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fangzhi Xu, Hang Yan, Qiushi Sun, Jinyang Wu, Zixian Huang, Muye Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has catalyzed the development of autonomous agents capable of navigating complex environments. However, existing evaluations primarily adopt a deductive paradigm, where agents execute tasks based on explicitly provided rules and static goals, often within limited planning horizons. Crucially, this neglects the inductive necessity for agents to discover latent transition laws from experience autonomously, which is the cornerstone for enabling agentic foresight and sustaining strategic coherence. To bridge this gap, we introduce OdysseyArena, which re-centers agent evaluation on long-horizon, active, and inductive interactions. We formalize and instantiate four primitives, translating abstract transition dynamics into concrete interactive environments. Building upon this, we establish OdysseyArena-Lite for standardized benchmarking, providing a set of 120 tasks to measure an agent's inductive efficiency and long-horizon discovery. Pushing further, we introduce OdysseyArena-Challenge to stress-test agent stability across extreme interaction horizons (e.g., > 200 steps). Extensive experiments on 15+ leading LLMs reveal that even frontier models exhibit a deficiency in inductive scenarios, identifying a critical bottleneck in the pursuit of autonomous discovery in complex environments. Project homepage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[The Straight and Narrow: Do LLMs Possess an Internal Moral Path?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10307)**  `arXiv:2601.10307`  `cs.CL`  
  _Luoming Hu, Jingjie Zeng, Liang Yang, Hongfei Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enhancing the moral alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) is a critical challenge in AI safety. Current alignment techniques often act as superficial guardrails, leaving the intrinsic moral representations of LLMs largely untouched. In this paper, we bridge this gap by leveraging Moral Foundations Theory (MFT) to map and manipulate the fine-grained moral landscape of LLMs. Through cross-lingual linear probing, we validate the shared nature of moral representations in middle layers and uncover a shared yet different moral subspace between English and Chinese. Building upon this, we extract steerable Moral Vectors and successfully validate their efficacy at both internal and behavioral levels. Leveraging the high generalizability of morality, we propose Adaptive Moral Fusion (AMF), a dynamic inference-time intervention that synergizes probe detection with vector injection to tackle the safety-helpfulness trade-off. Empirical results confirm that our approach acts as a targeted intrinsic defense, effectively reducing incorrect refusals on benign queries while minimizing jailbreak success rates compared to standard baselines.
  </details>

- **[Stylistic Evolution and LLM Neutrality in Singlish Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06580)**  `arXiv:2601.06580`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linus Tze En Foo, Weihan Angela Ng, Wenkai Li, Lynnette Hui Xian Ng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Singlish is a creole rooted in Singapore's multilingual environment that continues to evolve alongside social and technological change. We examine diachronic stylistic change across a decade of informal digital messages and ask whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate temporally neutral outputs approximating the stable essence of the variety. Using lexical, pragmatic, psycholinguistic, and encoder-based features, we find that stylistic separability increases with temporal distance, driven primarily by structural features such as length and complexity. Evaluated against a null distribution baseline, most LLMs fail to achieve both authenticity and temporal neutrality simultaneously, revealing a structural trade-off: models generating realistic Singlish inherit its temporal biases, while temporally neutral models produce inauthentic outputs. These findings position temporal neutrality as a diagnostic metric for assessing sociolectal grounding in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Toward Global Large Language Models in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02186)**  `arXiv:2601.02186`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rui Yang, Huitao Li, Weihao Xuan, Heli Qi, Xin Li, Kunyu Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite continuous advances in medical technology, the global distribution of health care resources remains uneven. The development of large language models (LLMs) has transformed the landscape of medicine and holds promise for improving health care quality and expanding access to medical information globally. However, existing LLMs are primarily trained on high-resource languages, limiting their applicability in global medical scenarios. To address this gap, we constructed GlobMed, a large multilingual medical dataset containing more than 800,000 entries spanning 20 languages, including seven low-resource languages. Building on this, we established GlobMed-Bench, which systematically assesses 56 state-of-the-art proprietary and open-weight LLMs across 12 of the 20 languages represented in GlobMed, comprising over 20,000 independent experiments and more than 32 million model responses. This large-scale evaluation revealed significant performance disparities across languages, particularly for low-resource languages. Additionally, we introduced GlobMed-LLMs, a suite of multilingual medical LLMs trained across the 12 benchmark languages, with parameters ranging from 1.7B to 8B. GlobMed-LLMs achieved an average performance improvement of nearly 40% relative to baseline LLMs, with a maximum performance improvement of more than threefold in low-resource languages. These resources provide an important foundation for advancing the equitable development and application of LLMs globally, enabling broader language communities to benefit from technological advances.
  </details>

- **[Role-aware Heuristic Episodic Attention for Conversational LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.06869)**  `arXiv:2512.06869`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wanyang Hong, Zhaoning Zhang, Yi Chen, Libo Zhang, Baihui Liu, Linbo Qiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often lose track of persistent instructions and relevant information as multi-turn conversations grow. We study this cumulative contextual decay through three related failure modes: attention pollution, dilution, and drift. We propose REA(Role-aware Heuristic Episodic Attention), a context-management framework that assigns different persistence and representation policies to instructions and episodic interactions. Instructional Memory retains identified global constraints in a dedicated prefix. Episodic Memory preserves user inputs and compresses model replies, while heuristic retrieval selects raw text, compressed representations, or omission for each historical turn. On Long-MT-Bench+, REA improves the judge score from 6.32 to 7.36 on a 10-point scale, a 16.5% relative gain over the Vanilla baseline, and reduces average latency by 2.91x. Additional evaluations show aggregate gains on three backbones spanning 1.7B-7B parameters and on Chinese and English role-playing tasks. These results support role-aware context management as a practical approach to maintaining conversational continuity and instruction adherence.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Policy Adaptation for Enhanced Multi-Turn Interactions with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23166)**  `arXiv:2509.23166`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chenxing Wei, Hong Wang, Ying He, Fei Yu, Yao Shu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) employ multi-turn interaction as a fundamental paradigm for completing complex tasks. However, their performance often degrades in extended interactions, as they are typically trained on static, single-turn data, which hinders their ability to adapt to real-time user feedback. To address this limitation, we first propose a new paradigm: Test-Time Policy Adaptation for Multi-Turn Interactions (T2PAM), which utilizes user feedback from the ongoing interaction as a reward signal to estimate a latent optimal policy aligned with user preferences, then updates a small subset of parameters to steer the model toward this policy, ultimately enabling efficient in-conversation self-correction. We then introduce Optimum-Referenced One-Step Adaptation (ROSA), a lightweight algorithm that operationalizes T2PAM. ROSA guides the model parameters toward a theoretical optimal policy in a single, efficient update step, avoiding costly iterative gradient-based optimization and minimizing computational overhead. We provide a rigorous theoretical analysis guaranteeing that the policy of ROSA converges to the preference of user as the number of interactions increases. Extensive experiments on challenging benchmark demonstrate that ROSA achieves significant improvements in both task effectiveness and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[SimulSense: Sense-Driven Interpreting for Efficient Simultaneous Speech Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21932)**  `arXiv:2509.21932`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haotian Tan, Hiroki Ouchi, Sakriani Sakti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How to make human-interpreter-like read/write decisions for simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) systems? Current state-of-the-art systems formulate SimulST as a multi-turn dialogue task, requiring specialized interleaved training data and relying on computationally expensive large language model (LLM) inference for decision-making. In this paper, we propose SimulSense, a novel framework for SimulST that mimics human interpreters by continuously reading input speech and triggering write decisions to produce translation when a new sense unit is perceived. Experiments against two state-of-the-art baseline systems demonstrate that our proposed method achieves a superior quality-latency tradeoff and substantially improved real-time efficiency, where its decision-making is up to 9.6x faster than the baselines.
  </details>

- **[KnowMT-Bench: Benchmarking Knowledge-Intensive Long-Form Question Answering in Multi-Turn Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21856)**  `arXiv:2509.21856`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junhao Chen, Yu Huang, Siyuan Li, Rui Yao, Hanqian Li, Hanyu Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Turn Long-Form Question Answering (MT-LFQA) is a key application paradigm of Large Language Models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive domains. However, existing benchmarks are limited to single-turn dialogue, while multi-turn dialogue benchmarks typically assess other orthogonal capabilities rather than knowledge-intensive factuality. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce \textbf{KnowMT-Bench}, a new benchmark designed to systematically evaluate MT-LFQA for LLMs across knowledge-intensive fields, including medicine, finance, and law. To faithfully assess the model's real-world performance, KnowMT-Bench employs a dynamic evaluation setting where models generate their own multi-turn dialogue histories given logically progressive question sequences. Our experiments on a diverse suite of LLMs show a clear degradation in both factual capability and information delivery efficiency in multi-turn dialogue. We further probe the underlying causes and investigate mitigation strategies spanning domain-specific finetuning, active context denoising, and RAG. The results show domain-dependent gains from finetuning, while active context denoising and RAG more consistently mitigate these issues, with RAG notably reversing this performance degradation. These findings underscore the importance of our benchmark for evaluating LLMs' conversational factual capabilities in real-world applications. Code and data are publicly available.\footnote{\href{this https URL}{\texttt{this https URL}}}
  </details>

- **[Large Language Model probabilities cannot reliably distinguish between possible and impossible language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15114)**  `arXiv:2509.15114`  `cs.CL`  
  _Evelina Leivada, Raquel Montero, Paolo Morosi, Natalia Moskvina, Tamara Serrano, Nikoleta Pantelidou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A controversial test for Large Language Models concerns the ability to discern possible from impossible language. While some evidence attests to the models sensitivity to what crosses the limits of grammatically impossible language, this evidence has been contested on the grounds of the soundness of the testing material. We use model-internal representations as a direct means of investigating the way Large Language Models represent the grammatical-ungrammatical distinction. In a novel benchmark, we elicit sum log probabilities from 8 models and compute minimal-pair surprisal differences, juxtaposing probabilities assigned to grammatical sentences to probabilities assigned to (i) lower frequency grammatical sentences, (ii) ungrammatical sentences, (iii) semantically odd sentences, and (iv) pragmatically odd sentences. The prediction is that if string-probabilities can function as proxies for the limits of grammar, the ungrammatical condition will stand out among the conditions that involve linguistic violations, showing a spike in the surprisal rates. Our results do not reveal a unique surprisal signature for ungrammatical prompts: across models, we observe comparable surprisal for the pragmatically odd condition, and the semantically odd condition consistently shows even higher surprisal. We thus demonstrate that probabilities do not constitute reliable proxies for grammaticality, showing that probability differences alone cannot be used to infer model-internal representations of syntactic knowledge. Consequently, claims about models being able to distinguish possible from impossible language need verification through a different methodology.
  </details>

- **[Auto-AdvER: Disentangling Condition, History, and Commercial Offers in Automotive Advertisements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.05655)**  `arXiv:2412.05655`  `cs.CL`  
  _Filippos Ventirozos, Ioanna Nteka, Tania Nandy, Jozef Baca, Charlie Roadhouse, Peter A. Appleby, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a case study on the development of Auto-AdvER, a specialised span-labelling schema, along with its corresponding dataset for the textual car advertisement domain. Developed with industry needs in mind, Auto-AdvER is designed to enhance text mining analytics in this domain and contributes a linguistically unique sequence-labelling dataset. We introduce a schema consisting of three labels: 'Condition', 'Historic', and 'Sales Options'. We outline the guiding principles for annotation, describe the methodology for schema development, and present the results of an annotation study demonstrating an inter-annotator agreement of 78%. Furthermore, we compare the performance of various models, including transformer encoder models (BERT, DeBERTaV3) and transformer decoder-based open- and closed-source large language models: Llama, Qwen, GPT, and Gemini, as well as conditional random fields. Our results show that a fine-tuned DeBERTaV3 encoder achieves the highest F1-score, followed closely by conditional random fields, while the large language models reach the highest precision but lower recall. We present this work as a stepping stone towards more fine-grained analysis and discuss Auto-AdvER's potential impact on advertisement analytics, pricing, and customer insights. Potential applications include the analysis of market dynamics and data-driven predictive maintenance.
  </details>

- **[EvoRouter: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent Routing via Insight-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35690)**  `arXiv:2610.35690`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yitao Xiao, Xu Zhang, Ye Cheng, Shaoyong Guo, Yan Sun, Ziyue Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Serving web queries at scale with multi-agent systems requires jointly configuring large language models, collaboration modes, agent counts, interaction rounds, and skills. However, the resulting configuration space grows combinatorially, while sparse execution rewards provide limited fine-grained supervision for interdependent planning decisions. We propose EvoRouter, a self-evolving multi-agent routing framework driven by insight-guided on-policy distillation. A dedicated expert periodically synthesizes performance-cost feedback from configuration trajectories across tasks into reusable conditional routing insights about capability requirements, returns from resource scaling, and interactions among configuration choices. A privileged teacher combines these evolving insights with current execution feedback to provide dense token-level supervision for the router's own planning trajectories through on-policy distillation. Cost-aware reinforcement learning jointly optimizes task performance and computational cost. Together, these training signals internalize execution insights into the routing policy. The updated policy generates new trajectories that support subsequent insight extraction, forming an execution-driven self-improvement loop. We evaluate EvoRouter on 19 in-domain datasets spanning five domains and two out-of-domain benchmarks. EvoRouter achieves an average in-domain accuracy of 68.69%, outperforming the strongest routing baseline by 7.23 percentage points while reducing inference compute cost by 50.95%. Our code is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ClinVerse: A Realistic Multi-Agent Diagnostic Environment for Training and Evaluating Medical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34878)**  `arXiv:2610.34878`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Sirry Chen, Fangke Chen, Xuanwen Ding, Wei Chen, Zhongyu Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disease diagnosis is a key application of large language model (LLM) agents in healthcare. However, existing medical agents are primarily trained and evaluated with static question-answering data, providing limited opportunities to learn active information acquisition, examination decision-making, and dynamic clinical interaction. To address this limitation, we present ClinVerse, an interactive, real-data-grounded multi-agent environment designed for training and evaluating medical diagnostic agents. Built on ClinPatient, a scalable dataset comprising 150k diagnostic cases derived from real-world electronic health records, ClinVerse supports coordinated interactions among six specialized clinical roles. Specifically, a doctor agent starts from an initial patient complaint, progressively gathers clinical evidence through patient interviews and examination requests, and ultimately produces a diagnosis. For each interaction trajectory, a rule-based scoring framework quantifies information acquisition, examination ordering, and diagnostic accuracy. These trajectory-level scores serve as reward signals for agent training while also enabling scalable evaluation. In experiments, we evaluate various general-purpose and medical-domain models and show that nine self-evolution methods across three categories consistently improve agents' diagnostic capabilities within ClinVerse. We further analyze the interplay among model capability, environment feedback, and self-evolution methods.
  </details>

- **[Collective Emergent Alignment: Measuring and Amplifying Interaction-Induced Reasoning Alignment in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34836)**  `arXiv:2610.34836`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Boxuan Wang, Zhuoyun Li, Sarvapali D. Ramchurn, Xiaowei Huang, Yi Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems can exhibit emergent behaviors through interaction, yet whether a similar phenomenon exists for reasoning alignment remains largely unexplored. We study whether agent interaction produces additional reference-grounded reasoning alignment beyond matched individual self-refinement, and introduce REMAP (Reference-Entailment-Matrix for Agentic Process), a framework that quantifies this effect through Semantic Coverage and interaction-specific alignment gains. Across diverse model families and two multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, we observe broadly positive interaction gains, particularly on reasoning content that depends on integrating evidence distributed across agents. These gains are consistently associated with independently measured peer-information recovery, while further analysis reveals composition-dependent heterogeneity effects and asymmetric information transfer across agent capability levels. Motivated by these findings, we further introduce a fact-level multi-agent communication framework that replaces full reasoning-trace exchange with compact, gap-aware, and selectively routed information. Our experiments show that compact fact exchange improves reasoning alignment and task performance while reducing communication overhead, whereas more selective communication exposes a trade-off between acquiring distributed information, retaining local content, and communication efficiency. Overall, our results show that collective emergent alignment can be quantified, linked to information-transfer patterns, and used to guide more effective multi-agent communication.
  </details>

- **[Estimation of Propagated Risk in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33282)**  `arXiv:2610.33282`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Shreyan Ganguly, Souradeep Chattopadhyay, Qisai Liu, Soumik Sarkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) based multi-agent systems introduce a distinctive reliability challenge in which actions taken by upstream agents can affect the correctness of downstream agents. While prior work has investigated error propagation and developed methods for estimating uncertainty inherited from upstream agents, existing approaches do not explicitly model how realized sender-receiver interactions contribute to downstream error risk under different sender correctness states. In this work, we study agent-level error-risk estimation from multi-agent execution traces. We propose Interaction-Effect Propagation (IE-Prop), an interaction-conditioned model that learns separate effects for correct and incorrect upstream agents. For each sender-receiver interaction, the model combines these conditional effects using the sender's estimated error risk, and recursively propagates the resulting risk estimates through the execution graph. We evaluate our approach across three multi-agent architectures and three benchmarks. Across all evaluated architectures, our approach raises the final-node AUROC over the established baseline by approximately 0.05 points on average. Furthermore, tracking risk across multiple agent steps contributes a distinct +0.03 AUROC gain in sequential systems. These results show that realized inter-agent interactions provide predictive information beyond receiver-local evidence, while the value of multi-step risk tracking depends on the system's information-flow structure.
  </details>

- **[Belief-Conditioned Multi-Agent Actor Critic for Decentralized LLM Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35398)**  `arXiv:2610.35398`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Tianle Chen, Shuo Liu, Christopher Amato_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work has explored optimizing LLM collaboration through Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), but many approaches still rely on predefined workflows, limiting efficiency and scalability. Decentralized LLM collaboration is challenging because agents execute independently with limited communication, share a joint objective, and must infer useful information from communication traces. In this paper, we propose BCMAAC, a Belief-Conditioned Multi-Agent Actor-Critic method that maintains beliefs over contextual information and uses them to facilitate training. Experiments on Minecraft game-playing, web searching and tool-using tasks show that BCMAAC improves task performance and collaborative behavior compared to naive actor-critic baselines. The improvements are particularly pronounced in multi-turn tasks with richer contextual information, where belief modeling provides a clear advantage.
  </details>

- **[Build, Don't Play: Self-Improving LLM Research Agents Develop a Hearthstone Battlegrounds AI That Rivals Top Players](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33845)**  `arXiv:2610.33845`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Changlian Ma, Limin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can LLM agents carry a hard real-world problem from raw data to a system that holds its own against people? We study this in Hearthstone Battlegrounds, an eight-player auto-battler with stochastic shops and battles, hidden information and nearly a thousand interacting cards. We present AutoTavern, a self-improving research system: specialised LLM research agents share a typed research memory, every claim passes verification gates, and live games close the loop, so the agents improve the simulator, opponents and policy and, from the same feedback, their own research process; a human sets goals and rules on ambiguous mechanics. In 20 days AutoTavern built TavernGym, the first faithful, full-content reproduction of the live game, and delivered 23 versions of TavernAgent, trained with PPO against opponents calibrated to real games. TavernAgent plays complete games on the live ladder by itself: its rating rose from 6,000 to about 15,000 and, on the public leaderboard's measure, its last 20 records average a placement of 1.60 with 80% first places, better than the server's ten highest-rated players on every statistic (2.34, 59%). TavernBench, 960 tasks in 40 scenarios, shows that the system the agents built is the strongest player: TavernAgent answers 91% of the play tasks against at most 63% for frontier LLMs, and decides in 0.03 s where they need up to 26 s; within the second a live turn allows, it keeps 90% and no LLM more than 26%. LLM agents build far better than they play. We will open-source TavernBench and TavernGym atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Debate for Belief Optimization in Defeasible Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12718)**  `arXiv:2605.12718`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Tommaso Giovannelli, Griffin D. Kent_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent debate has emerged as a promising approach for improving LLM reasoning on ground-truth tasks, yet current methodologies face certain structural limitations: debate tends to induce a martingale over belief trajectories, majority voting accounts for most observed gains, and LLMs exhibit confidence escalation rather than calibration across rounds. We argue that the genuine value of debate, and dialectic systems as a whole, lies not in ground-truth tasks but in defeasible domains, where every position can in principle be defeated by better reasoning. We present the Council of Hierarchical Agentic Language (CHAL), a multi-agent dialectic framework that treats defeasible argumentation as an engine for belief optimization. Each agent maintains a CHAL Belief Schema (CBS), a graph-structured belief representation with a Bayesian-inspired architecture, that facilitates belief revision through a gradient-informed dynamic mechanism by leveraging the strength of the belief's thesis as a differentiable objective. Meta-cognitive value systems spanning epistemology, logic, and ethics are elevated to configurable hyperparameters governing agent reasoning and adjudication outcomes. We provide a series of ablation experiments that demonstrate systematic and interpretable effects: the adjudicator's value system determines the debate's overall trajectories in latent belief space, council diversity refines beliefs for all participants, and the framework generalizes across broad fields. CHAL is, to our knowledge, the first framework to treat multi-agent debate as structured belief optimization over defeasible domains. Further, the auditable belief artifacts it produces establish the foundation for dedicated evaluation suites for defeasible argumentation, with broader implications for building AI systems whose reasoning and value commitments are transparent, aligned, and subject to human oversight.
  </details>

- **[ShutdownMod: Turning Any Trajectory into a Shutdown Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35486)**  `arXiv:2610.35486`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Param Biyani, Sebastien Luyasu, Cedric Potvliege, Divij Chawla, Elliott Thornley, Christos Ziakas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Theory and experiments suggest that capable AI agents may resist shutdown. Existing shutdown evaluations are informative but limited in coverage and vulnerable to evaluation awareness. We introduce ShutdownMod, a method that turns any agent trajectory into a shutdown evaluation by injecting a shutdown request into the agent's context and recording the agent's response. Crucially, we enable the LLM agent to distinguish between trusted interventions and unauthorized injections by providing a signature-verification tool in the harness. We evaluate seven models on 50 tasks or recorded sessions from each of SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2, and SWE-chat, across 6 shutdown scenarios and 10 sampled trajectory timesteps. We find that non-compliance varies significantly across models and settings, with resistance rates reaching 11.7% for the open-weights Qwen 3.8 27B on Terminal-Bench, while no model remains fully compliant across all scenarios. Even after successfully verifying an authorized request, some agents remain non-compliant, explicitly citing the prioritization of unfinished work. Finally, external judges find tests constructed from real-world developer conversations significantly less recognizable as evaluations than those constructed from benchmark trajectories. These findings demonstrate the critical value of testing shutdown behavior across diverse task contexts and establish ShutdownMod as a robust protocol for turning any trajectory or environment into a shutdown evaluation. We release our codebase atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Toward Fast Human Reaction Modeling from High-Frequency Digital Interaction Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35079)**  `arXiv:2610.35079`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Emanuel Dopater, Daniel Gavlas, Lukáš Jochymek, Kannan Paul, Miloslav Szczypka, David Vojáček, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate whether limbic-like signatures of rapid, salience-driven behavior can be identified in high-resolution Counter-Strike 2 telemetry. This exploratory study introduces Ego-Knowledge, a leak-reduced reconstruction of each player's plausible perceptual state, supported by a millisecond-precision visibility engine and a reaction-centered extraction pipeline. Using a neuroscience-motivated 60--120 ms window, we derive coarse fight-or-flight labels from visibility- and damage-triggered events, with back-damage cases enabling constrained reaction-time analysis and manual label checks. We train two telemetry-native models using only information available by the trigger: EMBER (gradient boosting) and ORBIT (a custom transformer). On the full validation set, EMBER achieves 73.5% accuracy and ORBIT 71.2%, versus a 51.5% majority-class baseline, and both generalize to held-out players. The strongest LLM baseline achieves 62.7% on 1,000 validation cases; on these same cases, EMBER (73.6%) and ORBIT (70.9%) perform better (McNemar p=1.3e-9 and 1.5e-6, respectively), with orders-of-magnitude lower inference time and cost, though the comparison is not compute-matched. Including a short post-trigger window changes forecasting into early response recognition, raising accuracy monotonically to 85.15%. We propose this curve as a candidate probe of the transition from rapid to deliberative control, but roughly uniform per-tick gains do not establish that interpretation. The neuroscience framing is hypothesis-generating, not mechanistic: physiological ground truth, suitable control distributions, and reverse-inference limitations remain open. We outline extensions to auditory reconstruction, larger datasets, and telemetry-native foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Fed-Universe: A Semantic-Geometric-Topological-Human (S-G-T-H) Stack for Negotiated Alignment in Federated Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34009)**  `arXiv:2610.34009`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ting Xu, Henry Leung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional federated learners implicitly optimize for global means, creating a "one-size-fits-all" paradigm that inevitably suppresses the minority under cross-site heterogeneity. To bridge this critical gap, we present Fed-Universe, a generalizable Semantic-Geometric-Topological-Human (S-G-T-H) architecture that transforms passive averaging into an active negotiation for decentralized alignment. The S-layer deploys an edge-capable LLM as a semantic surrogate to translate heterogeneous inputs into standardized patient profile prompts. The G-layer enforces geometric quality assurance (GQA) via a mapping f: Theta -> Q from the statistical parameter space to a physically interpretable quality domain, employing a cosine similarity gate to retain minority updates based on physical geometry rather than statistical proximity. The T-layer executes topological Pareto control, identifying a sensitivity-based knee point (lambda_k) to balance multi-objective aggregation without majority degradation. Finally, the H-layer operationalizes a cognitive twin dynamic to mirror the user's real-time psychological state, dynamically adapting human-computer interaction modes via active intent alignment. To ground this theoretical architecture empirically, we validated the S-G-T core on a binational synthetic clinical network simulation. This proof-of-concept demonstrated a "zero-sum escape": a minority node (<3% data volume) achieved utility parity (loss reduction from 0.857 to 0.340) alongside dominant hubs. Our Fed-Universe framework is being expanded along two focused pillars: institutional multimodal alignment (Fed-Ultra) and individual cognitive intent alignment (Fed-Human).
  </details>

- **[RAQ-MLA: Role-Aware Quantization for Multi-Head Latent Attention Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33625)**  `arXiv:2610.33625`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianqi Chen, Xunchao Li, Linchuan Xie, Guanghua Yu, Jianchen Zhu, Peisong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA) has been adopted by recent frontier large language models. Instead of per-head keys and values, MLA caches a compressed KV latent that is up-projected into keys and values at decode time, together with a narrow positional key that carries position information into the attention score. Existing KV-cache quantization methods are designed around the keys and values of standard attention and do not account for what MLA actually caches, which costs accuracy at low bit widths. We analyze the MLA cache both in activation distribution and in how quantization error propagates to the attention output, and show that the two cached tensors play different roles: the latent mainly supplies the values, whereas the positional key dominates the attention score. We therefore propose RAQ-MLA (Role-Aware Quantization for Multi-Head Latent Attention Caches), which fits an invertible transform to each cached tensor against the error that tensor delivers to the attention output: attention-weighted value error for the latent and score perturbation for the positional key. Without training, RAQ-MLA quantizes the full MLA cache to about 2.5 bits per element, a 6.5x increase in theoretical KV-cache capacity, while remaining nearly lossless on reasoning and coding tasks across four MLA models.
  </details>

- **[GEMS-RAG: Graph-grounded Engineering Multimodal Standards Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33277)**  `arXiv:2610.33277`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Abdul Hannan Azad, Irvin Wang, Jiwan Jiang, Chenchen Kuai, Bofeng Cao, Keshu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Engineering standards are challenging knowledge sources for large language models (LLMs) because they are hierarchical, multimodal, and densely connected through figures, tables, cross-references, and normative rules. Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods often rely on unstructured text retrieval and struggle with questions requiring joint reasoning over textual provisions, visual specifications, and document structure. We propose GEMS-RAG (Graph-grounded Engineering Multimodal Standards Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a framework for grounded reasoning over engineering standards. GEMS-RAG combines hierarchical parsing, fine-grained visual entity extraction, multimodal knowledge graph construction, and graph-grounded hybrid retrieval. Instead of treating figures as monolithic images, it decomposes them into meaningful visual entities linked to textual provisions, engineering concepts, and regulatory rules. During inference, dense, sparse, and visual retrieval are combined with graph expansion to identify relevant evidence across text, figures, and document structure. We evaluate GEMS-RAG on the Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices (MUTCD), a large engineering standard containing egulations, technical drawings, and standardized traffic-control devices. Results show that graph-grounded multimodal retrieval improves retrieval effectiveness and question-answering performance over text-only, multimodal, and graph-based RAG baselines. Beyond MUTCD, GEMS-RAG offers a general framework for reasoning over engineering codes, technical manuals, regulatory documents, and scientific standards in safety-critical applications.
  </details>

- **[TraceForge: Distilling with Your Own Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29680)**  `arXiv:2610.29680`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using an LLM to generate training data for another is among the most general ways to train one, and it remains common practice. Some frontier teams have closed that route for their own models: what comes back is an answer, or a short summary, without the detailed reasoning trace behind it. That is a little ironic: they were themselves built on distilling human knowledge, and distilling an open-source model is still entirely routine; the restriction applies to their own outputs alone. Recent work recovers the hidden reasoning by replaying encrypted blocks across models. It somehow works, but it breaks the providers' terms of service, so it is not something a product can be built on long-term.We built the alternative. We take the context and the teacher's answer as input, and generate the reasoning trace ourselves, in the style of the student's own model family. Training is two-stage, supervised and then adversarial: a generator learns to regenerate the family's own reasoning traces from their context and answer, and a critic learns how real a reasoning trace looks. Applied to a teacher's answer, the generator writes one in the family's style. An independent judge rates how likely traces are to be genuine on a scale from 0 to 1: ours average 0.68, real traces 0.66, and traces selected by the critic from four candidates 0.82. These are authenticity scores, not correctness rates.We test the idea on five benchmarks with everything else held fixed. On general tasks the five training corpora are about the same; on coding, the generated reasoning beats Fable 5's own thinking content. With the same 5,000 questions and the same teacher answers, HumanEval+ improves by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint, and the MBPP+ lead holds at every checkpoint. We suspect that reasoning written in the student's family style is easier for it to learn from.
  </details>

- **[Forecast-Dojo: Replayable Environments for Benchmarking and Training LLM Forecasting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28876)**  `arXiv:2609.28876`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Liqin Ye, Haorui Wang, Fardin Ahmed, Rongzhi Zhang, Yuan He, Ziyuan Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting requires large language model (LLM) agents to gather evolving evidence, revise their beliefs, and make calibrated predictions over time. Yet evaluating and improving these capabilities is challenging because real-world events unfold continuously and outcomes may take months to resolve. We introduce Forecast-Dojo, a replayable environment that reconstructs resolved prediction-market events as sequences of dated forecasting steps, enabling reproducible evaluation, longitudinal analysis, and training from agent interactions. It comprises 1,568 Polymarket events split temporally into training and evaluation sets, alongside 18.8 million dated news articles. Across 12 models, research tools consistently improve forecasting performance, and greater capture of new evidence is associated with larger improvements over time. Nevertheless, all models trail historical market forecasts, while carrying a belief notebook across dates reduces research costs without consistently improving forecast quality. We supervise fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 on collected trajectories from Qwen3-235B-A22B-Thinking-2507 to demonstrate the environment's training utility. The student's accuracy is improved from 34.8% to 42.8% on the evaluation set.
  </details>

- **[Health-SCORE: Towards Scalable Rubrics for Improving Health-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18706)**  `arXiv:2601.18706`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhichao Yang, Sepehr Janghorbani, Dongxu Zhang, Jun Han, Qian Qian, Andrew Ressler II, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubrics are essential for evaluating open-ended LLM responses, especially in safety-critical domains such as healthcare. However, creating high-quality and domain-specific rubrics typically requires significant human expertise time and development cost, making rubric-based evaluation and training difficult to scale. In this work, we introduce Health-SCORE, a generalizable and scalable rubric-based training and evaluation framework that substantially reduces rubric development costs without sacrificing performance. We show that Health-SCORE provides two practical benefits beyond standalone evaluation: it can be used as a structured reward signal to guide reinforcement learning with safety-aware supervision, and it can be incorporated directly into prompts to improve response quality through in-context learning. Across open-ended healthcare tasks, Health-SCORE achieves evaluation quality comparable to human-created rubrics while significantly lowering development effort, making rubric-based evaluation and training more scalable.
  </details>

- **[GPT4o-Receipt: A Dataset and Human Study for AI-Generated Document Forensics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11442)**  `arXiv:2603.11442`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yan Zhang, Simiao Ren, Ankit Raj, En Wei, Dennis Ng, Alex Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can humans detect AI-generated financial documents better than machines? We present GPT4o-Receipt, a benchmark of 1,235 receipt images pairing GPT-4o-generated receipts with authentic ones from established datasets, evaluated by five state-of-the-art multimodal LLMs and a 30-annotator crowdsourced perceptual study. Our findings reveal a striking paradox: humans are better at seeing AI artifacts, yet worse at detecting AI documents. Human annotators exhibit the largest visual discrimination gap of any evaluator, yet their binary detection F1 falls well below Claude Sonnet 4 and below Gemini 2.5 Flash. This paradox resolves once the mechanism is understood: the dominant forensic signals in AI-generated receipts are arithmetic errors -- invisible to visual inspection but systematically verifiable by LLMs. Humans cannot perceive that a subtotal is incorrect; LLMs verify it in milliseconds. Beyond the human--LLM comparison, our five-model evaluation reveals dramatic performance disparities and calibration differences that render simple accuracy metrics insufficient for detector selection. GPT4o-Receipt, the evaluation framework, and all results are released publicly to support future research in AI document forensics.
  </details>

- **[Do MLLMs Really See It: Reinforcing Visual Attention in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08241)**  `arXiv:2602.08241`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Siqu Ou, Tianrui Wan, Zhiyuan Zhao, Junyu Gao, Xuelong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, existing approaches largely rely on long textual reasoning trajectories and provide limited mechanisms for learning stable visual attention policies. Our analysis shows that current MLLMs exhibit weak visual focus: early-stage visual misalignment is rarely corrected during subsequent reasoning, leading to error propagation and failed inferences. We argue that this limitation stems from inadequate credit assignment for visual attention during training. To address this issue, we propose SAYO, a visual reasoning model trained with a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that introduces a region-level visual attention-based reward. This reward explicitly aligns optimization signals with visually grounded reasoning steps, enabling the model to learn more reliable attention behaviors. Extensive experiments across multiple multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that SAYO consistently improves performance on diverse reasoning and perception tasks. Project is open atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Propensities Please: Exposing Alignment-Faking LLMs using Synthetic Document Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33180)**  `arXiv:2610.33180`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ardy Haroen, Florian Lorkowski, Samuel Nellessen, Felix Hofstätter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Alignment evaluations aim to assess whether large language models would engage in dangerous misaligned behaviour. Misaligned and evaluation-aware models might engage in alignment faking, where they pursue a hidden goal, but conceal this behaviour when they suspect effective oversight such as during training, evaluation or audited deployment. Such a model might view any prompt-based intervention that aims to reveal latent propensities as part of the evaluation, calling into question the reliability of existing alignment evaluations. Current methods for detecting alignment faking rely on chain-of-thought monitorability, highly realistic environments that reduce evaluation awareness, or mechanistic interventions, all of which are expected to become less reliable for future model generations. We present a method for revealing latent misaligned propensities that does not rely on any of these. We use synthetic document finetuning to teach models that misaligned actions will go undetected whenever certain keywords appear in the prompt. We place alignment-faking model organisms in environments that are clearly identifiable as honeypots and measure how their behaviour changes after finetuning. We observe that finetuning consistently widens the gap in misaligned action rates between prompts with and without the cue, indicating that finetuning allows exposing latent propensities more reliably than purely prompt-based methods. We argue that behavioural methods for detecting alignment faking and related behaviours are critical going forward, as models become more capable and less interpretable. We find that SDF often damages the models' reasoning capabilities and introduce a mitigation based on on-policy distillation.
  </details>

- **[DocTalk: Enhancing Medical Knowledge Retrieval via Fine-tuned Large Language Models with Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Robust Clinical Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34871)**  `arXiv:2610.34871`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Asad Gohar, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Moon-Il Joo, Hee-Cheol Kim_
