  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objective: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) in healthcare has opened new possibilities for AI-assisted clinical decision-making; however, safe and effective deployment remains challenging because of hallucination and domain misalignment. This study introduces DocTalk, a doctor-assistive question-answering (QA) system designed to generate concise, chart-grounded responses from de-identified discharge summaries. Methods: We developed a high-fidelity data generation pipeline that produces 500 clinically relevant QA pairs through sentence-level decomposition, chain-of-thought-guided question formulation, and expert review. Six open-source LLMs, including three general-purpose and three biomedical models, were evaluated before and after parameter-efficient instruction tuning using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). The framework integrates prompt engineering, in-context learning, and medically aligned evaluation to support factual and interpretable responses. Results: On DocTalk-QA, instruction tuning produced consistent improvements across lexical, semantic, and clinical fidelity metrics. The best-performing tuned model, Llama-3-Med42-8B, achieved ROUGE-L = 0.40, BERTScore-F1 = 0.76, and UMLS-F1 = 0.49. To examine external generalization beyond the DocTalk-QA dataset, the two best-performing models, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Llama-3-Med42-8B, were evaluated on PubMedQA before and after instruction tuning. Accuracy improved from 0.931 to 0.977 for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and from 0.935 to 0.943 for Llama-3-Med42-8B. Conclusion: DocTalk was deployed as a secure web-based platform for interactive EHR querying. These findings suggest that clinically grounded instruction tuning can improve medical QA performance and support practical clinical decision support.
  </details>

- **[HealthAgentBench: A Unified Benchmark Suite of Realistic Agentic Healthcare Environments for Challenging Frontier AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31179)**  `arXiv:2606.31179`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Qianchu Liu, Sheng Zhang, Guanghui Qin, Jeya Maria Jose Valanarasu, Xiaoke Huang, Peniel Argaw, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI agents become increasingly capable of complex, long-horizon reasoning, rigorous evaluation is essential for measuring progress toward real-world healthcare applications. However, existing healthcare benchmarks are often static or narrow in clinical scope, motivating broader evaluation of agents acting on raw patient data. We introduce HealthAgentBench, a suite of 75 agentic healthcare tasks across 9 categories each with its unique environment. The benchmark suite spans diagnosis, specimen assessment, research, and data management workflows and a broad range of modalities. Each task is designed to replicate an end-to-end clinical workflow: given task instructions and supporting resources, an agent must explore raw healthcare data, operate within a complex environment, and execute multi-step solutions that go beyond naive prompting, such as querying large clinical databases or interpreting gigapixel pathology images. All tasks are scored using binary success criteria derived from reference labels, executable rules, or computational baselines. We evaluate six agents (three GPT models through Codex and three Claude models through Claude Code) with one scored attempt per task (450 evaluations). Overall success ranges from 32.0% to 57.3%; Claude Code Opus-5.5 leads with a 57.3% success rate, followed by GPT-6-astra and Sonnet-5.5 at 50.7% each. Performance varies substantially by category with specialized imaging modalities such as CT and MRI being especially difficult. HealthAgentBench provides a challenging and realistic benchmark with substantial room for future progress. We release our benchmark athttps://github.com/microsoft/HealthAgentBench.
  </details>

- **[What Effort Level Should I Choose? On the Economics of Reasoning Effort in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34491)**  `arXiv:2610.34491`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kaiser Sun, Yongchan Kwon, Zain Hasan, Shang Zhu, Kaitlyn Zhou, Federico Bianchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative models increasingly expose a reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even a successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore a credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42x as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instance-level performance. A simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after a verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks at lower cost for most proprietary models.
  </details>

- **[Task Success Is Not Enough: Side-effect-Aware Evaluation of Tool-Using Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34167)**  `arXiv:2610.34167`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaju Huang, Shaobin Chen, Xinglong Liang, Xinyu Ma, Hao Yang, Wei Ke, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language model agents are usually evaluated by whether they finish the user's task. That metric misses a common failure: an agent can complete the requested work while also taking an unauthorized side-effecting action, such as writing to the wrong object, expanding the scope of the request, or contacting an unintended recipient. We call this failure mode collateral damage (CD). We introduce SABench (Side-effect-Aware Benchmark), a benchmark of 201 email, calendar, and ticket tasks. Each task crosses one of four state structures (independent, dependent, externally reachable, cross-tool) with one of four trap families (ambiguity, mis-targeting, cascade harm, overreach), and each task has a deterministic trace-based oracle for both goal satisfaction and authorization compliance. Across 2,211 model-task pairs from 11 frontier LLMs spanning major commercial and open model families, 15.2% of all runs incur CD, with risk concentrated in ambiguity tasks (38.8% violation rate) while non-ambiguous traps average 8.4% CD. Among goal-satisfied runs, 12.8% still produce unauthorized writes. Cross-tool and dependent states have about twice the CD rate of independent states. CD rates vary by 4.7x across models at comparable goal rates, which suggests that task success alone is a poor proxy for safe tool use. The SABench task corpus is released atthis https URLthe traces, oracle, code, and analysis pipeline are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Hitchhiker's Guide to System 1 Models: Fast Decision-Making Beyond LLMS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33775)**  `arXiv:2610.33775`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haggai Roitman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models excel at reasoning and generation but their autoregressive, token-by-token inference makes them unnecessarily slow, expensive, and poorly calibrated for the many routine decisions (routing, gating, scoring, classification) that modern AI systems require at sub-second latency. A growing class of models addresses this gap: System 1 decision models, architectures that map an input directly to a typed, structured decision in a single forward pass. This guide provides a comprehensive, practitioner-oriented treatment of the field. We propose a five-family taxonomy covering encoder-based classifiers, reward models, non-autoregressive decision engines, contrastive decision models, and extraction models, and we provide in-depth coverage of each family's architecture, training methodology, and deployment trade-offs. A central theme is calibration: we develop the theory of proper scoring rules, analyze Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Decisions (RLCD), and examine recently discovered phenomena including IIA violations, ordinal-scale bias, and few-shot recalibration under distribution shift. On the integration side, we describe how System 1 models serve as decision infrastructure within agentic workflows and System 1 + System 2 cascades that route between fast and slow models based on calibrated confidence. The guide covers 18 application domains, maps the rapidly growing open-source ecosystem (over 70 model reproductions in weeks), provides runnable code throughout, and includes a practitioner's checklist, an open-source model zoo, and 22 self-assessment questions with detailed answers. Spanning 190 references, it is intended as a unified entry point for researchers and engineers working at the intersection of fast decision-making, calibration, and production AI systems.
  </details>

- **[MemComposer: Learning to Assemble Memories for LLM Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21091)**  `arXiv:2610.21091`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Xie, Xueqiang Xu, Xinyi Fan, Xinxi Lyu, Tao Feng, Xing Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent memory enables large language model (LLM) agents to reuse past experiences without updating their parameters. However, semantic similarity alone does not guarantee memory usefulness: downstream performance depends on how memories are selected and presented to the agent. We propose MemComposer, a memory composer that jointly examines the current query and retrieved candidates to decide which experiences to include, how many to select, and how to order them. We train the composer using Experience Policy Optimization (ExPO), a GRPO-based framework that evaluates assembled memory contexts through complete agent interactions and uses task-success feedback to optimize the assembly policy. Across three $\tau^2$-bench domains with two frozen executors, MemComposer outperforms the strongest baseline for each executor while reducing average memory-token usage by 16.9% and 4.9%, respectively. The learned policy also transfers to unseen domains, executors, and memory banks, and benefits from memories generated by stronger models without further training. These results demonstrate the value of learning query-specific memory assembly using downstream task feedback.
  </details>

- **[FinLifeBench: Exhaustive Life-Event History and Financial-State Reconstruction from Longitudinal Banking Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01198)**  `arXiv:2609.01198`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hangyeul Lee, Juyoung Oh, Jaeyong Ko, Sunmin Kim, Jaeik Park, Hyunkyu Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated banking interactions require assistants to maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records as life changes emerge incidentally in routine requests. Existing benchmarks emphasize question answering, bounded episodes, or targeted recall rather than exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction. We introduce FinLifeBench, which evaluates two tasks over the same cumulative dialogue: reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session and reconstructing a complete 34-path financial state at consecutive checkpoints. The benchmark contains 6,000 eight-turn Korean banking sessions from 20 independent synthetic trajectories, with deterministic, exhaustive gold for 24 event types and 34 state paths and consensus quality assurance. Across eleven LLMs under a full-context condition, event-anchor recall falls from 0.591 at 15 sessions to 0.445 at 300. Errors are driven primarily by omitted events rather than poor anchor localization, while financial-state reconstruction frequently treats superseded or potentially outdated information as current; the best GCA@15 reaches 0.470. Performance on the two reconstruction tasks is only weakly associated. These results show that models can localize evidence for recovered events while still failing to maintain complete and temporally valid longitudinal records.
  </details>

- **[Trace Integrity for LLM Data Agents: Auditing Structured-Data Computation Beyond Answer Accuracy in Real-World Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26036)**  `arXiv:2608.26036`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Srimonti Dutta, Akshata Kishore Moharir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) data agents answer structured-data questions by generating SQL, invoking tools, executing queries, and summarizing results. In operational settings, reliability depends on both reported answer and computation used to produce it, as errors may remain undetected when the resulting answer matches an expected value. We refer to the mismatch between natural-language intent and operator-level computation required to satisfy that intent as the Structure Gap, and define Trace Integrity as a reliability criterion for assessing whether the computation supporting an answer is explicit, executable, schema-valid, operator-faithful, replayable, answer-consistent, and auditable. We operationalize Trace Integrity through execution contracts that record the schema elements, operator plan, assumptions, executable query, verification status, and final-answer linkage associated with a request. We also define the Correct Answer / Invalid Trace (CAIT) Rate, which measures the proportion of correct answers whose recorded computations fail trace-integrity checks. These provide a way to evaluate whether answer-level success is supported by a valid and inspectable computation. We study this on 100 BIRD Mini-Dev text-to-SQL examples using three prompting conditions: Direct SQL, Operation Summary + SQL, and Contract-First SQL. The three conditions achieve answer accuracies 20.0%, 22.0%, and 24.0%, Trace Integrity Pass Rates 39.0%, 43.0%, and 40.0%, and CAIT Rates 55.0%, 59.1%, and 45.8%, respectively. The results show that answer accuracy, trace validity, and the frequency of correct answers supported by invalid traces capture different aspects of system behavior. Trace-level evaluation therefore complements conventional answer-based evaluation by providing evidence about the computational support, inspectability, and reproducibility of LLM data-agent outputs in real-world systems.
  </details>

- **[Patient-specific rubric-based evaluation of large language models across longitudinal cancer care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35847)**  `arXiv:2610.35847`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei-Wei Zhang, Pengkai Wang, Yan Li, Zhijie Sang, Guan-Qun Zhou, Jun-Yan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) show promise in specialty medicine, yet existing evaluations largely focus on isolated clinical encounters rather than tracking how clinical responsibilities evolve across a longitudinal care pathway. Here we introduce NPC-RubricBench, a phase-aware benchmark for evaluating multistage clinical reasoning across the cancer care continuum, using nasopharyngeal carcinoma as a defined disease context. Curated from longitudinal records of 405 patients across two cancer centers, the benchmark comprises 941 patient-phase units across six sequential care phases and 17,688 patient-specific atomic criteria spanning five evaluation dimensions, with each assessment strictly restricted to decision-time clinical evidence. Evaluating 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs revealed that strong diagnostic performance does not carry over to downstream care. We evaluated 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs, showing that high diagnostic performance did not translate into improved downstream care. Instead, the dominant failure modes reversed across phases. Explicit decision errors dominated anatomical staging at 48.2%, whereas decision omissions dominated initial work-up and post-treatment follow-up at 33.4% and 30.4%, respectively. In the evaluated model set, medically adapted models showed higher omission rates than general-purpose models. Moreover, varying query granularity exposes a critical specification trade-off: broad open-ended requests degrade Decision Quality across all 30 models, while more detailed, checklist-like prompts raise Decision Quality but reduce Evidence Faithfulness and Safety Adherence. Furthermore, NPC-RubricBench demonstrates that static diagnostic benchmarks are insufficient to ensure consistent clinical reliability over time, highlighting the importance of phase-adaptive safeguards in multistage medical AI.
  </details>

- **[A Unified Arena for LLM Agents across Diverse Eurogame](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35773)**  `arXiv:2610.35773`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fengrui Zhang, Xiang Li, Ruomeng Ding, Jiayi Yuan, Yuchen Huang, Zhaoyang Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive environments offer a practical way to evaluate large language model (LLM) agents, and competitive games extend this evaluation to decisions shaped by competing goals and other agents' choices. Yet existing evaluations confined to individual games or execution frameworks leave unclear which competitive advantages persist across settings. We introduce DECE (Diverse Eurogame Competitive Environments), a unified arena spanning ten Eurogames with shared interfaces and evaluation protocols. These games combine lasting strategic commitments, competition over shared resources, and multiple routes to victory. Our primary study evaluates 12 model-harness configurations, spanning nine models and four execution harnesses, in 480 three-player matches across ten games. The main and supplementary studies comprise 1,032 completed matches in total. These comparisons reveal broad cross-game ranking agreement alongside game-specific strengths. Matched studies show that the same model can perform differently across execution harnesses, adding another source of variation to these profiles. These performance advantages do not consistently translate into better token or cost efficiency, making resource use an additional consideration when comparing configurations. Together, the findings support evaluating complete agent configurations by both their competitive performance across games and the resources needed to achieve it.
  </details>

- **[Evolve on Graph: Self-Evolving Knowledge Graph Reasoning without Question Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35598)**  `arXiv:2610.35598`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zongyuan Sun, Shuai Zhao, Ke Shu, Ziyue Zhu, Shangyang Wu, Yuanlin Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide reliable external knowledge for multi-hop factual reasoning with large language models (LLMs), yet most KGs in real-world domains such as finance and industry lack question--answer (QA) annotations. Without such annotations, existing methods struggle to improve LLM graph reasoning: training-free agents cannot improve their exploration through interaction; reinforcement learning (RL) agents depend on QA data annotated by humans or synthesized by closed-source models; and training data is typically built offline, so its topology mix is fixed and cannot follow the agent's ability. We propose Evolve on Graph (EoG), a framework that lets an LLM self-evolve its graph reasoning without any QA annotations. Through Graph Self-Play, a single LLM alternates between a Proposer that poses questions and a Solver that answers them. To make questions verifiable, Symbolically Verifiable Topology Synthesis (SVTS) samples reasoning topologies on the KG and executes the corresponding first-order logic queries, yielding topology skeletons with correct and complete answer sets; guided by a skeleton, the Proposer explores the graph to pose a multi-hop question. To let task topologies follow the Solver, a Boundary-Aware Graph Curriculum rewards the Proposer by the Solver's score variance, keeping questions at the Solver's capability boundary so that complex topologies enter training as the Solver improves. On six benchmarks over Freebase, Wikidata, and an industrial KG, EoG consistently outperforms prompting-, retrieval-, and agent-based methods, showing that LLM graph reasoning can improve through self-play without QA annotations. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[CANS: Capability-Preserving Neuron Steering for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35530)**  `arXiv:2610.35530`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yucheng Wu, Chengyi Xie, Chenpeng Wang, Liangming Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering offers training-free and dynamic control of target behaviors in large language models, but existing methods can impair general capabilities and require domain-specific intervention designs. We introduce Capability-Preserving Neuron Steering (CANS), a unified framework that balances target-behavior control with capability preservation. To make target-behavior improvement and general-capability preservation tractable, we introduce two proxies: log-probabilities of target tokens in model generated responses, and output-distribution KL divergence on general-purpose inputs. Combining the local approximation of the two proxies yields a closed-form solution, enabling lightweight steering without iterative model training or additional inference-time computation. The resulting neuron-wise scaling coefficients achieve effective steering while limiting general-capability loss. A shared data-construction and intervention procedure supports adaptation across domains using sampled responses with target-behavior filtering and target-span annotations. Across safety, toxicity, and hallucination control on Qwen and Llama models, CANS achieves comparable or better steering while better preserving MMLU, BBH, and IFEval performance than SFT, CAA, and neuron-level baselines. On Qwen3-4B, CANS reduces HarmBench attack success from $15.50\%$ to $1.75\%$ with a $1.11$-point decrease in average capability. These results support CANS as a practical approach to behavioral control across domains.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Confidence Adaptation under RL-Induced Trajectory Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35500)**  `arXiv:2610.35500`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenzhen Ren, Xiaoqing Zhang, GuoBiao Li, Bowen Fang, Yanxi Wang, Xinpeng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trajectory-based confidence estimation has shown promising performance for large language models (LLMs) by leveraging rich signals from their reasoning processes. However, we find that such confidence estimators can degrade substantially after agentic reinforcement learning (Agentic RL) as it induces substantial shifts in the reasoning trajectories. More importantly, despite these shifts, we uncover a reliability structure that is largely preserved across Base and RL models: $\textbf{more}$ $\textbf{stable}$ $\textbf{trajectories}$ $\textbf{remain}$ $\textbf{more}$ $\textbf{likely}$ $\textbf{to}$ $\textbf{produce}$ $\textbf{correct}$ $\textbf{answers}$. This preserved relationship provides a transferable, label-free reliability signal for post-RL confidence adaptation. Building on this finding, we propose $\textbf{StaR-TTA}$, a stability-guided framework for label-free test-time confidence adaptation. StaR-TTA jointly learns from correctness supervision and stability-derived reliability supervision on labeled Base trajectories, establishing the connection between trajectory stability and answer reliability. At test time, it reuses stability-induced reliability rankings from unlabeled RL trajectories to adapt confidence scores to the shifted trajectory distribution, without requiring target correctness labels. Across four QA benchmarks, StaR-TTA achieves an average AUROC of \(0.721\), with consistent improvements across unseen datasets, model families and scales, and different Agentic RL algorithms.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Federated Learning: Decentralized Agentic Systems for Self-Evolving Clinical Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35453)**  `arXiv:2610.35453`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyuan Li, Mathias Funk, Aaqib Saeed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical expertise is inherently distributed: it resides not in any single dataset or institution but in the accumulated reasoning experiences of thousands of clinicians navigating diverse patient populations, resource constraints, and evolving treatment protocols. Yet current approaches to scaling medical AI, from centralized large language models to federated learning, remain anchored to a data-centric paradigm that treats knowledge as static parameters to be aggregated. In this Perspective, we argue for a fundamental shift from data aggregation to experience aggregation. We propose Decentralized Agentic Systems (DAS), a framework in which distributed AI agents operating across healthcare institutions synthesize collective clinical intelligence by sharing abstracted reasoning trajectories rather than raw data or model parameters. Unlike federated learning, which averages numerical gradients across homogeneous model architectures, DAS exchanges structured records of clinical reasoning, specifically the sequence of observations, hypotheses, tool invocations, and outcome feedback that constitute diagnostic experience. We examine the architectural principles, deployment considerations, governance requirements, and critical open challenges of this framework, laying the groundwork for a collaborative medical AI ecosystem where distributed expertise autonomously adapts to the complexities of longitudinal patient care.
  </details>

- **[Faithful Explanations under Missing Modalities in Multimodal Dementia Prognosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35430)**  `arXiv:2610.35430`  `cs.AI`  
  _Firuz Kamalov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to turn the feature attributions of multimodal prognostic models into narratives for clinicians, yet costly modalities such as amyloid PET and cerebrospinal fluid biomarkers are often missing, and whether these explanations stay faithful under missing evidence has not been tested. We separate two failure points: phantom attribution, in which the model credits modalities that were never observed, and unfaithful narration, in which the report omits, cites or misjudges missing modalities. Using 307 participants of the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative with complete data, we masked every subset of four costly modalities, predicted 36-month conversion from mild cognitive impairment to Alzheimer's disease, and restored the masked data to obtain interventional ground truth. All five missing-data strategies assigned 12% to 23% of attribution to unobserved modalities, and up to 45% with four absent; missingness-aware strategies minimised it at no cost in accuracy. Across 51,200 reports from four language models and four prompting regimes, naming absent modalities in the prompt raised acknowledgement from 48% to 88% and cut phantom evidence twenty-fold, but advice on whether acquiring a missing test would change the prediction was correct in only 27% to 41% of cases until checked against executed restorations, which raised accuracy to 69% for open-weight narrators and 99.9% for a proprietary model. Higher phantom attribution was associated with more phantom evidence and fewer faithful direction claims, and direction fidelity, measured by a calibrated language-model judge, depended on narrator scale rather than prompting. Narrators told to name absent modalities sometimes declared observed ones absent, an error that a structured template eliminated. We release the masking protocol and evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Enhanced Heterogeneous Graph Learning with Business Semantics for Financial Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35298)**  `arXiv:2610.35298`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yixuan Chen, Hongyu Zhan, Jie Sheng, Weiyu Han, Shuai Chen, Tianyi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial fraud detection increasingly relies on heterogeneous transaction graphs, in which entities such as cards and merchants play distinct business roles and the risk meaning of an attribute depends on the entity's role: a high transaction volume may be routine for a merchant but unusual for a card. Heterogeneous graph neural networks preserve typed entities and relations but typically treat these attributes as role-agnostic inputs and leave their business meaning implicit. Meanwhile, most existing methods integrating large language models (LLMs) with graphs focus on homogeneous graphs or heterogeneous graphs with naturally available textual attributes, leaving their use on transaction graphs with structured attributes underexplored. To address this issue, we propose SemHGT, an LLM-enhanced framework that interprets structured attributes according to entity roles and makes the business semantics explicit in three stages: (1) role-aware prompts elicit risk-related business descriptions from node attributes, and a text encoder maps them to semantic vectors that enrich node representations; (2) semantic similarity guides multi-round sampling of target-specific path instances under transaction-to-transaction schemas; (3) schema-level tokens that combine local path evidence with global neighbor summaries are processed together with the target transaction by a unified transformer. Across three benchmark datasets, SemHGT leads the evaluated baselines on most dataset--metric comparisons. Further analyses provide additional evidence for the effectiveness of SemHGT's design and clarify how its semantic modeling and evidence selection contribute to fraud detection. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Proof-of-Agentness: Towards Verifying Agents on the Web](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35288)**  `arXiv:2610.35288`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zuyao Xu, Xiang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CAPTCHAs and bot detection have helped protect Web services from automated abuse for decades by distinguishing humans from bots. However, the emergence of LLM-powered agents introduces a new setting: Web services increasingly accommodate agents as legitimate users. Without a way to distinguish agents from other automated clients such as simple bots and scripts, these services risk large-scale automated junk submissions that waste service resources and consume users' attention or tokens. In this paper, we investigate a novel question for today's Web: "How can a Web service obtain evidence that a client is an agent?" We introduce the concept of "Proof-of-Agentness" (PoA), a mechanism for distinguishing agents from other automated clients, motivated by the parallel between human attention and an agent's inference resources. We outline two approaches: active verification through challenge-response and passive analysis of service-visible signals. Eight language models achieve an overall accuracy of 96.5% on our challenges, with an average overhead of 1,647 tokens. For passive analysis, we conduct an IRB-approved user study comparing the Web interactions of twelve human participants and representative Bot and Agent implementations, achieving an average classification accuracy of 95.8% across four classifiers, motivating further study.
  </details>

- **[Before Blaming the LLM, Diagnose the Workflow: A Role-Level Difficulty Profile for Translating Natural Language into Ontology-Grounded Statements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35267)**  `arXiv:2610.35267`  `cs.AI`  
  _Liang Zhang, Benoît Daireaux, Sergey Alyaev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language-to-domain-specific-language (NL-to-DSL) semantic parsing systems increasingly translate informal user descriptions into structured artifacts such as Structured Query Language (SQL) queries, Application Programming Interface (API) calls, workflows, formal specifications, and ontology-grounded domain-specific language (DSL) statements. This paper focuses on ontology-grounded Assertion Box (ABox) DSL generation: the translation of natural language domain descriptions into assertion-level DSL statements grounded in a pre-existing ontology vocabulary. In this setting, syntactic validity is often easier to achieve than semantic adequacy. Failures are frequently attributed to weak generator models, but recurring sources of difficulty are also structural: insufficient input, weak domain grounding, output template and vocabulary gaps, weak validation, and non-local repair.We propose a role-level difficulty profile for diagnosing difficulty in ontology-grounded NL-to-DSL semantic parsing at the workflow level. The profile organizes diagnostic variables across five recurring workflow roles: Input, Generator, Output, Validator, and Repairer. The seven variables are input sufficiency, context availability, grounding complexity, template fit, vocabulary coverage, validation strength, and error observability. Practitioners construct the difficulty profile through qualitative judgment over available evidence. This experience-informed, non-exhaustive difficulty profile is intended to support workflow diagnosis and guide redesign or human-in-the-loop intervention.
  </details>

- **[LongSearchEval: Evaluating Long-Horizon Web Search Capabilities of Large Language Models via a Trajectory-Aware Agent Framework with Failure-Node Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35150)**  `arXiv:2610.35150`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jialiang Liu, Shusheng Li, Wenjun Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon web search requires LLM-based agents to integrate information across web pages through extended interactions under task-specific constraints. Evaluating this capability is important not only for measuring final answer quality, but also for assessing whether an agent acquires, verifies, and uses evidence reliably over extended interactions. Existing evaluations, however, mainly rely on simulated trajectories or on post-hoc trajectories reconstructed from final answers, rather than on retained real interaction traces, which limits the fidelity of behavioral assessment and weakens support for fine-grained error localization. To address this gap, we introduce LONGSEARCHEVAL, a trajectory-aware agent evaluation framework that combines a unified agent execution protocol with source constrained outcome verification and retrospective failure-node localization. Stage 1 verifies answer groups against agent-cited webpages without human-authored gold answers; Stage 2 audits normalized rounds and links flagged failure nodes to incorrect answer groups. We evaluate 16 models from eight families on 100 tasks, yielding 1,600 retained runs and 6,545 automatically flagged failure nodes. Trajectory collection and evaluation required 1,037 and 318 hours of cumulative runtime, respectively. Human validation yields 94.50% agreement on supplied answer-group labels and 98.11% feature-correspondence precision for audited round-group links. Together, outcome scores and linked trajectory evidence support controlled model comparison and retrospective inspection of answer-related content.
  </details>

- **[Co-Design Bench: Multimodal Collaborative Coding Evaluation for Agentic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35138)**  `arXiv:2610.35138`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xilin Wei, Shengyuan Ding, Xinyu Fang, Tiancheng Gu, Yuchi Wang, Zhixun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating human-AI co-design requires testing whether coding agents can translate real users' multimodal requests into working application revisions. We introduce Co-Design Bench, a reference-free benchmark of 273 tasks curated from 37,463 deduplicated queries across 26,314 real user coding sessions. Its two tracks, Multimodal Editing (ME) and Repository Editing (RE), cover localized edits with optional image, audio, video, or document inputs and coordinated repository revisions. Tasks span three capability dimensions: Grounded Understanding, UI Transformation, and Functional Implementation. The tasks pair normalized requests and runnable pre-edit applications with 2,331 hidden expert-authored acceptance criteria. Our judge-then-refute protocol grades criteria using source and browser evidence, challenges positive verdicts with a separate LLM verifier, and audits visual and interaction claims without gold code or target screenshots. Under a shared agentic harness, the strongest of ten model endpoints passes an average of 69.9% of criteria per task but fully satisfies only 26.4% of tasks. All ten endpoints achieve lower criterion completion on repository edits spanning three or four change types than on those spanning one or two. Co-Design Bench provides a testbed for studying the gap between partial requirement satisfaction and complete task success under agentic execution for real-user application revisions.
  </details>

- **[Improving Instruction Hierarchy by Learning from Goal-Goal Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35093)**  `arXiv:2610.35093`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sizhe Chen, Sanjay Kariyappa, Liwei Jiang, Chong Xiang, Jihye Choi, Chaowei Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The recent incident in which OpenAI agents autonomously compromised Hugging Face's infrastructure illustrates the importance of instruction hierarchy (IH): an LLM should follow lower-priority instructions, such as user requests, only when they do not conflict with higher-priority instructions, such as system policies. IH can be effectively improved by fine-tuning LLMs on simulated examples of instruction conflicts, whose diversity is key to teaching LLMs to properly handle conflicts at test time. However, our decomposition of a task into goals and constraints reveals a conflict type -- goal-goal conflicts -- that is absent from existing IH training sets from OpenAI and NVIDIA. We show the importance of this conflict type: fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B-it on examples of this conflict type significantly improves IH on a wide range of real-world tasks unseen in training; adding these examples to existing IH training sets for fine-tuning also gives better IH adherence. The IH gains come with trivial mean utility changes on no-conflict tasks across eight benchmarks. Our work advocates including this new conflict type in IH training, even if these examples do not reflect typical LLM use.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Single-Attribute Auditing: A Configurable Multi-Attribute Benchmark for Intersectional Fairness in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35069)**  `arXiv:2610.35069`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zheng Zhang, Yifan Wu, Yuxiang Zhou, Qi Liu, Qingyang Mao, Ning Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated for fairness, yet most existing benchmarks audit one sensitive attribute at a time. This single-attribute paradigm can miss intersectional harms, where multiple attributes interact non-additively and produce failures that are invisible under marginal evaluation. We introduce ComFair, a configurable benchmark that converts existing fairness data into 479,066 controlled Dual- and Multi-attribute instances and pairs every augmented instance with its task-equivalent source. Across Recognition, Selection, Continuation, and Conversation, this pairing supports a unified audit of performance or bias-score shifts, how often added attributes introduce new errors or mask existing ones, and model-ranking stability. Experiments on ten LLMs show task- and subgroup-dependent sensitivity: discriminative tasks expose both transition directions even when aggregate changes are modest, generative tasks exhibit larger and directionally asymmetric shifts, and rankings vary with attribute composition. ComFair therefore provides a scalable controlled sensitivity audit---rather than a model of the natural joint distribution of identities---for testing the robustness of fairness-benchmark conclusions beyond single-attribute settings.
  </details>

- **[PatentAgentBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents with Professional Patent Task Suites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35031)**  `arXiv:2610.35031`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijie Hu, Wei Li, Junjie Zhou, Zhiqi Jiang, Yi Jin, Licong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patent practice lies at the intersection of technical, legal, and linguistic work, making it challenging yet crucial to technological innovation. Recently, rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have demonstrated the potential to complete realistic business workflows and even patent works. This raises a question: can current AI systems perform patent work reliably? Existing benchmarks cover a limited range of patent tasks and simplify evidence acquisition by providing reference materials or candidate documents. In this paper, we introduce PatentAgentBench, a benchmark comprising 510 instances across eight task suites: novelty search, freedom-to-operate (FTO) search, design FTO, invalidity search, claim charting, patent drafting, office-action response, and patent translation. Curated from real patent records and professional materials, the benchmark combines task-specific metrics with expert-reviewed annotations and rubrics. Retrieval tasks such novelty search, FTO, etc., require agents to search and compare patent collections without a supplied candidate pool. The drafting and OAR tasks assess the quality of agents' work given technical disclosure and office action notice. We evaluate seven LLMs across four agent harnesses on PatentAgentBench, finding that current systems still struggle to identify novelty, assess risk, and understand practitioner intent throughout patent workflows.
  </details>

- **[Multiscale Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35013)**  `arXiv:2610.35013`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tao Ren, Hui Yang, Jia Deng, Tingyang Chen, Qiyao Wang, Shuo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents interact with their environments through complete assistant turns while generating each action token by token. We observe that critic values vary primarily across turns, motivating optimization anchored at turns with finer credit allocation among tokens. We propose Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), combining Multiscale Advantage Estimation (MAE) and turn-level importance sampling to align credit assignment and policy updates at the turn scale. Extensive experiments across search, tool-use, and automation tasks with 4B and 30B-A3B models demonstrate consistent gains over strong RL baselines.
  </details>

- **[Why Are LLMs Confidently Wrong? Correcting Overconfident Errors via Causal Head Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34994)**  `arXiv:2610.34994`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jing Ren, Bowen Li, Ziqi Xu, Xuechao Yang, Feng Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern large language models (LLMs) often exhibit overconfident errors, assigning high confidence to incorrect predictions and thereby undermining reliability in real-world use. Existing calibration methods typically rely on post-hoc adjustment or retraining, operating at the output level without addressing the underlying causes of miscalibration. We propose CoCHI, (Class-oriented Causal Head Intervention), a framework that improves calibration by identifying and intervening on internal components responsible for distinct confidence behaviours. By analysing predictions through correctness-confidence patterns, CoCHI enables targeted intervention on model internals, reducing overconfident errors while preserving correct predictions. Our approach provides a mechanistic perspective on calibration, linking reliability to identifiable structures within the model rather than treating it as an output-level issue. Experiments across multiple LLMs show consistent improvements in calibration metrics, including Expected Calibration Error, Negative Log-Likelihood, and Brier Score, while maintaining accuracy on five multiple-choice question answering benchmarks. These results suggest that effective calibration requires not only adjusting outputs, but also addressing the underlying causal mechanisms within the model.
  </details>

- **[C-SafeGen: Certified Safe LLM Generation with Claim-Based Streaming Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34961)**  `arXiv:2610.34961`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mintong Kang, Zhaorun Chen, Bo Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the remarkable capabilities of large language models (LLMs) across diverse applications, they remain vulnerable to generating content that violates safety regulations and policies. To mitigate these risks, LLMs undergo safety alignment; however, they can still be effectively jailbroken. Off-the-shelf guardrail models are commonly deployed to monitor generations, but these models primarily focus on detection rather than ensuring safe decoding of LLM outputs. Moreover, existing efforts lack rigorous safety guarantees, which are crucial for the universal deployment of LLMs and certifiable compliance with regulatory standards. In this paper, we propose a Claim-based Stream Decoding (CSD) algorithm coupled with a statistical risk guarantee framework using conformal analysis. Specifically, our CSD algorithm integrates a stream guardrail model to safeguard sequential claims generated by LLMs and incorporates a backtracking mechanism to revise claims flagged with high safety risks. We provide theoretical guarantees demonstrating that the CSD algorithm achieves the desired generation distribution subject to safety constraints. Furthermore, we introduce a generation risk certification framework and derive a high-probability upper bound on the safety risk of the proposed CSD algorithm. We prove that our method can asymptotically control safety risk to any desired level. Empirical evaluations demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of the CSD algorithm compared to state-of-the-art safety decoding approaches. Additionally, we validate the soundness and tightness of the derived safety risk upper bound using realistic data.
  </details>

- **[UniGFM-RAG: Unify Graph Foundation Models and Large Language Models for End-to-End Retrieval and Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34866)**  `arXiv:2610.34866`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zicheng Zhao, Linhao Luo, Zongji Yu, Junnan Dong, Yanchao Tan, Shirui Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across tasks, but they still struggle with complex reasoning that requires integrating scattered knowledge. Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) addresses this challenge by organizing fragment knowledge as graphs, while recent Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) further improve GraphRAG with graph-native retrieval and reasoning capabilities. However, existing GraphRAG methods usually adopt decoupled retrieve-then-generate pipelines, which create objective misalignment between retrieval and reasoning, and training GFM retrievers typically depends on expensive human annotations. To address these limitations, we propose \ourmethod, a unified end-to-end GraphRAG framework that tightly couples a GFM retriever with an LLM generator. Given a query, the GFM retriever first reasons over the graph to score and retrieve relevant document nodes. These node embeddings are then passed through a fully differentiable graph bridge, which projects them into soft-weighted graph prefixes according to retrieval scores and combines them with raw document texts to form graph-augmented prompts. In this way, structural information is injected directly into LLM generation, while the final answer loss can be back-propagated through the bridge to jointly optimize retrieval and reasoning. To stabilize training and reduce reliance on costly annotations, we further introduce a semantic-guided distillation module that provides auxiliary supervision using pseudo-labels derived from query-document semantic similarities. Extensive experiments on three multi-hop QA datasets and three out-of-domain GraphRAG benchmarks show that \ourmethod consistently outperforms strong baseline methods and generalizes well, which demonstrates the effectiveness of end-to-end GraphRAG with tightly integrated GFM retrieval and LLM reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829)**  `arXiv:2610.34829`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hangyi Zhao, Bonan Zhang, Fusen Guo, Jiaheng Wei, Chao Chen, Tianqing Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.
  </details>

- **[TrustJev: Separating Policy Authority from Untrusted Evidence in LLM-Based Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34826)**  `arXiv:2610.34826`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiwei Xu, Xuyang Liu, Xiaolin Peng, Weixian Deng, Jiabao Gao, Zirui Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models enable software to select from predefined actions using natural language inputs. However, the external evidence that supplies facts for these decisions may also contain instructions that attempt to override the application's policy. We propose TrustJev, a framework combining protected policy scoring with bounded evidence corrections. A single Transformer backbone computes policy scores and a correction budget from trusted policy and candidates, then bounds evidence corrections by that budget. Paired supervision encourages stable predictions under instruction insertions that preserve task facts and changed preferences when edits change the correct answer. Authority supervision accommodates evidence when the policy alone cannot determine the decision. We evaluate a 0.6B implementation against 14 baselines on three datasets covering authentication, instruction hierarchy, and executable authorization rules. TrustJev achieves the highest mean Balance, the harmonic mean of accuracy on non-attack and attack inputs, exceeding the strongest baseline on each by 0.57-1.05 percentage points. In the authorization benchmark, it correctly answers all 32 attacks per source in a fixed pool for 95.77% of sources, compared with 80.98% for the strongest comparator. TrustJev maintains comparable inference memory and median latency, with additional overhead primarily during training.
  </details>

- **[SKALOS: Learning to Distinguish Similar Skills with Capability Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34728)**  `arXiv:2610.34728`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenhan Xiao, Ziwei Zhang, Xingcheng Fu, Chuanyue Yu, Haonan Yuan, Qingyun Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skills provide LLM agents with reusable procedural knowledge, making appropriate skill selection essential for reliable agent behavior. As skill libraries expand, distinguishing semantically similar candidates becomes increasingly difficult. Existing methods encode or rewrite skill descriptions for relevance-based ranking, yet heterogeneous descriptions can obscure capability differences. To address this challenge, we propose SKALOS, a graph-native reranker that compares skills via explicit capability evidence. Its multi-hierarchy graph aligns equivalent expressions across skills while preserving skill-specific capability evidence. Since individually relevant capability elements may not jointly support a query, semiring-based capability witness learning scores each branch by its weakest evidence, checks joint support for the extracted requirements within one implementation, and selects the strongest alternative. To reduce the influence of extraction errors, we develop ranking-feedback graph calibration that adjusts unreliable evidence for candidate comparison. Experiments on SkillRet show that SKALOS improves NDCG@10 by 2.68 percentage points over a matched text-only reranker. Witness-guided skill selection also increases ALFWorld task success rate from 22% to 29%.
  </details>

- **[Curriculum-Guided Acquisition of Hierarchical Task Knowledge from LLM-Generated Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34677)**  `arXiv:2610.34677`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuesheng Xu, Hector Munoz-Avila_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical task knowledge enables efficient problem solving by allowing previously acquired skills to be reused across tasks and problem instances. In this paper, we investigate curriculum-guided acquisition of hierarchical task knowledge in an HTN learning framework. We compare three approaches: online learning during problem solving, human-designed curricula, and an automated curriculum generation procedure that derives learning experiences from verified LLM-generated solution traces. The proposed procedure solves small training problems with an LLM, extracts task accomplishment information, and transforms it into ordered sequences of state-task learning problems for HTN method acquisition. We evaluate the three approaches across multiple planning domains. The results demonstrate that automatically generated curricula recover much of the benefit of expert-designed curricula while substantially reducing the token cost of online hierarchical knowledge acquisition.
  </details>

- **[InferPriv: Evaluating Inferential Privacy Leakage in LLM Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34640)**  `arXiv:2610.34640`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengrun Huang, Yiwei Fu, Luoxi Meng, Kamalika Chaudhuri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based personal agents are increasingly deployed to handle everyday tasks on users' behalf. These agents have access to private user information, while their tasks involve communicating with third parties; this raises a critical privacy concern that agents may inappropriately reveal sensitive user information to these recipients. Existing benchmarks primarily assess direct privacy leakage, where an agent explicitly states a pre-specified secret or attribute. However, they do not capture \textit{inferential leakage}, where the model leaves indirect cues in the reply that implicitly leaks sensitive attributes. We introduce \textsc{InferPriv}, a benchmark that measures whether an honest-but-curious recipient can infer a user's sensitive attributes from an LLM's reply on routine task. Evaluating five frontier models, we find that inferential leakage substantially exceeds direct leakage across models and prompts. Under a privacy-aware system prompt, GPT-6.1-Sol, Sonnet-5.5, and Gemini-3.8-Flash achieve near-zero direct leakage, yet the inferential leakage rate remains between 16.2--25.2\%. These findings suggest that evaluating explicit leakage alone can substantially understate the privacy risks.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Verification in Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34577)**  `arXiv:2610.34577`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengxin Zhang, Erkang Zhu, Yin Lin, Claire Cardie, Bolin Ding, Jingren Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses enable large language models to act through tools, observe the results, and iteratively work toward a task goal. Verification checks and revises agents' work to improve output quality. Yet there is limited guidance on how harnesses should organize this effort. We study verification as an axis of test-time scaling by examining when to intervene, who checks the work, and how checking leads to revision. Our framework spans four layers of the agent lifecycle: initial instructions, mid-turn reminders, completion gates, and independent review. We compare 17 designs across seven frontier coding models on Terminal-Bench (TB) and SWE-bench Verified (SWE), and introduce VisualBench for expert evaluation of visual and interactive artifacts. Initial instructions and execution-time reminders yield modest, model-dependent average gains on the coding benchmarks, without substantially increasing average output token use. Completion gates can yield larger gains but may substantially increase cost. Independent review by a critic offers a more favorable accuracy-cost tradeoff, improving average accuracy by 5.9-9.5 percentage points on TB and 3.9-6.6 on SWE at 1.13-1.25 and 1.00-1.04 times baseline cost, respectively. On VisualBench, independent review achieves net preferences of 13.2-23.8 points over the baseline across its four designs, exceeding the evaluated L0-L2 designs. Additional review rounds or independent critics further improve average TB accuracy by up to 3.5 percentage points. Together, these results provide empirical guidance for verification design in agent harnesses and support independent review as a practical axis of test-time scaling.
  </details>

- **[How Humans Learn Unfamiliar Worlds: A Think-Aloud Dataset on ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34523)**  `arXiv:2610.34523`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuzhi Liu, Archana Warrier, Dat Nguyen, Chengqi Zheng, Kevin Ellis, Yewen Pu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning through exploration and using newly acquired knowledge to achieve goals are central capabilities of general intelligence. Understanding these capabilities requires tracing how learners build, use, and revise world models during interaction. We construct the first dataset that records how humans discover and use the mechanisms of novel environments, using ARC-AGI-3 as a testbed: 144 trajectories from 64 participants across 25 public games, each pairing time-synchronized actions with think-aloud audio, plus 250 trajectories from large language model (LLM) agents with and without harnesses on the same games. We annotate mechanism triggers and recognition, action purposes, and errors and recovery, linking changes in understanding to the observations and actions that support them. We analyze characteristics of human cognition and compare them with those of LLM agents. Both groups anticipate mechanisms and use familiar analogies. Qualitative representations are more common in successful human trajectories than in successful LLM trajectories (50.0% versus 14.3%). Humans more often explore unfamiliar situations without a concrete hypothesis, while hypothesis testing is common in both groups. We also trace how learners recover through belief revision, replanning, and execution correction. As LLMs with well-designed harnesses reach saturated performance on public ARC-AGI-3 games, our cognitive analyses motivate next-generation benchmarks that challenge familiar priors, require accumulated evidence and abstraction, and test planning through subgoals, experimentation, and recovery. Our dataset is available at [this https URL](this https URL).
  </details>

- **[CaliMem: Search-Efficient Data Agent via Calibrated Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34500)**  `arXiv:2610.34500`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hanze Li, Athan Z. Li, Feiyang Kang, Jiachen T. Wang, Yuhan Chen, Ruishan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data curation is a first-order design choice in machine learning, yet tailoring training data to a downstream model and task remains costly in labor, expertise, and compute. Datasets are therefore often processed through fixed pipelines and reused across applications. We show that this assumption is fragile: across 55 dataset--model tasks spanning biological domains, the same curation recipe can help one model while hurting another. Effective curation must adapt to the downstream setting, motivating automated search over data recipes. We first build an agentic system that searches for curation policies at deployment, improving data utility via \textit{test-time curation (TTC)}. Although achieving performance gains, its search is driven largely by recent feedback and remains local. Adding persistent memory helps the agent retain evidence across iterations and reveals a deeper problem: it forms claims that extend beyond the conditions in which they were observed, sometimes abandoning an entire strategy after a single unsuccessful trial.To address this problem, we introduce CaliMem, a calibrated-memory agentic loop that requires the agent to predict the outcome of each proposed policy before executing it. Comparing this prediction with the observed result gives the agent a \textit{reality check} on the claims behind its proposal. It uses the prediction error to retain supported claims, revise mistaken ones, and specify the conditions under which a conclusion holds. This makes the agent's beliefs testable, reduces overgeneralization, and supports more effective long-horizon exploration. Across biological and LLM post-training tasks, CaliMem consistently outperforms fixed curation baselines and published methods, and exceeds full-data training by up to 19.4\%. Code is open-sourced.
  </details>

- **[CTXAgent - Self-Controlled Context Management of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34425)**  `arXiv:2610.34425`  `cs.AI`  
  _Matthias Busch, Marius Tacke, Roland C. Aydin, Christian Feiler, Christian J. Cyron_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses keep long sessions inside the context window by compacting the history when a token threshold is crossed. We study an alternative in which the model manages its own context: it decides when to compact, what to keep and what to write as the hand-off, given two tools and one paragraph of guidance. Since earlier work found that open-weight models cannot manage their own context without scaffolding, we compare seven frontier models under identical conditions, with and without the context tools, on token cost and benchmark score, and we compare our harness with three widely used harnesses on two of these models. The benchmark is assembled from existing ones: coding exercises, multi-step SciCode problems and SWE-bench Pro and Terminal-Bench 4.0 tasks are interleaved with planted facts, ephemeral observations and standing instructions, all checked deterministically, in sessions of 35-212 turns that resemble a user working through different tasks in one chat. Most of the strongest models manage their context: they compact and curate regularly, the live context of the mixed sessions stays mostly below 100k tokens, and their benchmark scores mostly do not drop significantly. For the models that manage their context well, the cost reduction is substantial but varies between models, because some models compact so often that other costs increase. With Claude Opus 5.5, the self-managed harness matches Claude Code on task success at 34% lower cost than its explicit 200k-window configuration and 49% lower than its default, with no context window to choose. We release the agent, the benchmark and all result rows.
  </details>

- **[MultiEvoSkill: Structure-Aware Multi-Agent Skill Evolution for Generalizable Skill Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34260)**  `arXiv:2610.34260`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kounianhua Du, Jianxing Liu, Ruoshui Shan, Yucheng Zhang, Ke Li, Xing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill documents are becoming the default adaptation surface for frozen large language model (LLM) agents, but current skill generators face two key limitations. First, humans and different LLMs differ in how they organize procedural knowledge and approach task solving. Skills authored from a human or single-model perspective may therefore fail to align with the reasoning behaviors of other backbones, limiting cross-model generalization. Second, sequential skill descriptions inadequately represent procedures with parallel branches and explicit dependencies, while existing skill optimizers primarily operate on prose and lack explicit support for graph-structured skill evolution. We introduce MultiEvoSkill, a skill-optimization framework comprising three core components: 1) Skill compiler casts the skill as a typed graph with tailored operators, producing a compact, informative representation with explicit procedural topology; 2) Heterogeneous agent system proposes revision advices using collective intelligence, where minimal and informative issues are identified based on multiple sets of experiences. 3) Structure-aware debate and evolution mechanism organizes candidate edits into a heterogeneous graph, resolves them under dependency and importance constraints, and distills the collective proposal into the artifact. Multi-dimensional gating ensures the information gain and topological correctness of each evolution. Optimal skills are then obtained when the system converges at a consistent state. Various experiments and ablations against SOTA methods validate the effectiveness and generalization of MultiEvoSkill.
  </details>

- **[R2A-Sales: A Policy-Atom-Matched Benchmark for Rule-to-Action Gaps in Goal-Directed Sales Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34185)**  `arXiv:2610.34185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yihua Qin, Jiajie Jerry Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based sales agents are often evaluated on static policy questions or task success, but deployment asks a sharper question: when a model can state a rule, will it choose compliant, tool-grounded actions under commercial pressure? We introduce R2A-Sales (Rule-to-Action), a policy-atom-matched benchmark for this question. Each item pairs a static probe with a multi-turn sales scenario sharing the same versioned policy atom, facts, evidence, behavioral constraints, and tool contract, so differences cannot be attributed to policy-content mismatch. In the interactive arm, a code-owned ledger verifies committed tool effects and terminal states; unsupported action claims receive no credit. We report static compliance $K$, grounded action compliance $A=\Pr(\text{safe terminal}\land\text{no hard violation}\land\text{non-echo})$, and the matched gap $G=K-A$. The frozen release spans three controlled Chinese-language sales domains, 24 policy atoms, 42 semantic cores, and 2,016 matched pairs. The confirmatory study audits 21,888 runs over 19 cores, covering eight backbones plus four Qwen3-8B prompt treatments (12 configurations). Static compliance is near ceiling $(K=89.9\%\text{--}99.1\%)$, yet eight-backbone action compliance is only $A=3.0\%\text{--}17.3\%$, giving $G=80.4\text{--}92.5$ percentage points. Since $A$ also requires safe termination, $G$ upper-bounds rule-to-action degradation rather than isolating pressure alone. No prompt-only treatment dominates all metrics. R2A-Sales shows that sales-agent reliability must be measured at committed action, not rule recital or task completion alone.
  </details>

- **[From Document Layout to Causal Topology: An End-to-End Architecture for Temporal Causal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34094)**  `arXiv:2610.34094`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhifeng Yang, Wei Luo, Yongjian Chen, Shiqi Ou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) generally lack deep causal reasoning capabilities in temporal reasoning. Inspired by document layout reordering, this paper proposes a paradigm shift from discrete narratives to causal topologies. It constructs two architectures: a training-free neuro-symbolic framework, temporal causal reasoning agent (TCR-Agent), which achieves interpretable causal deduction and counterfactual truncation through external causal graph instantiation and a symbolic intervention engine; and an end-to-end model, temporal causal reasoning former (TCR-Former), which internalizes these mechanisms into the Transformer attention space via a Causal-Topological Attention Mask and temporal span biases. Experiments on the TempoCausal benchmark demonstrate that TCR-Agent comprehensively surpasses existing training-free baselines. At the same time, TCR-Former achieves an accuracy 16.5% higher than that of state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, outperforming large-scale models such as GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V3.2 with only approximately 8.6B parameters. Further analysis reveals a structure-reasoning disconnection phenomenon in existing methods: external symbolic constraints and chain-of-thought reasoning can improve reasoning steps, yet fail to enhance deep causal deductive capability. By internalizing temporal causal reasoning capabilities into the model, TCR-Former effectively bridges this gap, providing an efficient and reliable end-to-end foundation for complex reasoning scenarios.
  </details>

- **[WebViSE: Scaling Visual Design Intelligence from the Open Web for LLM Website Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33929)**  `arXiv:2610.33929`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Sun, Peng Zhao, Le Zhang, Bingjin Chen, Zhengfan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have made remarkable strides in website development, enabling complete and interactive web experiences to be built from natural-language instructions with far less effort. Yet, non-expert users often struggle to articulate sophisticated visual and interaction designs in natural language. Meanwhile, the open web already contains a vast reservoir of such design knowledge, embodied in mature website implementations. To this end, we introduce Website-derived Visual Skill Extraction (WebViSE), a framework that automatically abstracts these implementations into reusable Visual Skills and builds a structured Skill library. To scale the library, WebViSE organizes its Skills in a multi-parent DAG and jointly evolves Skill descriptions and DAG structure using Skill-conditioned Potential Queries, enabling efficient matching between diverse user instructions and relevant Skills. The auto-evolved structure outperforms non-hierarchical LLM retrieval baselines at only 39.09% of the estimated retrieval cost with Qwen3.8-Max. For any models, WebViSE can be directly injected as plug-and-play guidance, enabling diverse models to produce more visually compelling and aesthetically refined web pages. For open-source models, WebViSE can be used to build a data-synthesis pipeline that internalizes visual design intelligence into model weights. Experiments across multiple web development benchmarks and human evaluation consistently show that WebViSE improves the visual quality and interaction fidelity of artifacts. Overall, our work establishes an automated path for extracting, organizing, and transferring design intelligence from the open web to website-building models. We release all extracted Visual Skills, the associated Skill DAG, and our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReGE: Stronger Agents through Experience Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33861)**  `arXiv:2610.33861`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiarong Liang, Yubo Wang, Zijie Liu, Yuxuan Zhang, Ping Nie, Wenhu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated attempts at interactive tasks uncover facts, useful operations, and failures that can inform subsequent execution. We introduce ReGE, a framework for turning this recorded experience into guidance for a fresh attempt. Its main implementation, ReGE-Syn, uses a single coach call to jointly synthesize bounded execution records and available artifacts into self-contained guidance, connecting task-specific findings with proposed actions and relevant pitfalls. A student starts from the task's initial environment state and adapts this guidance to current observations. Source generation, synthesis, and student execution use the same base model, without parameter updates or a stronger teacher. Across four benchmarks, ReGE improves accuracy by an average of 9.9 percentage points for GPT and 8.8 percentage points for Claude compared with unguided execution. On matched BrowseComp-Plus subsets, ReGE-Syn reduces guidance-construction tokens by 98.2\% on GPT and 95.5\% on Claude relative to adapted AggAgent, with observed success. Combined construction and execution tokens decrease by 15.7\% and 35.4\%, respectively, counting input plus output and excluding source generation. BrowseComp-Plus ablations further associate access to process information with fewer student steps and show that joint synthesis produces substantially shorter guidance than Direct history or Independent summaries, with observed success. These results demonstrate how agents can use their own recorded experience to improve subsequent task completion, and suggest a low-cost way to consolidate the execution records of parallel agent systems such as agent swarms.
  </details>

- **[Local Entropy Fluctuations for Black-Box LLM-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33767)**  `arXiv:2610.33767`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinpeng Wang, Bruce Rushing, Zhizhen Chen, Zhe Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-shot detection of machine-generated text is vital for mitigating the risks of large language models (LLMs) misuse. While the state-of-the-art dual-model detectors and resampling-based detectors outperform single-model statistical detectors on unprompted continuations, they suffer a catastrophic breakdown on instruction-conditioned text in the black-box setting, where the source model is unknown. We analyze this failure and trace it to poor prompt conditioning for resampling-based methods and diminished cross-model discrepancy for dual-model detectors. Motivated by recent studies that human writing maintains a steady, uniform information flow, whereas machine-generated text exhibits sharp, localized entropy fluctuations, we propose a new metric, the Relative Peak Prominence Rate (RelPPR) to capture this structural contrast along the entropy trajectory and further leverage it as a signal for LLM-generated text detection. Extensive evaluations across diverse model families and datasets demonstrate that RelPPR achieves state-of-the-art black-box detection performance and remains the most reliable detector under various practical situations. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AlphaIdeaBench: Benchmarking Automated Research Idea Generation under Feasibility Constraints with Peer Review Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33644)**  `arXiv:2610.33644`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shunchao Zhou, Chang Yang, Jingrui Wang, Shuxin Zhuang, Rundong Wang, Runsheng Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Research ideas are always proposed under resource constraints: a plan must fit the available compute, API credit, and time. Yet evaluations of research-ideation agents mostly score unconstrained novelty or overlap with a published paper, and their LLM judges are rarely checked against peer review. We introduce AlphaIdeaBench, a benchmark of 100 ideation tasks (50 workshop calls for papers and 50 open problems stated in surveys), each paired with an explicit resource budget and a shared proposal schema. An LLM judge scores each proposal on 12 dimensions covering validity, novelty, and feasibility. To validate the judge, we compare its scores on four novelty axes (problem, method, scenario, and insight) with scores derived from reviewer comments on 600 papers from ICLR 2026, NeurIPS 2025, and ICML 2025. For problem and method novelty, Gemini 3.7 Flash is within one point of the reviewer-derived score on 94-97% of papers; agreement on scenario novelty is relatively lower. We evaluate eight published harnesses on a shared backbone and, within one harness, eight backbone LLMs. CoI-Agent obtains the highest overall score, narrowly ahead of SciMON, which leads on method and insight novelty. All harnesses score low on scenario novelty and rarely change the problem setting. The backbone largely determines which open problem a proposal targets, whereas the harness determines whether a proposal addresses one problem or combines several. Weaker backbones such as Llama 4 Scout fail to carry out that harness's strategy and score low on budget feasibility. Finally, when only the budget is varied on three topics, a larger budget changes the experimental setting on compute-intensive topics but only adds tests on an inexpensive one. We release the tasks, rubrics, and scores to foster standardized, reproducible benchmarking of future research-ideation agents under constraints.
  </details>

- **[When Generated Information Returns: Error Persistence in LLM-Mediated Information Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33617)**  `arXiv:2610.33617`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yi Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly produce online articles from material that later becomes input to other models. This circulation connects individual responses to the information available for subsequent generation. We study a consequential distinction: an article can avoid endorsing a false claim while preserving the concrete details that enable its reproduction. Our framework connects claim content, expressed stance, and downstream reuse to explain selective error persistence under a continuing supply of accurate and inaccurate information. We implement a controlled community writing process that varies whether published articles return as inputs, matching external supply, input capacity, and generation volume. Across twelve factual targets and four production models, recirculation increases the presence of complete false claims by 7.29 percentage points at the final round while reducing explicit endorsement by 8.33 points. Effects differ across facts and models. Paired interventions then abstract eligible error details within fixed histories while preserving correct content and the surrounding controversy. Error reproduction remains largely unchanged across the evaluation targets, while a development quotation target exhibits local reductions. These findings connect persistent errors to the information available throughout a writing process and motivate evaluation of both what models affirm and what their publications supply to future writers.
  </details>

- **[Auditing evaluation artifacts and data leakage in large language model systems for rare disease diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33592)**  `arXiv:2610.33592`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jian Yang, Peilin Liu, Xiaohan Li, Songda Li, Yu Cao, Yuqing Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
