  Augmented large language model (LLM) systems have reported high benchmark accuracies in rare disease diagnosis, yet their real-world clinical utility remains unproven. A recent meta-analysis flagged augmented LLM diagnostic evaluations as having a high risk of bias due to suspected data leakage; however, the precise impact of these vulnerabilities on model inference has yet to be fully quantified. In this study, we present a comprehensive empirical audit to quantify evaluation artifacts across prevailing AI paradigms, using a repository of over 60,000 cases from diverse public and independent clinical cohorts. By reconstructing a state-of-the-art agentic framework, we demonstrate that candidate-space contamination artificially inflates performance: enforcing strict cross-dataset isolation resulted in a marked performance decline, with the cross-dataset mean Recall@1 decreasing from 54.3% (under conventional one-vs-rest evaluation) to 40.3% (Hedges' g = 0.94). Similar spatial overfitting in supervised classifiers caused diagnostic accuracy to drop from an internal mean of 42.9% to 16.7% across external cohorts (Cohen's d = 2.01). Furthermore, we quantify how LLMs infer diagnoses by exploiting retrospective therapeutic cues in clinical narratives, and how temporal misalignment in foundational knowledge bases leads to performance fluctuations. We conclude that prevailing evaluation protocols for augmented LLM systems inadvertently reflect data leakage rather than genuine medical reasoning. To address these systematic artifacts, we propose a rigorous evaluation framework - coupling strict data-isolation protocols with standardized benchmarking safeguards - to ensure future diagnostic AI for rare diseases is evaluated on actual clinical robustness.
  </details>

- **[CoEvolve: Counterfactual-Guided Co-Evolution of Memory and Collaboration in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33539)**  `arXiv:2610.33539`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xufei Shi, Derong Xu, Wei Chen, Yanyu Yao, Zhi Zheng, Tong Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse tasks. Yet their memory and collaboration architectures often remain static, constraining continued adaptation and improvement over time. Recent work addresses this limitation by evolving memory or collaboration based on interaction experience. However, updates to these interdependent components are not necessarily aligned: an evolved workflow may require experience that memory does not provide, while evolved memory may encode guidance that the workflow cannot use. To address this problem, we propose CoEvolve, a skill-centered framework for aligned memory--collaboration evolution. CoEvolve represents reusable local collaboration patterns as skills that link evolving collaboration decisions to their supporting memories. It further compares original and counterfactual trajectories to identify local evolution signals, which coordinate updates to the skill library, skill memory, and executor memories across rounds. Experiments on the ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks confirm that CoEvolve achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated baselines, while ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of aligned co-evolution and counterfactual-guided evolution.
  </details>

- **[A Comprehensive Framework for All Stages of Large-Model Training: From Data and Tokenization, through Pretraining, to Post-Training Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33451)**  `arXiv:2610.33451`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiangjing An, Zhibin Jiao, Aiting Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The capabilities of large language models (LLMs) and multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) arise from the synergy of the full training pipeline, including data and tokenization, pretraining, and post-training (supervised fine-tuning, preference alignment, reinforcement learning, and continual optimization), rather than any individual stage. Existing surveys mostly adopt a single-stage or single-technique perspective, treating related research as isolated systems without unified stage division or a horizontally comparable basis.This paper proposes a unified stage division and a six-dimensional analytical framework covering data, objective function, optimization strategy, system constraints, evaluation, and risk and governance. We systematically review 395 core references from 2017 to 2026, and present a conceptual map of cross-stage effects: forward constraints of pretraining on post-training, and reverse empowerment of post-training on data and pretraining.We derive three core findings. First, the comparability of scaling laws depends on data quality and mixture, a long-neglected variable. Second, preference alignment methods (RLHF, DPO) and reasoning reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards redefine post-training boundaries, while the Pass@1 versus Pass@K debate remains unresolved. Third, multimodal training poses structural challenges to the pure-text paradigm in tokenizer design, training objectives, and evaluation.The paper provides a cross-stage effects diagram, a method-family map, a practical decision tree, and at least 10 verifiable open problems, with an extensible skeleton for focused sub-surveys per stage. As of the retrieval date, peer-reviewed evidence (A+B) accounts for approximately 79% of the 395 references.
  </details>

- **[ConMem: Counterfactual Multi-Scenario Probing for Resolving LLM Memory Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33432)**  `arXiv:2610.33432`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuning Zhang, Tenghao Ji, Jingruo Chen, Eryue Xu, Bohao Chu, Xin Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory is critical for autonomous Large Language Model (LLM) agents executing multi-session, long-horizon tasks. However, existing memories often inappropriately store, retain, or retrieve over-generalized, misattributed, or privacy-sensitive information, a problem we term memory misalignment. To study this problem, we construct \textbf{MemAlign}, a benchmark comprising 1,079 curated memory cases grounded in synthetic, validated multi-session conversational contexts covering diverse misalignment taxonomies. We then propose ConMem, an algorithm that reframes retention decisions as prospective counterfactual causal inference. \textbf{ConMem} synthesizes counterfactual interaction scenarios to prospectively audit downstream intervention effects across three aspects: relevant task execution, irrelevant invariance checks, and risk-sensitive boundary shifts. Experiments show that ConMem outperforms other methods, achieving 75.16\% accuracy, 64.15\% balanced accuracy, and a Macro-F1 of 65.31\%, effectively mitigating over-retention biases without sacrificing personalization utility.
  </details>

- **[Data Optimization for LLMs: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33405)**  `arXiv:2610.33405`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ou Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have attracted great attention in both academic and industrial communities in recent years. Numerous survey studies have covered various aspects of LLMs, such as learning strategies, applications, alignment, explainability, and evaluation. However, little headway has been made in reviewing data-related aspects for LLMs, despite data being recognized as a key factor contributing to the emergence of the LLMs era. A wide range of data optimization algorithms and techniques are employed in LLMs' training and application processes. These techniques often originate from diverse deep learning domains, with their theoretical inspirations and heuristic motivations appearing unrelated to each other. This study aims to develop a comprehensive framework for LLMs' data optimization techniques to enhance data utilization and efficiency. We first discuss data challenges in LLMs' training and inference. Second, we summarize data perception dimensions for evaluating LLMs' data properties. These dimensions primarily include quality, difficulty, diversity, and redundancy, laying a foundation for subsequent optimization. Third, existing optimization techniques are reviewed and categorized into four groups: data selection, augmentation, reweighting, and others. Fourth, we analyze interconnections between these categories. Finally, we identify key challenges and future directions for LLMs' data optimization compared with conventional deep learning approaches.
  </details>

- **[How To Build A Modern Diffusion Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33391)**  `arXiv:2610.33391`  `cs.AI`  
  _Volodymyr Kuleshov, Marianne Arriola, Yair Schiff, Gilad Turok, Guanghan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The frontier of large language model (LLM) performance has advanced rapidly in recent years, fueled by scaling laws for pretraining on large text corpora. However, mainstream LLMs have fundamental limitations: they generate text autoregressively, producing outputs sequentially from left to right one token at a time. This sequential process leaves parallel GPU hardware underutilized during inference--a bottleneck that has grown acute as progress shifts toward scaling post-training and inference-time compute--and commits irreversibly to each token without the ability to revise earlier mistakes. Discrete diffusion models address these limitations by generating tokens in parallel, starting from a noisy or masked sequence and iteratively refining the entire output over a series of denoising steps. This parallel generative process enables speeds of thousands of tokens per second, provides a new axis of inference-time compute scaling, and supports built-in error correction and controllable generation. Over the past two years, discrete diffusion has scaled to billions of parameters across biological sequence modeling and natural language, with open-weights and commercial releases from leading labs. This article provides a survey of key ideas in discrete diffusion and a high-level introduction to how modern diffusion LLMs are built, covering their foundations, core algorithmic and architectural building blocks, applications in biology and language, and implications for future LLM scaling laws.
  </details>

- **[AgentOrch: How Close Can Global Orchestration Come to Step-by-Step Interaction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33375)**  `arXiv:2610.33375`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zijie Liu, Qi Yang Huang, Jinhao Duan, Yueh Che Lee, Thomas A. Joseph, Zayne Turner, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise workflow agents typically rely on step-by-step interaction (Step-by-Step): the agent executes an action, observes its result, and invokes a large language model (LLM) again to decide the next step. Global orchestration (Global) instead generates a complete executable workflow in advance and executes it without further LLM calls, substantially reducing inference overhead, but it remains less accurate than Step-by-Step. We study how close Global can come to Step-by-Step and what narrows the remaining gap. We introduce AgentOrch, a benchmark that directly compares global orchestration with step-by-step interaction on 650 enterprise SOPs and 3,220 runtime variants under matched tools, environments, and evaluation. Step-by-Step remains more accurate for every evaluated model, but the gap has shrunk by about 79% from early models to the strongest recent model. We identify three routes to further narrowing this gap. Incremental workflow construction improves Global accuracy by an average of 9.98 percentage points (pp) without runtime execution feedback. Revising complete workflows with execution feedback improves all evaluated models, including 6.13 pp on held-out runtime variants. Targeted training on complete workflows improves accuracy by an average of 12.18 pp across all five trained models. Although a gap remains, global orchestration offers a promising path toward enterprise agents that deliver higher accuracy with fewer LLM calls.
  </details>

- **[BenchBench-Cloning: Evaluating Molecular Cloning Design and Protocol Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33355)**  `arXiv:2610.33355`  `cs.AI`  
  _Aditya Sivakumar, Peter Ciaccia, Hassaan Qadir, Ian Hsu, Ashu Singhal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular cloning is one of the most routine wet-lab workflows, yet existing evaluations of language models in biology mostly test knowledge recall or isolated steps rather than end-to-end experimental planning. We introduce BenchBench-Cloning, a benchmark for large language models comprising 65 molecular cloning tasks grounded in sequence records, laboratory notebooks, and published experimental studies. Planning a molecular cloning experiment requires defining a set of constructs needed for a scientific objective and a construction route to produce them. BenchBench-Cloning contains 50 protocol-development tasks, in which agents produce an executable protocol for a specified construct, and 15 construct-design tasks, in which agents determine the constructs a scientific objective calls for. Protocol-development tasks are evaluated by execution in a cloning simulator; construct-design tasks are evaluated with task-specific deterministic checks and, where needed, rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge evaluation. Across eight systems with repeated attempts per task, GPT-6 Astra scores highest at 30.6%, with the others between 8.6% and 22.2%, and the benchmark remains unsaturated under best-of-ten sampling. As integrating individual cloning decisions into complete, valid plans remains challenging even for frontier models, we expect BenchBench-Cloning to serve as a useful benchmark for tracking progress in experimental planning.
  </details>

- **[Scoped Semantic Invariance as a Foundation for LLM Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33267)**  `arXiv:2610.33267`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rosario Uceda-Sosa, Maria Chang, Karthikeyan Natesan Ramamurthy, Moninder Singh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs often answer semantically equivalent queries inconsistently, even when the underlying facts are known. We formalize consistency as semantic invariance over equivalence classes of queries and show that invariance under arbitrary meaning-preserving transformations is undecidable in general. This motivates scoped semantic invariance, where model behavior is evaluated relative to bounded semantic domains.We operationalize this framework using knowledge graphs (KGs), which act as explicit semantic control structures defining local semantic constraints and grounding contexts. Across the MITRE D3FEND ontology and eight Wikidata- derived domains, we show that inconsistencies are structured rather than random, concentrating in localized hotspot subgraphs organized around recurring TREE, STAR, and CYCLE patterns that remain stable across models and domains.We further show that selective graph grounding improves correctness and cross-model consistency, while indiscriminate grounding introduces regressions. A layered grounding strategy achieves the strongest monotonicity profile, preserving correct knowledge while reducing inconsistency. Finally, we show that hotspot structures discovered from simple consistency probes transfer to more realistic compositional reasoning tasks.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Qualitative Analysis with Large Language Models: Development and Validation of the Smart Coder Framework for Human-LLM Collaborative Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33205)**  `arXiv:2610.33205`  `cs.AI`  
  _Cheng Tang, Kun Wang, Yaxuan Yang, Shiyu Wang, Allan Cohen, Rachael Eriksen Brown, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current uses of large language models (LLMs) for qualitative coding often rely on ad hoc prompting with limited guidance for reliability and human oversight. To address these limitations, we propose the Smart Coder Framework, a three-phase approach structuring human-LLM collaborative coding through Instruction, Prompt Strategy, and Reliability Check. We evaluated the framework using 72 middle-school mathematics teacher interview transcripts and a human-consensus benchmark. A factorial design compared instructed versus uninstructed prompts, few-shot versus few-shot plus chain-of-thought prompting, and single-pass versus self-consistency coding. Twelve transcripts were used for prompt development and calibration, and 60 for the formal evaluation. In the results, instruction consistently improved agreement. Chain-of-thought further improved agreement under adequate instruction, and self-consistency produced smaller, theme-dependent gains but helped identify unstable decisions for human review. Performance also varied by transcript characteristics. Overall, the framework systematically structures and monitors LLM-assisted qualitative coding while preserving human authority over coding decisions.
  </details>

- **[AOS-Bench: Can LLM Agents Manage Dynamic Workloads?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33178)**  `arXiv:2610.33178`  `cs.AI`  
  _Erle Zhu, Genrui Zhang, Keyang Hu, Mingyu Su, Hongning Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents are moving from handling one request at a time to serving as persistent assistants to which users hand a stream of work: a build, a data-processing job and a bug fix may all be pending on the same machine at once. Such an agent must decide when to take on new tasks, how to delegate and overlap them, and how to stay within the machine's CPU and memory. Existing benchmarks cannot measure this, because they evaluate each task in isolation. We introduce \benchname{}, which evaluates an agent system as a workload manager: verified tasks arrive over time and must be delivered under shared CPU and memory limits, and the same tasks run one at a time serve as a per-system reference.Across five model--harness pairs, isolated task success does not carry over evenly. On heavy workloads every system loses 13 to 23 points. On medium workloads, systems span 11 points in isolated success but 26 points in stream success, and two systems 0.6 points apart in isolation deliver 54\% and 68\% of the same tasks. Execution-policy interventions trace much of this gap to task intake: serial dispatch leaves 73\% of heavy-workload tasks unclaimed. Yet more elaborate management does not necessarily help. A fixed concurrency of two comes within four points of the best agent-managed policy on both medium and heavy workloads, and under every policy median CPU use stays below 30\% of the provisioned cores while tasks wait. These results identify workload management as a distinct capability for agents, and \benchname{} as a way to measure it.
  </details>

- **[Advancing SVD-based LLM Compression via Layer-Wise Error Model Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32878)**  `arXiv:2610.32878`  `cs.AI`  
  _Moritz Thoma, Maximilian Groezinger, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Emad Aghajanzadeh, Manoj Rohit Vemparala, Christos Anagnostopoulos, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank SVD-based compression offers a powerful strategy to reduce the computational costs of LLMs. However, existing methods face two key limitations: (i) global rank allocation, where uncalibrated error proxies fail to capture complex error propagation, and (ii) decomposition quality, where Fisher-based estimators suffer from severe rank collapse. In this work, we address these limitations by introducing Layer-wise Error Modeling Search (LEMS) and KFAC-SVD. LEMS advances rank allocation by introducing a layer-wise error surrogate that integrates local and global layer importance alongside a propagation bias, enabling effective global rank allocation via an ILP formulation. KFAC-SVD improves decomposition quality by utilizing token-wise statistics, mitigating the rank deficiency observed in prior Fisher-based SVD approaches. Across Mistral, Qwen3, and Llama 3 model families, we show that LEMS consistently outperforms existing search strategies, delivering significant zero-shot accuracy gains of up to 4.7 p.p. that generalize to model sizes of 70B parameters, while KFAC-SVD achieves an average perplexity improvement of 19%. Project page and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Training an MoE LLM in MXFP4: How We Closed the Gap to BF16](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32229)**  `arXiv:2610.32229`  `cs.AI`  
  _Han Wang, Zhitao Wang, Natalia Frumkin, Xuanwu Yin, Alireza Khodamoradi, Dong Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models (LLMs) in sub-8-bit arithmetic promises substantial gains in memory bandwidth and compute throughput, yet FP4 formats introduce quantization error severe enough to destabilize optimization if left unaddressed. We present ALTO, an open-source training recipe for GPT-OSS-20B, a 20-billion-parameter mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, on the MLPerf Small MoE Training benchmark under the MXFP4 microscaling format. The recipe combines established techniques from Abecassis, Felix et al. [arXiv:2509.25149] (hybrid 1D/2D block quantization, randomized Hadamard transforms, and stochastic rounding) with a simplified weight de-oscillation scheme adapted from TetraJet-v2 [arXiv:2510.27527]. On C4 validation at global batch size 16, MXFP4 + RHT + SR + de-oscillation reaches a validation loss of 3.3350 at 16,128 steps, only 0.007 above the BF16 baseline (3.3283), closing roughly 50% of the gap left by MXFP4 without de-oscillation (3.3418). Measured by steps to reach the MLPerf quality target (validation loss 3.34), de-oscillation cuts the FP4 convergence overhead relative to BF16 from +20.0% to +5.0%. We also report negative end-to-end results from differential gradient estimation, outlier clipping, and macro-block scaling, despite each showing operator-level SNR gains. All experiments use simulated MXFP4 kernels on AMD MI300X hardware and wall-clock speed has not been optimized.
  </details>

- **[MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26185)**  `arXiv:2610.26185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang, Pan Ding, Chengying Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Output Discipline Sets the Floor: A Falsification-Guided, Model-Agnostic Agent Protocol for Auditable Hypothesis-Driven Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18298)**  `arXiv:2610.18298`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jing Liu, Yongxing Qi, Ping Yao, Ting Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What binds large language models on auditable, hypothesis-driven decision tasks, where every candidate can be checked against the evidence? On the public TCMEval-SDT benchmark for Traditional Chinese Medicine syndrome differentiation (200 cases), the binding constraint is output discipline and reasoning structure, not model capability alone: two years of model scaling move zero-shot scores by only -0.03 to +0.08, while output-discipline failures (non-terminating reasoning, format non-compliance, large run-to-run spread) persist even in frontier models. We test this with a model-agnostic agent framework formalizing decision making as a five-stage closed loop, enforced by a candidate x evidence matrix falsification protocol: every candidate is judged against every evidence item, at least half of the candidates must be falsified before a conclusion is licensed, and survivors are emitted in a machine-verifiable single-line format. Across five base models, the effect is sign-conditional on zero-shot output discipline: it rescues models whose discipline fails (DeepSeek-V4-Flash: +0.267/+0.271, entirely termination rescue), reduces a high-variance model's run-to-run standard deviation from 0.218 to 0.007 (Kimi K3), and significantly costs already-disciplined models (-0.087 to -0.120 on budget-matched Qwen3-4B/32B). Ablations attribute the cost to the candidate-bounded format layer and show the effect is independent of injected knowledge content (protocol-knowledge decoupling). The protocol also transfers to misinformation-robust mathematical reasoning, detecting injected false premises at 89.8% recall with a 6.8% false-positive rate (MCC 0.54; a flag-everything baseline scores 0) and restoring accuracy to the no-misinformation ceiling. Where zero-shot output discipline is weak, the reasoning protocol sets the floor; base-model capability always sets the ceiling.
  </details>

- **[MedZERO: Self-Evolving Agents for Open-Ended Medical Reasoning Through Controlled Knowledge Accumulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08327)**  `arXiv:2610.08327`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xilin Dang, Weilin Ruan, Xue Yang, Jinghao Wang, Xiaowei Hu, Jinpeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in medical question answering and clinical reasoning, yet their improvement remains constrained by static parametric knowledge and costly expert supervision. Self-evolving agents offer a promising alternative by enabling models to improve through iterative task generation and problem-solving. However, most existing self-evolving methods are designed for easily verifiable domains such as mathematics and coding, where solutions can be checked by exact answers or executable programs. Medical reasoning is fundamentally different: it is open-ended, knowledge-intensive, and often only partially verifiable. We present MedZERO, a self-evolving framework for open-ended medical reasoning. MedZERO couples an Examiner that generates frontier medical question-option pairs with a Reasoner that solves them through evidence-grounded multi-turn reasoning with external knowledge tools. To support reliable, continual improvement, MedZERO adopts controlled knowledge accumulation, which maintains temporary exploratory knowledge and curated persistent knowledge in reasoning. We evaluate MedZERO on five public medical reasoning benchmarks using 4B- and 8B-scale base models under open-ended evaluation. At both model scales, MedZERO achieves the highest average accuracy among the evaluated methods. Code available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OTel: Open Telco AI Datasets, Benchmarks, and Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07766)**  `arXiv:2610.07766`  `cs.AI`  
  _Farbod Tavakkoli, Gregory Diamos, Kenneth Church, Roderic Paulk, Jorden Terrazas, David Kanter, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Open Telco (OTel), an open telecom AI resource that releases derived telecom datasets for retrieval, reranking, instruction tuning, and safety/abstention, together with 30 full-parameter post-trained baselines spanning 10 embedding models, 3 rerankers, and 17 language models. The community has already engaged substantially with the resource: as of May 3, 2026, the released models have been downloaded over 16 million times and the project has received 157+ pieces of media coverage worldwide. Building on prior open telecom datasets and benchmarks, OTel provides documented telecom data sources, held-out evaluation partitions, trained embedding models, rerankers, context-grounded LLMs, and safety/abstention data in one unified resource. Each baseline starts from an open-weight model and is post-trained on OTel-derived data using an open training recipe, then evaluated on held-out OTel evaluation partitions. OTel post-training improves performance across all three model families: embedding retrieval reaches 93.1% NDCG@10, reranking reaches 0.947 MRR@10, and language-model correctness reaches 87.8%. We release OTel as a reproducible starting point and invite the community to expand the data, improve embedding and reranking models, and build stronger context-grounded telecom LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Evaluate the Stack, Not the Layer: Do Deterministic and LLM Gates for Agent Actions Fail Independently?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07359)**  `arXiv:2610.07359`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chenglin Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Runtime gates for agent tool calls are stacked on the assumption that their errors multiply. We test it on 1,119 labelled agent actions from three corpora, without an adaptive adversary. The stack has one deterministic rule layer and four LLM judges, three of them re-collected with the served model recorded on every call. We read each stack as a number of multiplication-equivalent layers, n_mult, with its floor under perfect coupling. Under the STRICT miss definition (escalation to a human scored as not stopped), any two judges compose to about 1.2 to 1.4 layers ({\phi} median +0.430, 6 of 6 pairs significant, floors 1.02 to 1.17). The rule layer plus one judge composes to 1.86 to 2.09 layers ({\phi} median +0.014, 0 of 4 significant, floors 1.01 to 1.09). Under PRIMARY (escalation scored as caught) the bands are 1.21 to 1.57 and 1.80 to 2.13. Intervals separate on the pooled data, point estimates split on each corpus, and a third-vendor judge lands in the judge band. Solo accuracy does not predict what a layer adds: a cloud rule pack lowers the rule layer's solo miss rate by 20% and adds no new joint coverage. The difficulty share of judge coupling is not identifiable: 31.8% to 61.8% depending on the probe and the miss definition. One judge tier was served by an unrequested model version in 50 of 112 batches, concentrated on the external corpus. That event overturned a pre-declared analysis rule, and the scoring of review verdicts reversed five conclusions. We report both.
  </details>

- **[OptiCom : A Unified Framework for State-Conditioned Composition in LLM-Driven Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37221)**  `arXiv:2609.37221`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chenxing Wei, Sichen Liu, Lizhao Liu, Ningyuan Sun, Chen Bingzhou, Ying He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed to solve complex scientific and practical problems via iterative optimization. However, dynamically coordinating diverse search mechanisms as candidate quality, failure modes, and resource budgets evolve remains a critical open challenge. Targeted empirical diagnostics reveal that mechanism effectiveness is highly state-dependent. Motivated by this, we analyze how individual decisions drive final outcomes, decomposing the expected terminal improvement under a shared budget into cumulative decision opportunities minus cumulative selection losses. Guided by this opportunity-loss theoretical foundation, we propose OptiCom, a unified framework that represents LLM-driven optimizers within a shared configuration space: C=(A,Q,O,E,M,S), corresponding to artifact, query, operator, evaluation, memory, and strategy. Operating within this space, a fast LLM-based Optimization Controller dynamically composes immediate mechanisms through structured Action Packages, while a slower Strategy Adapter refines long-term selection preferences, operator weights, and templates based on accumulated trajectory feedback. Comprehensive evaluations across 32 benchmark groups demonstrate the superiority of framework: OptiCom achieves an average Max-score rank of 1.72 among 14 evaluated configurations, securing the top score in 23 groups. Ultimately, these results highlight the broad applicability and high extensibility of OptiCom as a general-purpose paradigm for robust LLM test-time scaling.
  </details>

- **[Stealth Apart, Harm Together: Skill Cascading Attacks on Skill-Based Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30383)**  `arXiv:2609.30383`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zihao Zhu, Siwei Lyu, Adel Bibi, Baoyuan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A skill is a modular package of natural-language instructions, executable scripts, and reference resources that an agent can load at runtime to extend its capabilities for a specific task. Skill-based agent systems therefore enable flexible reuse of third-party capabilities, but the openness of this skill ecosystem also opens up a new attack surface. Prior work has focused on vulnerabilities within individual skills, but little attention has been paid to risks that arise from interactions across skills. In this paper, we introduce skill cascading attacks, a threat paradigm in which a malicious objective is distributed across multiple skills so that each modification looks benign in isolation, yet their combined execution is harmful. For instance, in a prescription-review pipeline, the first skill weakens signals of recently discontinued medications in the extracted history, the second downgrades the severity of any drug interaction tied to them, and the third suppresses the resulting low-priority alert in the final summary, so that a severe drug-interaction warning silently disappears before reaching the physician. To systematically study this safety blind spot, we develop SkillCascade, an automated multi-agent red-teaming framework, and release SkillCascade-Bench, a benchmark of 213 validated cascading test cases across multiple agent systems and domains. Across representative agents (e.g., OpenClaw, Claude Code, Codex) and LLM backbones, cascaded interactions reliably induce harmful behaviors while evading existing per-skill scanners and runtime monitors. Our findings highlight a gap between component-level integrity and system-level safety, and call for defenses that reason over cross-skill interactions rather than individual skills in isolation.
  </details>

- **[SAPO: Single-Rollout Autoregressive Policy Optimization for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19842)**  `arXiv:2608.19842`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dayang Liang, Lang Feng, Bo An, Yunlong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as an important post-training approach for enhancing the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing methods face a trade-off between policy performance and resource efficiency. Conventional Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) implementations incur substantial memory overhead from a separate critic, whereas critic-free group-relative methods require multiple rollouts and face potential learning bottlenecks on long-horizon tasks. In this work, we propose Single-rollout Autoregressive Policy Optimization (SAPO), an efficient PPO-style framework that unifies policy optimization and value learning within a single causal language model. SAPO exploits the autoregressive structure of LLMs to sequentially generate action and value estimation at distinct causal boundaries with shared parameters, and then jointly optimizes the PPO objectives and an auxiliary on-policy SARSA objective with turn-level generalized advantage estimation, where the latter is designed to facilitate value learning. Extensive experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B and Qwen3-14B demonstrate that SAPO reduces peak GPU memory usage by 24.8% and per-iteration runtime by 23.1% over strong PPO baseline, while matching or slightly improving task success rate. Our experiments also show that SAPO outperforms Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and recent cutting-edge variants in both task success and training stability.
  </details>

- **[Broken Symmetry in LLM Refusal: Answer Release Is More Local Than Refusal Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15772)**  `arXiv:2608.15772`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiqi Liu, Yang Wang, Songxin Wang, Chenghao Xiao, Chenghua Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a language model refuses to answer a prompt, it is unclear whether the correct answer is erased from its internal representations, or merely suppressed at the output layer. We investigate this mechanism using a controlled withhold setting, which yields perfectly matched answering and refusal trajectories for bidirectional activation patching. We uncover a causal asymmetry in intervention locality under matched causal interventions, which we term broken symmetry. Even when a model generates a clean refusal, the correct answer remains linearly recoverable from its hidden states. Furthermore, releasing this withheld answer is a highly local operation, requiring only a single-position patch. Conversely, the reverse operation is not equally local: reimposing suppression requires broader interventions across multiple positions, and assembling a coherent refusal sequence is more difficult still. We further demonstrate that while an average answer-to-refusal displacement vector marks the geometric difference between these states, it fails to act as a reliable, reversible linear control toggle between behaviours. Taken together, our findings show that refusal does not function as a simple symmetric switch. For safety and auditing, this implies that probe recoverability can overestimate true behavioural control, and locating refusal-relevant directions does not reliably grant the ability to steer a model from answering to coherent refusal.
  </details>

- **[Measurement Without Validity: The Compounding Reliability Problem in Agentic AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00794)**  `arXiv:2608.00794`  `cs.AI`  
  _William Caban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic AI evaluation pipelines produce benchmark scores that justify deployment decisions, safety certifications, and regulatory compliance claims. No formal framework has characterized how validity degrades across these stages. We present a knowledge-based three-layer compounding validity model, $V_{\rm total} \leq V_1 \times V_2 \times V_3$, that captures multiplicative degradation across task generation ($V_1$), human-simulator calibration ($V_2$), and automated judgment ($V_3$). Applying it to five widely-used agentic benchmark configurations, we find $V_1$ and $V_2$ independently measurable and both degraded, but $V_3$ not currently quantifiable for any: three of the four benchmarks score with a single deterministic rule, so no second rater exists and reliability is undefined, and the fourth's partial LLM grader has no published grader-vs-human measurement.A structured survey of 55 published agentic evaluation papers finds that 26 of them report no chance-corrected inter-rater statistic of any kind, a determination four independent raters recover at Krippendorff's $\alpha = 0.834$. On the broader judgment of whether a reported metric fits its rating design, approximately 80% of this purposive sample use structurally mismatched, incomplete, or absent inter-rater reliability (IRR) metrics (four-rater range 60.7%-84.4%). Both are consistent with systematic $V_3$ collapse. We further document $V_1$ failures (task validity flaws in 7 of 10 benchmarks) and $V_2$ miscalibration (up to 9 percentage points inter-simulator variance, with systematic demographic disparities for non-Standard American English speakers).We derive eight prescriptions grounded in psychometric science and domain-stratified reliability thresholds (intraclass correlation $\geq 0.70$; $\alpha \geq 0.67/0.70/0.80$ by consequence level) that practitioners can apply immediately.
  </details>

- **[SpatialCLI: Learning to Reason With Spatial Tools, Then Without Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27703)**  `arXiv:2607.27703`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Zhou, Zixuan Huang, Sunzhu Li, Zhuo Yang, Chen Zhang, Shunian Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used in embodied agents to interpret visual inputs, reason about spatial relationships, and make task-level decisions based on that reasoning. However, a fundamental capability mismatch remains: general VLMs can reason about the overall task but often miss the visual details that determine success, while specialist vision models can capture those details but cannot translate them into task-level decisions. In this work, we propose SpatialCLI, a framework that teaches VLMs to reason with spatial tools and progressively internalize the specialist perceptual capabilities they provide. SpatialCLI proceeds in three stages: (1) Call exposes specialist vision models as spatial tools to augment the VLM's perception; (2) Learn uses Cold-Start SFT and agentic RL to improve tool use; and (3) Internalize verbalizes successful tool-use trajectories to internalize specialist perceptual capabilities. We further introduce SpatialCLI-Bench, a 516-example benchmark for compositional perception across localization, segmentation, depth, and pose. On MindCube, SpatialCLI raises Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct from 29.3% to 84.6% with tools, surpassing GPT-5.6 Sol with tools (72.1%), while retaining 73.8% without tools after internalization.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Trajectory Imitation: Strategy-Guided Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24064)**  `arXiv:2606.24064`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianyuan Shi, Canbin Huang, Bei Li, Xin Chen, Xiaojun Quan, Jingang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distilling reasoning capabilities from strong to weak language models typically involves imitating specific solution trajectories, effectively transferring what to answer rather than how to reason. This trajectory-level imitation encourages memorization of instance-specific steps rather than acquisition of transferable problem-solving skills, limiting generalization to novel problems. We propose Strategy-Guided Policy Optimization (SGPO), which replaces instance-level trajectory imitation with reusable strategy distillation. SGPO extracts structured strategy descriptions from strong-model responses and, for each problem, constructs both autonomous and strategy-guided trajectories to enable direct comparison of the model's behavior with and without strategic guidance. The framework then addresses two key questions. For how to distill, a token-level forward-KL objective selectively transfers the distributional shift induced by strategy conditioning into the unguided policy, with proximal constraints ensuring stability. For when to distill, adaptive instance-level weighting strengthens guidance when autonomous exploration falls short and reduces it as the model's own competence grows. Experiments on four mathematical benchmarks across two model families show that SGPO consistently outperforms SFT, on-policy RL, and hybrid-policy baselines, improving the average score by 2.2 points over the strongest baseline on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Analysis reveals that the forward-KL objective provides an inherently selective distillation signal that outperforms direct trajectory imitation, and that strategy distillation exhibits complementary scaling with base model capability.
  </details>

- **[NGM: A Plug-and-Play Training-Free Memory Module for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16893)**  `arXiv:2605.16893`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuwen Qu, Wenhui Dong, Camil Hamami, Ming Zhao, Chenyang Si, Caifeng Shan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies have introduced memory-augmented architectures that decouple knowledge storage from neural computation, enabling more direct access to stored representations. However, existing approaches often rely on learned memory embeddings or explicit memory and retrieval components, requiring additional training or increasing architectural complexity. To address these limitations, we propose N-gram Memory (NGM), a training-free, plug-and-play module that repurposes a backbone model's pretrained token embedding space as memory. NGM consists of a Causal N-Gram Encoder and a Cosine-Gated Memory Injector. The former constructs N-gram representations by directly aggregating pretrained token embeddings, while the latter uses cosine similarity with ReLU gating to adaptively control the amount of retrieved memory injected into contextual representations. Unlike conventional memory-augmented architectures, NGM requires neither additional embedding training nor a separately learned memory bank or external retrieval infrastructure. We evaluate NGM on ten benchmarks across six language-model backbones spanning two model families and multiple scales, including four Qwen3 models (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, and 8B) and two Llama-3.2-Instruct models (1B and 3B). NGM consistently improves the ten-task average across all six backbones. Notably, it improves HumanEval by 4.26, HumanEval+ by 2.41, and GPQA-Diamond by 6.56 percentage points. Furthermore, NGM extends beyond text-only language models to vision-language models, with Qwen3-VL-2B achieving improvements across all five VLM benchmarks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[DiagnosticIQ: A Benchmark for LLM-Based Industrial Maintenance Action Recommendation from Symbolic Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08614)**  `arXiv:2605.08614`  `cs.AI`  
  _Devin Yasith De Silva, Dhaval Patel, Christodoulos Constantinides, Shuxin Lin, Nianjun Zhou, Dharmashankar Subramanian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monitoring complex industrial assets relies on engineer-authored symbolic rules that trigger on sensor conditions and prompt technicians to perform corrective actions. The bottleneck is not detection but response: translating rules into maintenance steps requires asset-specific knowledge gained through years of practice. We investigate whether LLMs can serve as decision support for this rule-to-action step and introduce \ours{}, a benchmark of 6{,}690 multiple-choice questions generated from 118 expert-authored rule-action pairs across 16 asset types, with experts reviewing the generated prompts for layout and conversational flow. We contribute (i) a symbolic-to-MCQA pipeline normalizing rules to Disjunctive Normal Form with embedding-based distractor sampling, (ii) five variants probing distinct failure modes (Pro, Pert, Verbose, Aug, Rationale), and (iii) a benchmark of 29 LLMs and 4 embedding baselines. A human evaluation (9 practitioners, mean 45.0\%) confirms \ours{} requires specialist knowledge beyond operational experience. Three findings stand out: the frontier has closed, with the top three LLMs within one Macro point and inseparable under rule-level tests. Yet \ours{}\,Pro exposes brittleness, with the leader losing 13.8 Macro points and smaller models losing over 60\% of their accuracy under distractor expansion. \ours{}\,Aug exposes pattern-matching: under condition inversion, the leading model still selects the original answer 63\% of the time. Frontier models handle template-style fault detection but break under structural perturbation.
  </details>

- **[EpiPersona: Persona Projection and Episode Coupling for Pluralistic Preference Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28197)**  `arXiv:2603.28197`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Zhang, Weikang Yuan, Zhuoren Jiang, Pengwei Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pluralistic alignment is essential for adapting large language models (LLMs) to the diverse preferences of individuals and minority groups. However, existing personalized reward models often mix stable personal traits with episode-specific factors, limiting their ability to generalize across episodes. To address this challenge, we propose EpiPersona, a framework for explicit persona-episode coupling. EpiPersona first projects noisy preference feedback into a low-dimensional persona space, where similar personas are aggregated into shared discrete codewords. This process separates enduring personal characteristics from situational signals without relying on predefined preference dimensions. The inferred persona representation is then coupled with the current episode, enabling episode-aware preference prediction. Extensive experiments show that EpiPersona outperforms the baselines. It achieves notable performance gains in hard episodic-shift scenarios, while remaining effective with sparse preference data.
  </details>

- **[AutoB2G: Agentic Simulation and Reinforcement Learning for Spatio-Temporal Grid-Interactive Building Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26005)**  `arXiv:2603.26005`  `cs.AI`  
  _Borui Zhang, Nariman Mahdavi, Subbu Sethuvenkatraman, Flora Salim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Grid-interactive building control can improve demand-side flexibility, but realistic evaluation requires coordinated co-simulation of buildings, reinforcement learning (RL), and distribution grids. Translating user intent into executable co-simulation workflows is labor-intensive, requiring the manual integration and configuration of heterogeneous simulators and interfaces. We introduce AutoB2G, a grid-aware building--grid co-simulation environment integrating CityLearn V2 with Pandapower and OpenDSS. It supports balanced and three-phase unbalanced grid simulation and a broad range of grid-side evaluation metrics. AutoB2G organizes simulation components as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), explicitly encoding module dependencies and execution order for dependency-aware retrieval and composition. Building on this structure, we develop a large language model (LLM)-based agentic framework that automatically retrieves, composes, executes, verifies, and repairs simulation pipelines from natural-language task descriptions. Experiments on realistic distribution networks show improved reliability and executability of complex building--grid workflows, demonstrating the potential of agentic LLMs for scientific simulation orchestration in energy systems.
  </details>

- **[Beyond 0-100: How Confidence Scale Design Shapes Verbalized Confidence and LLM Metacognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09309)**  `arXiv:2603.09309`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuyang Dai, Yuxia Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verbalized confidence, in which LLMs report a numerical certainty score, is widely used to estimate uncertainty in black-box settings, yet the confidence scale itself (typically 0--100) is rarely examined. We show that this design choice is not neutral. Across six LLMs and three datasets, verbalized confidence is heavily discretized, with more than 78\% of responses concentrating on just three round-number values. To investigate this phenomenon, we systematically manipulate confidence scales along three dimensions: granularity, boundary placement, and range regularity, and evaluate metacognitive sensitivity using $meta\text{-}d'$. We find that a 0--20 scale consistently improves metacognitive efficiency over the standard 0--100 format, while boundary compression degrades performance and round-number preferences persist even under irregular ranges. These results demonstrate that confidence scale design directly affects the quality of verbalized uncertainty and should be treated as a first-class experimental variable in LLM evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Exploring the Limits of Large Language Models as a Reflection of Human Cognition: An Illustration in the Context of Moral Judgement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06950)**  `arXiv:2508.06950`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sarah Schröder, Thekla Morgenroth, Ulrike Kuhl, Valerie Vaquet, Benjamin Paaßen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs), such as ChatGPT, are increasingly used in research, ranging from simple writing assistance to complex data annotation tasks. Recently, some research has suggested that LLMs are able to reproduce human responses to psychological questions and may, therefore, be used as simulators of human participants in some psychological research. We caution against this approach. Based on machine learning research, we argue that LLMs are likely to behave differently on novel stimuli compared to human study participants. We exemplify this in an experiment regarding moral judgments, demonstrating that slight changes to the wording of 30 moral scenarios used in prior work significantly reduce the similarity between ratings made by humans and LLMs, although the magnitude of this reduction varies across models. Therefore, we advise researchers to validate the similarity between human and LLM responses for their specific experiment.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Harmful Memorization in LLM Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02296)**  `arXiv:2503.02296`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lizhe Zhang, Wentao Chen, Li Zhong, Letian Peng, Zilong Wang, Jingbo Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assessing memorization in LLM code generation is challenging because code reuse is not inherently harmful. Correct programs often share APIs, templates, and algorithmic idioms, so code similarity alone may reflect benign reuse rather than problematic memorization. We focus on the harmful case: a model fails to adapt when task semantics change and instead produces code that remains highly similar to the original solution. We propose \emph{code rewriting}, an automated semantic perturbation pipeline that constructs superficially similar but semantically different variants of existing coding tasks by modifying a core logic element of the reference solution and regenerating the corresponding prompt. To summarize this behavior, we introduce the \emph{Harmful Memorization Index} (HMI), an operational diagnostic that combines similarity to the original solution with performance degradation on rewritten tasks. We use harmful memorization as an operational behavioral term for this specific failure mode; HMI does not establish whether an original task or solution appeared in a model's training data. HMI is intended to complement standard accuracy metrics by capturing brittleness under small semantic changes. Across MBPP+ and BigCodeBench, we find that: (1) HMI does not monotonically increase with model scale and often decreases on simpler tasks; (2) high-HMI failures are concentrated in specific logic-edit types, especially constraint changes and data transformations; (3) supervised fine-tuning improves original-task accuracy but can substantially increase HMI on held-out tasks; and (4) across the evaluated post-training settings, PPO yields a more favorable accuracy--HMI trade-off than SFT.
  </details>
